. . . . . . . . . . . . 196 14.4 Characterization of the value function as a viscosity solution . . . . 199 3Introduction 

I will not spare the reader this now classical introduction to a new research book on mathematics: an almost apologetic justification of its existence. An easy, and slightly dishonest explanation could be to say that this (short) book simply origi-nates from the Peccot course I gave at Collège de France in 2023, where I presented some results I obtained on mean field game master equations and Hamilton-Jacobi equations, both set on spaces of measures. However, this does not justify at all the first half of what is about to follow... In fact, while discussing the possibility of publishing the lecture notes of this course with some colleagues, in particular with my great friend Sylvain Sorin, I got convinced that a new presentation of basic aspects of analysis on spaces of measures might be of interest, in particular to young or non-specialist (in this topic) mathematicians, especially around the notions of derivatives. I insist upon the plural for the term notion, as several of them are used in the literature: Lions, flat, Wasserstein or linear derivatives can be encountered... It is often the largest source of confusion among inexperienced mathematicians in this field, and, if it is true that some of these terms refer to the same objects, there are also co-existing notions which have very little to do with one another. One of my main objectives when writing this book is to reach a more understandable presentation of these concepts, or at least to give my point of view on these questions. The literature on analysis on spaces of measures, in particular on Wasserstein spaces, is growing at an incredible pace. It started with topics of dynamics: gra-dient flows, questions about what should be the equivalent of ordinary differential equations in this context,... and now concerns also partial differential equations (PDEs) on spaces of measures. Most of those mathematical objects arise from quite applied problems: the dynamics of optimal transport have proved to be of interest in image processing or economics for instance, mean field games are natural models in economics, mean field control problems naturally appear in engineering when dealing with decentralized protocols of large fleet of devices, contract problems in financial mathematics sometimes yield similar objects, and the fluctuations of models in statistical physics (or more generally mean field models) leads to the study of Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equations on spaces of probability measures. Several excellent references already define derivatives of a function of a (prob-ability) measure, such as Part II of the textbook of Ambrosio, Gigli and Savaré [10], the presentation of Otto’s calculus in Villani’s textbook on optimal transport [119], Lions’ lectures at Collège de France in 2007-2008, Chapter V in Carmona and Delarue’s textbook [43] or the short book of Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry and Lions on mean field game master equations [39]. The first two references do not 4include the more recent developments that were initiated by Lions to study mean field games, and the last two references have a presentation which is quite oriented toward the study of master equations, and may discourage researchers not inter-ested in mean field games. In the present work, I try to give a self-motivated, and thus quite abstract, study of functions of a measure argument. I will focus on basic notions such as convexity, sub-differentiability, optimality conditions, and keep a safe distance from second order derivatives. This presentation is not an end supposed to close a discussion, as several ques-tions remain open, nor a beginning, because of the already existing literature, but a step aside, trying to offer a new perspective on what may appear as con-fused developments. It aims at presenting in a concise way a point of view that turned out to be quite helpful in my research, and which I hope will help others as well: there are at least two ways to differentiate a function of a measure, the two approaches are extremely detached from one another, and both are helpful in different situations. The first way, which I shall call vertical, consists in adding or taking away mass of the current measure. The second one, which I shall call horizontal, consists in moving around the mass of the current measure. I will be quite caricatural in this distinction, insisting that the notion of vertical derivative needs almost nothing on the underlying space on which measures are built, and I hope that this exaggeration will accentuate the inherent distinction between the two notions, rather than discourage applied mathematicians from working with unnecessary abstract notions. Once these general developments are given in Part I, Part II of the book presents a study of mean field game master equations, which are non-linear trans-port equations written on spaces of measures. I will define weak notions of so-lutions, and show how to obtain existence, uniqueness and stability results for them, in what I believe is a rather concise presentation. Part II can serve as a comprehensive introduction to master equations, for people having already a small background on mean field games, namely on the forward-backward systems characterizing Nash equilibria. I will omit developments on second order master equations here (but still treat cases with common noise!). Part III is concerned with mean field control problems and the typical Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equations that arise from them. I will derive such equations from control problems, obtain results of uniqueness of viscosity solutions through com-parison principles, and prove results of existence by using, among other techniques, the representation of the solution with the value function of the problem. I then present a stochastic case, which motivates the study of such equations. While 5I give a rather complete study of deterministic problems, I will not talk about equations with singular terms, such as the ones arising from so called idiosyncratic noises. In my opinion Part III can serve as a general introduction to researchers interested in such questions. The three parts are rather mathematically independent of one another, and only a handful of results of Part I will be used in Parts II and III (results which I will highlight). The main reason for this independence is that the typical solutions of the partial differential equations at hand in Parts II and III are not, in general, sufficiently regular to be differentiable. However, the distinction between the tools used in Part II and III serves as an illustration of the co-existence between the two notions of derivatives introduced in Part I, and where the ideas might come from. I insist upon this because, at a formal level, all partial differential equations of interest can be written with either one of those notions, but only the vertical point of view shall be used in Part II while only tools from the horizontal one will help in Part III. Concerning the writing of this book, my first thanks go to Sylvain Sorin for his all-around help in this redaction, and mainly for pushing me to write what constitutes the first part of this work. As usual, I have made significant progress when trying to put on paper what I thought I understood. For this, I cannot thank him enough. I feel extremely grateful to Pierre-Louis Lions, on too many points to mention them all here, but especially for agreeing to write a preface of this book, for his valuable input on an early version of the manuscript and for the amazing lectures and seminars he gave during his tenure at Collège de France. I learned so much from them that I feel obliged to give the following advice: (mathematical) French is not that hard, it is worth it. Several discussions with Jean-Michel Lasry, especially on the Lagrangian point of view influenced me a lot, and for this I thank him here. Leaving typos or small gaps is one of my specialty and to remove them, I receive great help from Valentin Pesce, Charles Meynard and Giacomo Ceccherini Silberstein. There is no doubt that some remain, but they helped me to remove many of them. A great deal of this book was written in the kind atmosphere of the CEREMADE which I thank for welcoming me. A significant portion of this work has also been realized in the calm Lagrange Mathematics and Computing Research Center to which I am also grateful for its hospitality. The ERC is funding me this last year under the project PArtial DIfferential Equations on SEts of Measures. Last but not least, I want to thank Émilie and Georges for their love and patience. Charles Feral-Bertucci, Paris, July 2026. 6Notation 

Here are some general notations. Several more are to be introduced in the core of the book. The d dimensional Euclidean space is Rd while the flat torus is Td, which is defined as the quotient space Rd/Zd, where Z is the set of integers. The flat torus 

Td is equipped with the distance d(x, y ) = inf z∈Zd |x − y + z|, for | · | the Euclidean norm on Rd. The scalar product between x, y ∈ Rd is denoted x · y. The set of real 

d × d matrices is denoted by Md(R).Functional spaces are denoted as follows: 

• Given a Banach space E, a probability space (Ω , A, μ ) and p ∈ [1 , ∞], the p

Lebesgue space of equivalence classes of functions f : Ω 7 → E for the relation "being equal μ almost everywhere" is denoted by Lp((Ω , μ ), E ). When Ω ⊂ Rd

and μ is the Lebesgue measure, I simply write Lp(E), and the E can also be dropped when the context is clear. 

• Given a Banach space E, a measurable space (Ω , A), the space of bounded measurable functions f : Ω 7 → E is denoted by B(Ω , E ). In particular elements of B are defined everywhere while elements of L∞((Ω , μ ), E ) are only defined μ almost everywhere. 

• For X, Y two metric spaces, the set of continuous functions f : X 7 → Y is 

C(X, Y ), the set of α Hölder continuous functions f : X 7 → Y , for α ∈ (0 , 1) 

is Cα(X, Y ). The Lipschitz constant of a function f : X → Y is Lip (f ) := sup x̸=ydY (f (x),f (y))

.

• For k ≥ 1 integer and p ∈ [1 , ∞], the Sobolev spaces of Rd valued functions over a domain O ⊂ Rd with derivatives up to order k in Lp are denoted as usual W k,p (O, Rd), and Hk(O, Rd) when p = 2 .

• For O a domain of Rd, the space of continuously differentiable functions taking values in Rk is C1(O, Rk). The set of such functions which are infinitely many times continuously differentiable is C∞(O, Rk). When such functions are restricted to have compact support, I write C1

R+, smooth on (0 , ∞) such that ω(0) = 0 . In particular, it is sub-additive. For a function u : Rd 7 → R ∪ {−∞ ; + ∞} , the sub-differential ∂−u(x) of u at 

x ∈ Rd such that u(x) ∈ R is the set of elements p of Rd such that there exists a modulus of continuity ω such that for all y ∈ Rd

For E a topological space, the upper and lower semi continuous envelope of a lo-cally bounded function u : E 7 → R are denoted respectively by u∗ and u∗. They are defined as u∗(x) = lim sup y→x u(y) and u∗(x) = lim inf y→x u(y). Moreover, upper semi continuity and lower semi continuity are abbreviated respectively usc and lsc. A correspondence A between two sets X and Y is a map defined on X taking values in the subsets of Y , it is noted A : X ⇒ Y .Given a tuple x = ( x1, x 2, . . . , x n) and 1 ≤ i ≤ n, the map πi is the projection on the i-th coordinate, that is πi(x) = xi.For t ∈ R, the map et is the evaluation mapping at t. Hence, if I ⊂ R, θ : I 7 → X

and t ∈ I, then et(θ) = θ(t).For h ∈ Rd, τh : Rd 7 → Rd, x 7 → x + h.The topological dual of a topological vector space X is denoted by X′. The duality product will be noted by ⟨· , ·⟩ E′,E , or simply ⟨· , ·⟩ when the context is clear. In particular, when dealing with function spaces, it will always refer to the extension of the L2 scalar product. Given Y ⊂ X, a mapping f : Y 7 → E′ is monotone if for all x, y ∈ Y ,

x̸ = y.In a metric space (E, d ), the closed ball of radius R ≥ 0 centered at x0 ∈ E is denoted by B(x0, R ).The notation o(·) stands for the usual Landau notation. For p ∈ (1 , ∞), p′ = pp−1 is its conjugate exponent. 8Part I. Analysis on spaces of measures 

In this first part, I present several properties of functions U : X 7 → R for X a certain subset of the space of signed measures M(O) on a measurable space O. I will concentrate on cases in which X is either a subset of the space of signed measures equipped with total variation, or in cases where X is some Wasserstein space. I recall some standard definitions, as well as metric and topological properties of spaces of measures and their implications on the study of continuous functions. The core of this first part is concerned with notions of derivatives of a function 

U : X 7 → R. In practice, such a notion is most of the time used to compute 

lim 

> t→0+

U (mt) − U (m0)

t , (0.1) for some variation, or path, (mt)t∈[0 ,1] valued in X. As typical paths of interest can be irregular and of different natures, it is mathematically helpful to consider a more abstract notion of derivative than just giving a definition for the previous limits. That is why I will focus on defining notions of derivatives by finding equiv-alents of first order Taylor expansions of a map U near a measure μ, quite in the spirit of the Fréchet derivative. In order to present the different problems at hand, I will list some classical variations (mt)t∈[0 ,1] for which it might be interesting to be able to compute (0.1). I will then introduce two notions of derivatives, in two different settings: a vertical notion which requires nothing on the base space O but to be a measurable space, and a horizontal notion which shall rely on the possibility to consider differential calculus on the base space O itself. I then present different notions of convexity, of super-differentiability, C1,α regularity, as well as some optimality conditions and results of perturbed optimization. I feel the need to recall here that this part is quite independent of the rest of the book. Indeed, as the two next parts are concerned with the study of non-differentiable solutions to PDEs, differentiability shall play no role in what follows. Hence, for people only interested in precise statements on MFG master equations or on mean field optimal control, they can skip this Part a bit. However, I do not particularly encourage it, as it is always a good idea to learn to walk before trying to run, which here takes the form of understanding usual notions of derivatives before going to weaker notions. In any case, only Section 7 will be of use in both the next two parts. Section 3 allows one to understand the terms in MFG master equations in Part II, while Section 4 helps to understand the HJB equations of Part III. The notion of super-differentials of Section 5.4 is also fundamental in Part III. 91 Distances, topologies and continuous functions on spaces of measures 

In this part, I define the main objects of interest for the rest of the book. I also recall some well-known results about various topologies and distances on sets of measures. 

# 1.1 Measures and total variation distance 

In what follows, a measure μ on a measurable space (O, A) is a σ-additive real function on the σ-algebra A of O, which gives value 0 to the empty set. When 

O is a topological space, A is always taken as the Borel σ-algebra, that is the smallest σ-algebra containing all open sets of O. The positive and negative parts 

μ+ and μ− of a measure μ are given by Hahn-Jordan decomposition theorem and given by μ+(A) = sup B∈A ,B ⊂A μ(B) and by μ−(A) = − inf B∈A ,B ⊂A μ(B). The total variation μT V of μ is equal to μ+ + μ−. The norm of a measure μ is the quantity 

|μ| := μT V (O).

The set of finite measures , that is measures μ such that |μ| < ∞, is denoted by 

M(O). In particular, (M(O), | · | ) is a normed vector space, which is complete since R is. A measure μ is non-negative if μ− = 0 . The set of non-negative finite measures is denoted by M+(O), the set of non-negative measures with mass at most one is denoted by M1(O) and the set of probability measures, that is non-negative measures such that μ(O) = 1 , by P(O). Observe that M+(O), M1(O)

and P(O) are closed convex subsets of M(O).For μ, ν ∈ M (O), the total variation distance between μ and ν is the norm of the difference |μ − ν|. The Dirac mass at x ∈ O is denoted by δx.For ϕ a measurable real valued function on O, the Lebesgue integral, when it is well defined, of ϕ against μ is denoted by both 

⟨ϕ, μ ⟩ =

> ∫
> O

ϕ(x)μ(dx ).

Note in particular that if ϕ ∈ B (O), the set of bounded measurable functions over 

O, then for any μ ∈ M (O), the integral is well defined and ⟨ϕ, μ ⟩ ≤ ∥ ϕ∥∞|μ|. In particular, this implies that we can identify B(O) with a subspace of (M(O)) ′,the topological dual of M(O). The space (M(O)) ′ is much larger than B(O),for instance consider the case O = [0 , 1] (equipped with the Borel σ-algebra) and consider the map T : μ 7 → μs(O), where μs is the singular part of μ given by the Lebesgue decomposition Theorem. Clearly, T is linear, and |T (μ)| ≤ | μ|, hence it 10 is also continuous. However, if it were given by an element ϕ ∈ B (O), we should have ϕ = 1 , which is not possible. If ϕ is valued in a normed vector space, the notation ⟨ϕ, μ ⟩ will by convention refer to the Bochner integral of ϕ against μ, see II.2 of [66]. For a measurable mapping f : O 7 → Y , for Y another measurable space and 

μ ∈ M (O), the image measure f#μ ∈ M (Y ) is defined, for all B measurable set in Y , by 

f#μ(B) = μ(f −1(B)) .

# 1.2 Weak convergence of measures 

The previous development does not require any topology on O, and thus sees all points of O as infinitely far away from each other. In this section, O is a topological space and I want to consider topologies on M(O) which formalize the idea that two measures are close if they put similar mass on points which are close to one another. The most frequently studied topology which formalizes this idea is the so-called 

weak topology of measures. It is induced by the following notion of convergence: the sequence (μn)n ≥0 valued in M(O) weakly converges toward μ ∈ M (O) if for every function ϕ ∈ C b(O), the space of bounded continuous functions on O,

lim n→∞ ⟨ϕ, μ n⟩ = ⟨ϕ, μ ⟩.

For measures with finite norms, convergence in total variation implies weak con-vergence, as for any ϕ ∈ C b(O),

|⟨ ϕ, μ n − μ⟩| ≤ ∥ ϕ∥∞|μn − μ|.

The converse is false since (δxn )n≥0 converges in total variation toward δx if and only if (xn)n≥0 is equal to x after a certain rank, while it converges weakly as soon as (xn)n≥0 converges toward x in O.Complete separable metric spaces are of particular interest in measure theory, because all probability measures on them are in fact Radon measures (Chapter 7 in [34]), and thus quite regular. Such spaces are called Polish spaces . When O is a Polish space, there exist metrics which give the same notion of convergence as the one of weak convergence on P(O), this is for instance the case of the Lévy-Prokhorov metric (Theorem 8.2.2 in [34]). Furthermore, Prokhorov’s Theorem gives an easy way to verify pre-compactness of subsets of P(O) for the weak topology. 11 Theorem 1.1. Let O be a Polish space and A ⊂ P (O). The closure of A in P(O)

(for the weak convergence) is sequentially compact if and only if, for any ϵ > 0,there exists a compact set Kϵ ⊂ O such that inf {μ(Kϵ)|μ ∈ A} ≥ 1 − ϵ.

In particular, if O is a Polish compact set, then P(O) is also compact. 

# 1.3 Couplings, gluing and disintegration 

Let (O, d ) be a Polish space. For Y another Polish space, μ ∈ P (O), ν ∈ P (Y ),the set of couplings Π( μ, ν ) between μ and ν is defined by 

Π( μ, ν ) := {γ ∈ P (O × Y )| (π1)#γ = μ, (π2)#γ = ν}.

It is always non-empty, as we can always consider the independent coupling 

μ(dx )ν(dy ) ∈ Π( μ, ν ). A coupling γ is called deterministic if there exists a mea-surable map T : O 7 → Y such that γ = ( Id, T )#μ.The following result is called disintegration theorem, and it yields the existence of so-called conditional probability measures. It relies on a notion of measurability for a mapping Y 7 → P (O), which is a priori not canonical since I did not fix a 

σ-algebra, or even a topology, on P(O). By convention, for the rest of the book, it is chosen as follows. A mapping ψ : Y 7 → P (O) is called measurable if for any measurable set B ⊂ O , the map y 7 → ψy(B) is measurable.

f (x)μy(dx )ν(dy ).

Furthermore, two such mappings are equal ν almost everywhere. 

In what follows this result shall be used in two main arguments. In the first one, when given a coupling γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ), for μ ∈ P (O) and ν ∈ P (Y ), I shall use it to write γ(dx, dy ) = μ(dx )ψx(dy ), where ψ : O 7 → P (Y ) is a measurable map given by the disintegration Theorem along the map π1. The second one will be in the proof of the Gluing Lemma which follows. 

Lemma 1.3. Let O, Y, Z be Polish spaces, μ, ν, η in respectively P(O), P(Y ), P(Z).Let γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ), ˜γ ∈ Π( μ, η ). Then, there exists a Γ ∈ P (O × Y × Z) such that 

(π1, π 2)#Γ = γ, (π1, π 3)#Γ = ˜ γ. Furthermore, if either γ or ˜γ is deterministic, then there exists a unique such Γ.

12 Proof. By the disintegration Theorem, we can write γ(dx, dy ) = μ(dx )ψx(dy ).Writing Γ( dx, dy, dz ) = ˜ γ(dx, dz )ψx(dy ) produces a required coupling. The unique-ness part follows from the following: if Γ is a gluing of γ and ˜γ, and, denoting by 

Γx, γ x and ˜γx the disintegration of the three previous probability measures along 

π1, then, μ almost everywhere, Γx ∈ Π( γx, ˜γx).First, observe that this last property clearly implies the uniqueness of the coupling. Indeed, if γ (or ˜γ) is deterministic, it implies that μ almost everywhere, 

γx (or ˜γx) is a Dirac mass. Since the set of couplings between Dirac masses and anything else is reduced to a singleton, it follows that Γx is uniquely defined μ

almost everywhere which yields the result. Now this last property is implied by the fact that (π1, π 2)#Γ can be computed in two ways. On one hand it is given by γ, and on the other hand, it is also given by (( π1)#Γx(dy, dz )) μ(dx ). Hence, the uniqueness of the disintegration implies that necessarily, μ almost everywhere, (π1)#Γx = γx. The same obviously holds for the other component. 

# 1.4 Wasserstein spaces and first look at optimal transport 

To metricize weak convergence, the attention is often put on subsets P(O), equipped with so-called Wasserstein metrics. Here, (O, d ) is still a Polish space. Let

P∞(O) := { μ ∈ P (O), ∃R > 0, x 0 ∈ O , μ (B(x0, R )) = 1 } .

Theorem 1.4. For p ∈ [1 , ∞), the space (Pp(O), Wp) is a complete separable metric space. For any μ, ν ∈ P p(O), the infimum in Wp(μ, ν ) is reached.

The space P1(O) is rather special, namely because of the following property that satisfies its metric, which in fact comes from a norm as the next result shows. This result is called Kantorovich’s duality, and it is a classical example of duality result in constrained optimization. 

Theorem 1.6. Let μ, ν ∈ P 1(O), then

Finally, as mentioned above, Wasserstein metrics are naturally associated with weak convergence of measures, as the next result shows.

As an immediate corollary of the previous and Theorem 1.1, we have the fol-lowing. 14 Proposition 1.8. Let p, q ∈ [1 , ∞) with p < q . Then, bounded sets of Pq(Rd) are relatively compact in Pp(Rd).

The Wasserstein metrics are the distances of optimal transport, the problem of transporting optimally a measure μ toward a measure ν for a given cost. Entire textbooks are written on optimal transport and I will simply hint at why such a link exists here. Associated with a coupling γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ) is the idea of pairing elements of mass dx and dy by giving a mass γ(dx, dy ) to the pair. If the cost of transporting an element of mass from x to y is given by d(x, y )p then, the average cost associated to all the pairings induced by γ is clearly Cpp (γ). Thus Wpp (μ, ν ) is the infimum of all cost of the transports given by couplings. When O = Rd, there are other functionals, closely related to Wasserstein dis-tances, that will be helpful in what follows. For p ∈ (1 , ∞), the functional Ip is defined on Pp(Rd) × P p′ (Rd) by 

Ip(μ, ν ) := inf  

> γ∈Π( μ,ν )

−

> ∫
> Rd×Rd

x · y γ(dx, dy ).

Just as it was the case in the previous result, the infimum is always reached here. For p ∈ (1 , ∞), μ ∈ Pp(Rd), we denote by Mp(μ) := ∫ 

> Rd

|x|pμ(dx ). We then have the identity W22 (μ, ν ) = M2(μ) + M2(ν) + 2 I2(μ, ν ).I shall also use the notation Ppp ′ (X × Y ) := {γ ∈ Π( μ, ν )| μ ∈ P p(X), ν ∈Pp′ (Y )}.

# 1.5 Probabilistic formulation of problems in Pp(O)

Sometimes, it will be useful in what follows to pass from a problem posed on the set of probability measures, to a problem posed on the set of random variables. This procedure is called a lift , since the space of random variables is significantly larger than the set of their laws. Here, I make precise some concepts in this direc-tion. In this section, (O, d ) is a Polish space. To consider a lift, we first need a probability space (Ω , A, P), whose expectation is denoted E. This choice is mainly free. The reader should choose the one she or he wants. The only requirements are: that Ω is Polish, and that it is atomless (i.e. without ω ∈ Ω such that P({ω}) > 0) to apply Theorem 1.9 below. I will call such probability spaces standard . Measurable functions from this probability space to O will be called random variables. For a random variable X, its law, de-noted by L(X) is the probability measure X#P. I write X ∼ μ when X is of law μ.15 For p ∈ [1 , ∞) and X ∈ Lp(Ω , O), its law L(X) belongs to Pp(O). The p

Wasserstein distance can also be formulated through 

Wpp (μ, ν ) = inf   

> X∼μ,Y ∼ν

E[d(X, Y )p].

Note that when O ⊂ Rd, d(X, Y )p denotes the Euclidean norm of X − Y to the power p, and not some p-norm in Rd.When using lifting methods, two classical probability results shall be useful. 

Theorem 1.9. Let (Ω , A, P) be a standard probability space and μ ∈ P (O). Then, there exists a random variable X : Ω 7 → O such that L(X) = μ.Proof. I sketch a proof, in the case in which O is uncountable. The first step consists in constructing one-to-one mappings f : [0 , 1] 7 → O and g : [0 , 1] 7 → Ω

such that f, f −1, g and g−1 are all measurable ( [0 , 1] is equipped with the Lebesgue measure), see Theorem 13.1.1 in [69]. We have (f −1)#μ ∈ P ([0 , 1]) . We can consider ξ : ([0 , 1] , g −1# P) 7 → [0 , 1] whose law is (f −1)#μ by using the cumulative distribution functions, because g−1# P is atomless. Observe now that f (ξ(g−1)) is a suitable random variable. 

Theorem 1.10. Let (Ω , A, P) be a standard probability space, and consider two random variables X, X ′ which have the same law. Then, for any ϵ > 0, there exists a one-to-one measurable mapping τ : Ω 7 → Ω such that τ −1 is measurable, 

τ#P = ( τ −1)#P = P and 

∥d(X ◦ τ, X ′)∥∞ ≤ ϵ. 

For O ⊂ Rd, p ∈ [1 , ∞), X ∈ Lp(Ω , O) of law μ, we will consider functions of 

X, that is to consider f (X), for f ∈ Lpμ := Lp(O, μ ), the usual Lebesgue space of order p. These types of Lpμ space have to be manipulated with care as they are simply equivalence classes for the equivalence relation: "being equal μ almost everywhere". In particular, they can then contain elements which are not Borel measurable. As a convention, in what follows, I will always restrict my attention to elements of Lpμ which are Borel-measurable. As a convention, Lp(Ω , Rd) shall be denoted by Lp, and L2 = H. Recall that it is a Banach space, when equipped with the norm ∥X∥p = ( E[|X|p]) 1 

> p

, and that H

is a Hilbert space. Furthermore, I define the lift of a function U : Pp(Rd) 7 → R as the function U : Lp 7 → R defined by 

∀X ∈ Lp, U(X) = U (L(X)) .

16 1.6 Spaces of continuous functions over spaces of measures 

The Wasserstein and total variation metrics allow us to define naturally several regularity spaces for functions over Pp(O) or M(O), such as the regularity spaces 

C, Cα for α ∈ (0 , 1) , or spaces of Lipschitz continuous functions for instance. In addition to the standard properties of such spaces, I present some approximation of continuous functions of measures and the link with the continuity of the lift. 

Polynomials as approximations of continuous functions of measures 

I assume in this section that O is a compact set of Rd and I consider M1(O)

equipped with the weak topology. I say that a function U : M1(O) 7 → R is a 

monomial when either it is constant, or either there exist n ≥ 1 and n continuous functions (ϕi)i=1 ,...,n such that 

U (μ) = 

> n∏
> i=1
> ∫
> O

ϕi(x)μ(dx ).

A polynomial is a real linear combination of monomials. It is clear that all polyno-mials are continuous with respect to the topology of weak convergence on M(O),that the set of polynomials forms an algebra which separates points, and for all 

μ, there exists a polynomial which does not vanish on μ. Hence, by the Stone-Weierstrass Theorem, we have the following. 

Theorem 1.11. For any ϵ > 0 and U : M1(O) 7 → R continuous for the weak convergence of measures, there exists a polynomial P : M1(O) 7 → R such that 

sup 

> μ∈M 1(O)

|U (μ) − P (μ)| ≤ ϵ. 

Remark that, if we equip M1(O) with the total variation distance, it is no longer compact, and thus unsuitable for the use of Stone-Weierstrass’s Theorem. 

Approximation by Dirac masses 

Consider a compact subset O of Rd. There is a link between functions U : P(O) 7 →

R and symmetric functions U N : ON 7 → R that I now illustrate. Given a permutation σ : {1, . . . , N } 7 → { 1, . . . , N }, it acts on ON through 

σ(x) = ( xσ(1) , . . . , x σ(N )). A function U N : ON 7 → R is called symmetric if, for any permutation σ, U N (x) = U N (σ(x)) . Because of this symmetry, U N (x) is in fact a function not of x, but say of {x1; . . . ; xN }. This set can be represented by the empirical measure of x defined by μNx := N −1 ∑Ni=1 δxi . Hence, we can view symmetric functions on ON as functions of the empirical measure. Thus, as N

grows large, U N should resemble a function U : P(O) 7 → R. Of course a sort of uniform continuity assumption has to be satisfied by the U N for this sort of limit 17 to make sense in C(P(O)) . The following result makes this idea precise. I refer to the notation section for a precise definition of a modulus of continuity .

Theorem 1.12. Let p ∈ [1 , ∞), (U N )N ≥1 be a sequence of symmetric functions each defined on ON , C > 0 and ω a modulus of continuity such that, for all N ≥ 1,

U N : ON 7 → R satisfies 

∀x, y ∈ O N , |U N (x) − U N (y)| ≤ ω(Wp(μNx , μ Ny )) ,

∥U N ∥∞ ≤ C. 

Then, extracting a subsequence if necessary, there exists a continuous function 

U : P(O) 7 → R, with modulus of continuity ω, such that 

lim   

> N→∞

sup 

> x∈O N

|U (μNx ) − U N (x)| = 0 .

Proof. The main idea is classical and consists in considering an extension of U N

defined on the whole P(O), which has the same modulus of continuity. Let V N :

P(O) 7 → R be defined by 

V N (μ) = inf  

> x∈O N

{U N (μNx ) + ω(Wp(μNx , μ )) }.

The sequence (V N )N ≥1 is equi-bounded on P(O). It is also equi-continuous of modulus ω. Indeed, let μ, μ ′ ∈ P (O), and consider x ∈ O N which is ϵ-optimal for 

V N (μ′). Then 

V N (μ) ≤ U N (μNx ) + ω(Wp(μ, μ Nx )) 

≤ U N (μNx ) + ω(Wp(μ′, μ Nx )) + ω(Wp(μ, μ Nx )) − ω(Wp(μNx , μ ′)) 

≤ V N (μ′) + ϵ + ω(Wp(μ, μ ′) + Wp(μ′, μ Nx )) − ω(Wp(μNx , μ ′)) 

≤ V N (μ′) + ϵ + ω(Wp(μ, μ ′)) 

where I used the sub-additivity of ω. Hence, using Arzela-Ascoli Theorem, it follows that, up to a subsequence, (V N ) converges uniformly to some function 

U : P(O) 7 → R with modulus of continuity ω. To conclude, it is sufficient to remark that U N and V N agree on {μNx | x ∈ O N }.

Continuity of a function and continuity of its lift 

There is a natural link between topological properties of a function on Pp(Rd) and its lift. 

Proposition 1.13. Let p ∈ [1 , ∞), U : Pp(Rd) 7 → R and its lift U : Lp 7 → R. The function U is usc (resp. lsc, resp. continuous) on Pp(Rd) if and only if U is usc (resp. lsc, resp. continuous) on Lp.

18 Proof. Let p ∈ [1 , ∞) and U : Pp(Rd) 7 → R be usc. Let (Xn)n≥0 be converging toward some X ∈ Lp. This convergence implies that 

Wp(L(Xn), L(X)) ≤ ∥ Xn − X∥p −→  

> n→∞

0.

Hence, by upper semi continuity of U , lim sup n→∞ U (L(Xn)) ≤ U (L(X)) and thus 

U is also usc. Conversely, let U be usc and consider (μn)n≥0 converging toward μ ∈ P p(Rd).Consider X of law μ. By Theorem 1.10, for any n ≥ 0, there exists Xn of law μn

such that ∥Xn − X∥p ≤ W p(μn, μ ) + 1 

> n+1

. In particular, (Xn)n≥0 converges toward 

X in Lp and thus lim sup n→∞ U(Xn) ≤ U (X). Hence U is usc as well. The lsc and the continuity properties can be proven in the same way. 

# Bibliographical comments 

There are several excellent textbooks on measure theory, such as Bogachev [34] for instance. More modern approaches, especially concerning Wasserstein spaces, can be found in the books of Ambrosio, Gigli and Savaré [10], Villani [119] or Santambrogio [114]. A statement and proof of Prokhorov’s Theorem can be found in [34], Theorem 8.6.2. The disintegration Theorem is proven by Dellacherie and Meyer in [64], III-70. This version of the Gluing Lemma is presented in Lemma 5.3.2 in [10]. Theorems 1.4, 1.5, 1.6 and 1.7 can be found in Theorems 6.18, 5.10 and 6.9 in [119]. Theorem 1.10 is Lemma 5.23 in the book of Carmona and Delarue [43]. Theorems 1.11 and 1.12, as well as the generalization of lifting techniques, are due to Lions and were presented in his lectures at Collège de France [98]. Proposition 1.13 can be found in the work of Gangbo and Tudorascu [80]. 19 2 Variations on spaces of measures 

Before giving precise notions of derivatives in spaces of measures, I want to intro-duce several typical variations of measures of interest, both in terms of applica-tions and for theoretical purposes. It is typically along such variations (mt)t∈[0 ,1] 

in M(O) that one is interested in limits of the form 

lim 

> t→0

U (mt) − U (m0)

t , (2.1) for U : M(O) 7 → R.As a definition, a variation (or a curve, or a path) on a topological space X is a path (xt)t∈[0 ,1] ∈ C ([0 , 1] , X ). Usually, we would like to take limits in (2.1) using some sort of chain rule, which should hint at restricting our attention to more regular paths than simply continuous ones. Because in several cases of interest such extra regularity will not hold, I will forget it for the moment. As we shall see, there are several natural types of variations on spaces of measures, which can be quite different from each other. The aim of the present section is essentially to fix some vocabulary and to give some intuition to the reader unused to spaces of measures. The more informed reader is invited to skip or pass quickly over this section. 

# 2.1 Linear-like combinations 

The first variation that I want to insist upon may be the most natural one, as it does not rely on any structure on O, additional to the fact that it has a σ-algebra of course. This notion of variations relies uniquely on the structure of M(O) as a normed vector space equipped with total variation. A (straight) line is defined by two measures μ, ν ∈ M (O) as the set of points 

{μ+λν, λ ∈ R}, whereas the (straight) segment between μ and ν in M(O) is given by {(1 − θ)μ + θν, θ ∈ [0 , 1] }. Of course, if μ and ν are in P(O), then so is the segment between them, thus providing us with a notion of variations on P(O),and more generally on any convex subset of M(O).This notion of variation is compatible with the topology of total variation as, for any μ, ν ∈ M (O),

R 7 → (M(O), | · | )

λ 7 → μ + λν is continuous. Note that if O is a Polish space, p ∈ [1 , ∞), and μ, ν ∈ P p(O), then [0 , 1] 7 →Pp(O), θ 7 → (1 − θ)μ + θν is also continuous for Wp.Typical variations which are very different from straight lines are paths of the form (δx(t))t∈[0 ,1] , where (x(t)) t∈[0 ,1] is a smooth path in O (if it has a structure 20 which allows us to talk about a smooth path). Recall that such paths are not continuous in total variation distance. 

# 2.2 Action of functions 

Another natural type of variations on spaces of measures is given by the action of the semigroup ({f, f : O 7 → O , f measurable }, ◦) on M(O) through the operation 

f#μ for f : O 7 → O and μ ∈ M (O).Given a variation (ft)t∈[0 ,1] on (C(O, O), ∥·∥ ∞) and μ ∈ M (O), we can naturally consider the associated path (( ft)#μ)t∈[0 ,1] in M(O). The previous map is not continuous in general with respect to total variation. Consider for instance the case of translation when O = R. The map h 7 → (τh)#δ0 is not continuous for the total variation distance. However, it behaves nicely with the weak topology. Indeed, if O is a Polish space and ϕ ∈ C b(O) is uniformly continuous, then

Moreover, if we consider μ ∈ P (O), still with O a Polish space, then this type of action is quantifiable in terms of Wasserstein metrics. Indeed, for p ∈ [1 , ∞),

# 2.4 Flows of stochastic differential equations and Fokker-Planck equations 

In the same spirit as the previous section, and keeping O = Td or O = Rd, we can associate to a stochastic differential equation (SDE) a natural evolution on 

P(O). Consider a standard filtered probability space (Ω , A, (Ft)t≥0, P), (Wt)t≥0 astandard k dimensional Brownian motion on it, and the SDE 

dX t = b(t, X t) dt + σ(t, X t) dW t (2.4) where b : R+ × O 7 → Rd and σ : R+ × O 7 → Md×k(R). If (Xt)t≥0 is a weak solution to (2.4) (i.e. a process on possibly another probability space such that for a k

dimensional Brownian motion, the integral representation of (2.4) holds almost surely, together with some integrability conditions), then the evolution of its law 

(mt)t≥0 defines a natural path on P(O). Under appropriate assumptions on b, σ

and the integrability of X0, we can also characterize (mt)t≥0 as the unique weak solution to the following Fokker-Planck equation 

∂tm − 12

> d∑
> i,j =1

∂ij (( σσ T )ij m) + div (b m ) = 0 in (0 , ∞) × O . (2.5) 22 Weak solutions to (2.5) are defined by testing the previous equation against test functions as previously, but the test functions have to be taken with a C2 regularity in the space variable x here. Note that in this stochastic case, the regularity of (mt)t≥0 can still be measured in terms of Wasserstein metrics. Continuity can be obtained using quite standard stochastic analysis techniques. It is also a simple exercise to check that such variations are not Lipschitz continuous in general. This occurs for example in the case O = R, m0 = δ0, b = 0 , k = 1 and σ = 1 .

Remark 2.1. Other interpretations of the Fokker-Planck equation exist, in which it is derived by taking the limit N → ∞ of the empirical measure of a family

# 2.5 Variations associated to couplings and geodesics of optimal transport 

In these last two sections, we consider variations which are not defined as images of functions but rather through some Lagrangian point of view. I restrict the following to probability measures over a convex Polish set O.

Remark 2.2. The Lagrangian point of view is a general and quite ill-defined terminology which refers to the idea of looking at the trajectories of particles rather than at the trajectory of their density (or of the measure which describes their distribution). The latter is referred to as an Eulerian point of view. I come back to this distinction below. 

The most basic example of a Lagrangian variation consists in following a cou-pling. Given γ ∈ P (O2) with marginals μ and ν, thanks to the convexity of O,we can associate to γ a natural path between μ and ν. Indeed, for x, y ∈ O , we consider the path ((1 − t)x + ty )t∈[0 ,1] . The natural Lagrangian variation asso-ciated to γ is then the path (((1 − t)π1 + tπ 2)#γ)t∈[0 ,1] . At the level of random variables, this method is quite linear. Indeed, consider a couple of random vari-ables (X, Y ) ∼ γ. Then, the path we are considering is simply the one given by 

(L((1 − t)X + tY )) t∈[0 ,1] .

The case of optimal couplings When the coupling is optimal for some Wasserstein distance, we usually refer to it as a geodesic between the two measures, in the associated Wasserstein space. 23 Definition 2.3. A constant speed geodesic (or simply geodesic in what follows) of 

Pp(O) is a path (μt)t∈[a,b ], with a < b such that 

∀t, s ∈ [a, b ], Wp(μt, μ s) = |t − s|

b − a Wp(μa, μ b).

In particular, a geodesic is a path of minimal length between its extreme points. For instance, if γ is an optimal coupling in Wp(μ, ν ) for μ, ν ∈ P p(O), then it is a simple exercise to show that μt := ((1 − t)π1 + tπ 2)#γ is a geodesic in Pp(O).To consider more general trajectories than straight lines for the particles, it is natural to proceed as in the next case. 

# 2.6 Smooth Lagrangian flows and other continuity equations 

I conclude this list of variations by considering extensions of the continuity equa-tions presented above. This extension is mainly of interest for the optimal control problems studied in Part III. I consider here O to be either Td or Rd.There are two natural equivalent ways to consider general Lagrangian varia-tions. First, we can consider a random variable X0 on a probability space (Ω , A, P)

with values in O, of law μ, as a way to keep track of the labels of the particles (the labels are here the elements of Ω). Then the trajectories are represented by a stochastic process (Xt)t∈[0 ,1] which is almost surely continuous, with initial condition X0, on the same probability space. In this interpretation, Xt(ω) is the position of the particle labeled ω at time t.Or, we can directly work with a probability measure η ∈ P (C([0 , 1] , O)) , which is none other than the law of the process (Xt)t∈[0 ,1] . The first approach is clearly a lift of the second one, which can thus appear as more canonical. By definition, a Lagrangian path η is an element of P(C([0 , 1] , O)) . It is said to be smooth, Ck,... if it is concentrated on such curves, or equivalently if (Xt)t∈[0 ,1] 

is smooth, Ck,... almost surely. A Lagrangian path yields a variation in P(O) by considering (( et)#η)t∈[0 ,1] ,where I recall that et is the evaluation mapping at time t. It is the canonical map from P(C([0 , 1] , O)) into C([0 , 1] , P(O)) .This Lagrangian perspective is to be thought of in contrast to the Eulerian one, which consists in looking at the continuity equation (2.3) with velocity b.24 When the associated vector field b is smooth, there is a unique Lagrangian flow as-sociated to it because of the uniqueness of the solution to the associated ODE (2.2). When the velocity field is not sufficiently regular, the links between Eulerian and Lagrangian approaches become much more interesting. In such cases, the lack of uniqueness of solutions to (2.3) might raise some issues but a representation of solutions through Lagrangian curves is still possible. It is often referred to as Ambrosio’s superposition principle and can be stated as follows. 

Theorem 2.4. If (mt)t∈[0 ,1] is a continuous curve in P(O) for the weak topology and is a weak solution to (2.3) for a certain drift b such that, for some p ∈ (1 , ∞)

then there exists a process (Xt)t∈[0 ,1] such that mt = L(Xt) for all t and, almost surely in ω ∈ Ω

∞, then (L(Xt)) t∈[0 ,1] is a weak solution to (2.3) with drift b.

# Bibliographical comments 

ODEs, continuity equations and Fokker-Planck equations are now classical objects of mathematics and I will not give a history of their study. ODEs and continuity equations regained interest because of the DiPerna-Lions theory presented notably in [67]. Recent developments in this line on second order equations can be found in the book of Le Bris and Lions [96]. The link between absolutely continuous curves and the continuity equation has been key in the study of gradient flows in the book of Ambrosio, Gigli and Savaré [10], notably following the seminal work of Otto in [108]. Theorems 2.4 and 2.5 are respectively Theorems 8.2.1 and 8.3.1 in [10], where detailed proofs are given. I proposed the formulation of Proposition 2.6 in [21] to study optimal control problems in Pp(Rd). I also refer to the work of Jimenez, Marigonda and Quincampoix [90] for other related developments. 27 3 The flat or vertical derivative on spaces of measures 

This section is concerned with a notion of derivative aligned with the vector space structure of M(O), and more generally, with the inherited one of P(O) as a convex set of a Banach space. What follows is a standard presentation of the concepts of Fréchet or Gateaux differentiability in Banach spaces, adapted to the present case. The objective is here to fix notation and ideas, as all the developments of this section are standard. There are several ways to endow M(O) (or P(O)) with a Banach like structure. In this section, I shall focus on the simplest way to do so, that is to use the norm in total variation |·| and look at the Banach space (M(O), |·| ). Other structures could have been investigated, and I refer to Section 4.6 for such an example. Because I focus on the norm in total variation, in this section O is simply a measurable space. Several computations involving vertical derivatives are presented in Section 4.9.

# 3.1 On the normed vector space of measures 

Consider a function U : M(O) 7 → R. We would like, quite classically, to say that U

is (Fréchet) differentiable at μ0 ∈ M (O) if there exists a bounded linear operator 

A : M(O) 7 → R such that for any ν ∈ M (O),

lim 

> |ν|→ 0

U (μ0 + ν) − U (μ0) − A(ν)

|ν| = 0 .

In the previous the boundedness of A is with respect to the total variation norm. While working with this generality could certainly lead to interesting develop-28 ments, I shall focus here on a more restrictive definition by imposing that A can be represented by an element of B(O), the set of bounded measurable functions on O.

Definition 3.1. A function U : M(O) 7 → R is said to be vertically, flat or V-differentiable at μ0 ∈ M (O) if there exists φ ∈ B (O) such that, for ν ∈ M (O)lim 

> |ν|→ 0

U (μ0 + ν) − U (μ0) − ⟨ φ, ν ⟩|ν| = 0 .

In such a case, we set φ = ∇μU (μ0) or simply φ = ∇U (μ0). The function ∇μU (μ0)

is called the flat or V-derivative of U at μ0.

Remark 3.2. Note that the V-derivative, if it exists, is a bounded function and not an element of L∞(O, R). Working in L∞ is extremely dangerous here as, for 

ϕ ∈ L∞(O, R) and μ ∈ M (O), the product ⟨ϕ, μ ⟩ is not well defined. 

Definition 3.3. A function U : M(O) 7 → R is said to be C1 if it is V-differentiable everywhere and if the map ∇U : ( M(O), | · | ) 7 → (B(O), ∥ · ∥ ∞) is continuous. 

This Fréchet, flat or V-derivative enjoys natural properties on which I do not comment much, and only recall some of them. 

Proposition 3.4. Let U : M(O) 7 → R be V-differentiable at μ ∈ M (O). Then U

is continuous at μ for | · | .

Proposition 3.5. Given a map m : [ −1, 1] 7 → (M(O), | · | ), differentiable at t = 0 ,and a map U : M(O) 7 → R, V-differentiable at m(0) , the composition t 7 → U (m(t)) 

is differentiable at t = 0 and

# 3.2 Vertical derivatives on the space of probability measures 

In several cases, we are given a function U : P(O) 7 → R. The notion of its V-derivative is then slightly more complex than what we had in the previous section. The only point is here to treat the fact that P(O) is not a normed vector space, but only a convex subset of it, whose interior is empty. Once again in this section, we shall endow P(O) with the total variations distance. 29 3.2.1 Gateaux like derivative 

The most common approach in the literature has been to look at a sort of Gateaux derivative, namely by replacing usual variations in the vector space by convex combinations. 

Definition 3.6. A function U : P(O) 7 → R is said to be Gateaux differentiable at 

μ0 ∈ P (O) if there exists φ ∈ B (O) such that, for any ν ∈ P (O)lim 

> θ→0+

U ((1 − θ)μ0 + θν ) − U (μ0)

θ = ⟨φ, ν − μ0⟩. (3.1) In this case, φ is integrated against the difference ν − μ0, and not just ν. This implies that if φ satisfies (3.1) for any ν ∈ P (O), then the same holds for φ + c

for any c ∈ R. Quite often, a convention is then arbitrarily chosen to define the differential of U .

Definition 3.7. Let U : P(O) 7 → R be Gateaux differentiable at μ0 ∈ P (O).We denote by ∇gμU (μ0) or ∇gU (μ0) the unique element φ satisfying (3.1) for any 

ν ∈ P (O), such that ⟨φ, μ 0⟩ = 0 .

Remark 3.8. The previous is a correct definition since if two such elements φ1, φ 2

exist, then, for all ν ∈ P (O), ⟨φ1 − φ2, ν − μ0⟩ = 0 , which implies the equality up to a constant. 

Note that this choice of normalization might have unpleasant consequences, in particular when linking some convexity properties of a function to the sign of its second order derivatives, see 2.7.3 in [87]. As usual Definition 3.6 is much less restrictive than Fréchet derivative type definitions. For instance, it is well known that it does not imply continuity (even in finite dimension). 

3.2.2 Fréchet like derivative 

In order to establish chain rules, or more generally linear approximations of a map 

U around a point of differentiability μ, it is much more natural to work with the following notion of (Fréchet) differentiability. 

Definition 3.9. A function U : P(O) 7 → R is Fréchet or V-differentiable at μ0 ∈P(O) if there exists a bounded measurable φ : O 7 → R such that, with ν ∈ P (O),

lim 

> |ν−μ0|→ 0

U (ν) − U (μ0) − ⟨ φ, ν − μ0⟩|ν − μ0| = 0 .

If this element φ is such that ⟨φ, μ 0⟩ = 0 , we set φ = ∇μU (μ0) or φ = ∇U (μ0).

30 I recall standard properties of such a derivative. 

Proposition 3.10. Let U : P(O) 7 → R be V-differentiable at μ0 ∈ P (O). Then, (i) U is Gateaux differentiable at μ0 and the two derivatives coincide, (ii) U is continuous at μ0 (for the total variation distance), (iii) for a map m : [ −1, 1] 7 → (P(O), | · | ), differentiable at 0 such that m(0) = μ0,

t 7 → U (m(t)) is differentiable at 0 and 

ddt U (m(t)) |t=0 =

> ∫
> O

∇U (m(0))( x)m′(0)( dx ).

Remark 3.11. The notion of Fréchet derivative allows one to obtain Taylor-like expansions of U (m(t)) for more paths m(t) than simply affine ones, which would have been the case for Gateaux derivative. However, note that such generality is still quite restrictive as paths of the form (δx(t))t∈[−1,1] for (x(t)) t∈[−1,1] a C1 curve in O are still prohibited. 

The following result gives a sufficient condition on the Gateaux derivative of a function to state that it is equal to its Fréchet derivative, as is always the case in Banach spaces. 

Proposition 3.12. Let U : P(O) 7 → R be Gateaux differentiable everywhere and such that (P(O), | · | ) 7 → (B(O), ∥ · ∥ ∞)

μ 7 → ∇gU (μ)

is continuous. Then U is V -differentiable everywhere. 

# 3.3 Functions valued in a normed vector space 

The previous presentation is still meaningful when working with functions U :

M(O) 7 → E, for (E, ∥ · ∥ E ) a separable Banach space. The case of functions defined on P(O) is also quite similar. In such a context, the corresponding notion of Fréchet differential is given by 

Definition 3.13. A function U : M(O) 7 → E is said to be Fréchet, vertically or V-differentiable at μ0 ∈ M (O) if there exists a strongly measurable bounded function φ : O 7 → E such that 

lim 

> |ν|→ 0

∥U (μ0 + ν) − U (μ0) − ⟨ φ, ν ⟩∥ E

|ν| = 0 .

In such a case, we set φ = ∇μU (μ0) or simply φ = ∇U (μ0). The function ∇μU (μ0)

is called the flat or Fréchet derivative of U at μ0.

31 In the previous, the integral in ⟨φ, ν ⟩ is defined in the Bochner sense and requires the notion of strong measurability. I do not enter into such developments but refer to II.2 in [66] instead. Then, what has been previously seen can be adapted to this case. An important case is when E is a function space over a space X. In this case, we write U (μ)( x) for the evaluation at x ∈ X of U (μ)

for μ ∈ M (O). The derivative is then denoted through ∇μU (μ)( x)( y) for μ ∈M(O), x ∈ X, y ∈ O . The order between x and y clearly matters (they do not necessarily live in the same space, even if in most applications in MFG, X = O). With this convention, we have for instance for all μ ∈ M (O), x ∈ X, y ∈ O ,

> (

> ϵ→0

U (μ + ϵδ y) − U (μ)

ϵ

> )

(x) = ∇μU (μ)( x)( y).

This might seem confusing but it is the convention used in most of the literature. 

# Bibliographical comments 

The previous standard developments can be found in numerous textbooks, see for instance the one of Brezis [36]. It is probable that mathematicians have considered this notion of differential in the special case of measures or probability measures for quite some time, and it is very difficult to give a starting point. An influential early work is the approach used at the formal level in the so-called Otto calculus [108] as an intermediate computational step. It has also been extensively studied to address MFG master equations, see the course of Lions [98], the book of Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry and Lions [39] or the book of Carmona and Delarue [43] for instance. 32 4 Horizontal derivatives 4.1 Preliminary discussion 

In the previous section, I only used the fact that O is a measurable space. In most applications, we are interested in cases in which O has much more structure, e.g. 

Rd, Td, a domain of Rd or more generally smooth manifolds. Thus, when trying to use topological properties of the underlying space, we are led to work with the topology of weak convergence on the space of measures. When considering a more geometrical notion of derivative, aligned with the Wasserstein distances, it is much simpler to work on P(O) rather than on M(O),and the latter case is not presented in this book. Of course the normalization to 

1 is quite arbitrary and it is the non-negativity and the fixed mass which are of interest. This section is organized as follows: first I introduce the main concept of deriva-tive which is well-suited for all the variations considered above which are not linear combinations. I will then spend some time justifying this notion, making various links with more standard notions, and then present computations. I end this section with the important case of a derivative which is somehow between the vertical one of the previous section and the horizontal one that I am about to present. In all that follows in this section, O is the closure of a smooth convex domain of Rd, equipped with the Euclidean distance, and p ∈ (1 , ∞).

# 4.3 Interpretation of the derivative 

Above, I defined the differentiability of U : Pp(O) 7 → R at μ as the existence of a first order expansion of U of the type 

U (ν) = U (μ) + 

> ∫
> O2

DμU (μ)( x) · (y − x)γ(dx, dy ) + o(Cp(γ)) 

for γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ). I here give some intuition about this definition. 

The direction of the increment: In this point of view, we evaluate the derivative of U in the direction of the coupling γ, which is a Lagrangian direction. Indeed, recall that taking a coupling consists in giving mass to pairs of elements: the pair (x, y ) has a mass γ(dx, dy ). The underlying Lagrangian evolution follows the directions y − x with a weight of γ(dx, dy ). At the level of random variables, if we consider X ∼ μ and Y ∼ ν, we are simply considering the path ((1 − t)X +

tY )t∈[0 ,1] . In this case, the direction of the increment is Y − X, and it is this term that we recover in the (y − x)γ(dx, dy ). Quite naturally, the size of the displacement associated to a coupling γ is quantified by Cp(γ).The more abstract definition of Section 4.8 might also help to understand the term (y − x).

How we evaluate along this increment: There is a quite non-obvious fact in the way we evaluate along such Lagrangian evolutions, and the next section illustrates it. It can be phrased as follows: we are considering Lagrangian paths, but the derivative is somehow Eulerian. This can be observed in (4.1) as the fact that DμU (μ) only depends on x. A priori, this is not obvious. I refer to Theorem 4.6 for reasons why, or to Section 5.4 for the different situation of sub/super differentials, in which it is not the case. 36 4.4 Links with derivatives of functions of random variables

# 4.5 Links with the V-derivative 

Certain smooth functions are expected to be both V-differentiable and H-differentiable, i.e. differentiable in the senses of both Definitions 3.1 and 4.1. For such functions 

U , there is a natural link between ∇μU and DμU that I now present. The following two results state that if U is differentiable in a sense, and its derivative sufficiently smooth, then it is also differentiable in the other sense. In both cases, a notion of regularity has to be used for the derivative and I refer to Section 6 for more on this topic. The main result of this section is the formula (4.5).

# 4.6 The flat W1 case 

At this point, I recall that the description of the vertical (or flat) derivative of Sec-tion 3 was deliberately made provocative by the use of the total variation norm, and other distances could have been considered, with of course some appropriate changes. In this section, I present such a possible point of view, in the case of derivatives of functions U : P1(Rd) 7 → R.Denote by Lip (Rd) the space of Lipschitz functions on Rd, and recall the Kan-torovich duality formula valid for any μ, ν ∈ P 1(O)

W1(μ, ν ) = sup 

> ϕ∈Lip (Rd)Lip (ϕ)≤1
> ∫
> Rd

ϕ d (μ − ν).

The previous duality formula hints that P1(Rd) is a closed convex subset of a Ba-nach space. Instead of choosing this Banach space to be a version of W −1,1(Rd),defined usually as the topological dual of Lip (Rd), I introduce the following. 41 Let E be the Banach space obtained as the completion of the span of Dirac masses on Rd for the norm 

∥μ∥E := sup 

> ϕ
> ∫
> Rd

Proposition 4.14. The topological dual of E is isomorphic to Lip (Rd) endowed with the norm 

∥ϕ∥ := Lip (ϕ) + sup 

> x∈Rd

1 + |x|.

Proof. Let L ∈ E′. Denote ϕ(x) = L(δx). Observe that for x, y ∈ Rd,

|ϕ(x) − ϕ(y)| ≤ ∥ L∥E′ W1(δx, δ y) = ∥L∥E′ |x − y|.

Hence, ϕ is Lipschitz continuous and it is now easy to verify that we can identify 

E′ with a subspace of Lip (Rd). Conversely, every Lipschitz function defines an element of E′ by construction, thus the result holds. 

Proposition 4.15. The Wasserstein space P1(Rd) is a closed convex subset of E.Proof. Let μ ∈ P 1(Rd). By considering a sequence of discrete measures converging toward μ for W1, we obtain that μ ∈ E. It is closed as the distance of E yields the same topology as the W1 norm and the Wasserstein space is complete. The following definition is an analogue of defining the Fréchet derivative for a function defined on a closed convex subset of the Banach space E.

Definition 4.16. A function U : P1(Rd) 7 → R is said to be W1 differentiable at 

μ0 ∈ P 1(Rd) if there exists φ ∈ Lip (Rd) such that 

lim 

> W1(ν,μ 0)→0

U (ν) − U (μ0) − ⟨ φ, ν − μ0⟩W1(ν, μ 0) = 0 .

If φ is such that ⟨φ, μ 0⟩ = 0 , then we set ∇Eμ U (μ0) = φ.

As it was the case for the V-derivative, one can check that two functions φ1, φ 2

satisfying the previous requirement differ by at most a constant, and thus that no-tation ∇Eμ U is also meaningful here. Furthermore, standard properties of Fréchet derivatives also hold in this case. 42 It is tempting to think that the previous notion of derivative allows one to unify the horizontal and vertical points of view in the case P1(Rd). Indeed, if U is W1

differentiable at μ0, then because ∇Eμ U (μ0) is Lipschitz, it is almost everywhere differentiable on Rd. Considering its almost everywhere gradient ∇x∇μU (μ0), we are tempted to say that we recover, because of formula (4.5), the H-derivative of U at μ0. It turns out that it is not the case, because this almost everywhere differentiability of Lipschitz functions is not enough. Indeed, consider the case 

U (μ) = ∫ 

> Rd

|x|μ(dx ). This function U is clearly W1 differentiable everywhere with derivative x 7 → | x| ∈ Lip (Rd). However, it is not horizontally differentiable at δ0

for instance. 

# 4.7 Links with a more geometric notion of derivative 

When defining the horizontal derivative of a function U : Pp(O) 7 → R, we have allowed to consider variations along all couplings. This may seem quite demanding, and a more "geometric" point of view would have consisted in asking information only for couplings which are optimal for Wp. This may seem natural since, after all, we want to go from one measure to the other, not necessarily by arbitrary path. This leads to the definition 

Definition 4.17. A function U : Pp(O) 7 → R is geometrically differentiable at 

μ ∈ P p(O), if there exists ϕ ∈ Lp′

(( O, μ ), Rd) and a modulus of continuity ω such that, for any ν ∈ P p(O), γ optimal coupling in Wp(μ, ν )

> ∣∣∣∣

U (ν) − U (μ) −

> ∫
> O2

ϕ(x) · (y − x)γ(dx, dy ) 

> ∣∣∣∣

≤ ω(Wp(ν, μ )) Wp(ν, μ ).

Of course, Definition 4.1 is more demanding than Definition 4.17, so horizontal differentiability implies geometrical differentiability. A striking result is in fact the following, which is due to Gangbo and Tudorascu in [80], in the case p = 2 , O = Rd.

Theorem 4.18. Let U : P2(Rd) 7 → R be geometrically differentiable at μ. Then U

is horizontally differentiable at μ.

I omit the long and technical proof of this result. Also, the restrictions on p

and O can be relaxed. 

# 4.8 The case of probability measures on manifolds 

When we are concerned with probability measures over manifolds, and not simply over subsets of Rd, some care has to be taken when manipulating the horizontal differential. I present briefly this setting here, both to show this difference, but also as I believe it is a nice way to understand in more details Definition 4.1, and 43 in particular the term (y − x) in the integral. Furthermore, I focus on the case of a compact manifold without boundary, mainly to lighten the notation, but it plays little role in the following. Recall that the vertical point of view does not see the geometry of the space O, so it does not change anything here. Let M be a smooth compact Riemannian manifold without boundary. I adopt the usual notation: for x ∈ M , TxM is the tangent space to M at x, which is equipped with the norm | · | x. The tangent bundle is denoted by T M and I write 

ϕ : M 7 → T M , when, for any x ∈ M, ϕ (x) ∈ TxM . The exponential map is denoted by exp x(v), it is a map T M 7 → M and it yields the value at time t = 1 of the unique geodesic θ : [0 , 1] 7 → M such that θ(0) = x, θ ′(0) = v. When considering a probability measure η ∈ P (T M ), we denote its size by

Similar developments on this notion of derivative can be carried out and are omitted here. I insist on the Definition 4.19 and its link with Definition 4.1. Somehow, the term (y−x) in the integral in Definition 4.1 is not only the difference between the paired points in the coupling γ, but also the initial speed of the path joining them. Hence, instead of writing everything in terms of the coupling 

γ ∈ P (Rd ×Rd) joining the two measures, I could have used (perfectly equivalently) a coupling η ∈ P (Rd ×Rd) defined by η = ( π1, π 2 −π1)#γ. On manifolds, we express the Lagrangian paths in terms of pairs (initial position, speed), while on Rd we can equivalently do it in terms of pairs (initial point, arriving points). Finally, the integrability requirement on ϕ is imposed so that the integral in (4.6) is well defined. If we consider other costs that CM , then we can change the integrability requirement on ϕ as well, namely by using the (Lp, L p′

) duality. 44 4.9 Some useful computations of vertical and horizontal derivatives

Because the flow generated by (4.11) is not regular in either to-tal variation or Wasserstein metrics, additional requirements are needed on the derivatives of U in order to be able to compute the limit.

4.9.5 Wasserstein distances 

In this section, I give a simple result of differentiability of Wasserstein distances. To lighten notation, I restrict my attention to the case O = Rd and p = 2 , even though this does not play a strong role in the following. 

Proposition 4.30. Let μ0, ν ∈ P 2(Rd) be such that the unique optimal coupling in W2(μ0, ν ) is given by (Id, T )#μ0 for some measurable map T . Then, Φ : μ 7 →W22 (μ, ν ) is H-differentiable at μ0 and 

Dμ Φ( μ0)( x) = 2( x − T (x)) .

51 Proof. Let X ∈ H be of law μ0 and consider a sequence (ξn)n≥0 converging toward 

0 in H. Finally, for n ≥ 0, denote by Yn an element of H of law ν such that 

E[|Yn − (X + ξn)|2] ≤ Φ( L(X + ξn)) + ϵn, for ϵn = ∥ξn∥22. By definition of W22 :

Φ( L(X + ξn)) ≤ E[|X + ξn − T (X)|2] = Φ( μ0) + 2 E[( X − T (X)) · ξn] + ϵn,

Φ( μ0) ≤ E[|X−Yn|2] = E[|X+ξn−Yn|2]−2E[( X−T (X)) ·ξn]−ϵn+2 E[( Yn−T (X)) ·ξn].

(4.17) Rearranging leads to 

|Φ( L(X + ξn)) − Φ( μ0) − 2E[( X − T (X)) · ξn]| ≤ ∥ ξn∥2(2 ∥ξn∥2 + ∥Yn − T (X)∥2) + ϵn.

Hence the result follows, provided that lim n→∞ ∥Yn − T (X)∥2 = 0 . Passing to the limit in (4.17) yields that (( X, Y n)) n≥0 converges in law toward (X, T (X)) .Let δ > 0 and denote by f : Rd 7 → Rd a continuous bounded function such that 

∥f (X) − T (X)∥2 ≤ δ. Now remark that 

E[|Yn − T (X)|2] ≤ 2E[|Yn − f (X)|2] + 2 E[|f (X) − T (X)|2]

≤ 2E[|T (X)|2] − 4E[Yn · f (X)] + 2 E[|f (X)|2] + 2 δ2.

Using that (Yn)n≥0 is bounded in H and f is bounded, we obtain that (Yn ·f (X)) n≥0

is uniformly integrable and thus we can pass to the limit n → ∞ in the previous to obtain 

lim  

> n→∞

E[|Yn − T (X)|2] ≤ 2E[|f (X) − T (X)|2] + 2 δ2 ≤ 4δ2.

Hence the result follows. 

Remark 4.31. The previous result is in fact an equivalence, as the H-differentiability of Φ implies the uniqueness of an optimal coupling, which has to be deterministic. I refer to Alfonsi and Jourdain [6] Theorem 3.2 for a proof of this reciprocal. Iencourage the reader to verify as an exercise, when O = R and ρ is the normal distribution, that W 22 (·, ρ ) is not differentiable at δ0.

# Bibliographical comments 

The starting point of the development on horizontal derivatives is often taken as the introduction of a formal calculus by Otto in [108]. The study of gradient flows in Wasserstein spaces grew rapidly, and with it more precise developments of the horizontal derivative (sometimes called Wasserstein gradient). A sub-differential calculus can be found in the book of Ambrosio, Gigli and Savaré [10]. The latter is much more in the spirit of the geometric derivative presented in Section 4.7. 52 A breakthrough came through the lift of Lions (Section 4.4), which made pos-sible to define a notion of derivative of a function U : P2(Rd) 7 → R through the notion of Fréchet differentiability of its lift U : H 7 → R. This made possible to show the structure of the gradient of the lift as a consequence of the dependence on the law only, Lemma 4.7. These developments, mainly motivated by the study of MFG, were presented by Lions in his lectures [98, 99] and can be found in the notes of Cardaliaguet [37]. More precise developments can be found in Chapter 5 of the book of Carmona and Delarue [43], especially on the link between the verti-cal and the horizontal notions. As mentioned above, the equivalence between the restriction or not to optimal couplings was established by Gangbo and Tudorascu in [80]. Theorem 4.6 is a local refinement of previous results which were stated for functions which were differentiable at more than one single point. It can be found in [80] or in Alfonsi and Jourdain [6]. The more restrictive results which were generalized by it can be found in [37, 43, 99]. See also the thesis of Ceccherini Silberstein [48] for general notions of derivatives on Wasserstein spaces on smooth manifolds. 53 5 Convexity, monotonicity and sub-differential calculus 

This section is concerned with two notions of convexity, each linked with a different geometry on spaces of measures. One is the usual one and is related to the notion of vertical differentiability, while the other one is concerned with Lagrangian dis-placements and thus naturally linked to horizontal differentiability. En passant , I will also give associated definitions and properties of monotone mappings, namely because of the fundamental role that monotonicity plays in the study of mean field game master equations. After exploring notions of differentiability in the two previous sections, I will introduce here the weaker notion of sub-differentiability. This notion is particularly helpful in optimization and it will play a strong role in the study of Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equations in Part III. The main link between convexity and sub-differentiability is through the classical result: a convex function is everywhere sub-differentiable in the interior of its domain. This result is true in Banach spaces, or more generally in topological vector spaces in which the geometric version of Hahn-Banach separation Theorem holds. 

# 5.1 Flat convexity and monotonicity 

In this section, O is a measurable set. The notion of flat convexity, as the one of flat differentiability, is directly in-herited from the structure of vector space of M(O). It can be stated as follows. 

Definition 5.1. • A set X ⊂ M (O) is flat convex, or simply convex, if, for all μ, ν ∈ X, t ∈ [0 , 1] , (1 − t)μ + tν ∈ X. 

• Let X ⊂ M (O) be a convex set. The function U : X 7 → R ∪ { + ∞} is convex if its epigraph {(v, μ ) ∈ R× X| v ≥ U (μ)} is convex for the product convexity on R×X issued from the flat convexity, or equivalently, if μ, ν ∈ X, t ∈ [0 , 1] 

U ((1 − t)μ + tν ) ≤ (1 − t)U (μ) + tU (ν).

• A function U is concave if −U is convex. 

Standard properties of convex functions and sets hold in this case, and thus I state without proof the usual result. 

Proposition 5.2. • An intersection of convex sets is convex. 

• For any bounded measurable f : O 7 → R, the map μ 7 → ⟨ f, μ ⟩ is convex. 

54 • Let X ⊂ M (O) be a convex set, then a point-wise supremum of convex functions defined on X is convex. 

Furthermore, this notion is also compatible with the discretization evoked in Section 4.9.3. 

Proposition 5.3. Let U : M(O) 7 → R be flat convex and consider, for a finite family of measures (μi)i∈I , the function v defined in (4.15) . Then v is convex on 

RI .

The immediate proof is left as an exercise. As in finite dimensional cases, we can define an associated notion of mono-tonicity. 

Definition 5.4. Let A ⊂ M (O) be a convex set. A set valued function F : A ⇒

(M(O)) ′ is said to be 

• (flat) monotone if for all μ, ν ∈ A, f ∈ F (μ), g ∈ F (ν)

⟨f − g, μ − ν⟩ ≥ 0,

• strictly (flat) monotone if for all μ, ν ∈ A, with μ̸ = ν, f ∈ F (μ), g ∈ F (ν)

⟨f − g, μ − ν⟩ > 0.

The previous applies naturally when F is a single valued mapping. The next classical result is stated without proof. 

Proposition 5.5. Let U : M(O) 7 → R be a V-differentiable function. Then 

∇μU : M(O) 7 → (M(O)) ′ is flat monotone if and only if U is convex. 

# 5.2 Flat super-differentials 

Let O be a measurable space. Since (M(O), | · | ) is a Banach space, a super-differential calculus can be defined on it. Here, I recall such a classical notion. To obtain results such as the sub-differentiability of convex functions, I will not be able to restrict my attention to elements in (M(O)) ′ which can be represented as bounded measurable functions, as I did for vertical derivatives. 

Definition 5.6. Let U : M(O) 7 → R be a usc function. We say that ℓ ∈ (M(O)) ′

belongs to the flat super-differential of U at μ and we write ℓ ∈ ∂+

> f lat

U (μ) when there exists a modulus of continuity ω(·) such that for all ν ∈ M (O)

U (ν) ≤ U (μ) + ℓ(ν − μ) + ω(|ν − μ|)|ν − μ|.

By definition, ∂+

> f lat

U (μ) is the set of such ℓ.For a lsc function V : M(O) 7 → R, the set ∂−

> f lat

V (μ) is equal to −∂+(−V )( μ).

55 Remark 5.7. Following Section 4.6, a similar notion can be developed if we are interested in (E, ∥ · ∥ W −1,1 ), which has the advantage of having a more convenient dual. 

These super-differentials enjoy several standard properties such as the ones listed in the following proposition. 

Proposition 5.8. Let U : M(O) 7 → R be a usc function. (i) If U attains a maximum at μ ∈ M (O), then 0 ∈ ∂+

> f lat

U (μ).(ii) If U is V-differentiable at μ ∈ M (O), then ∂+

> f lat

U (μ) = ∂−

> f lat

U (μ) = ∇U (μ).(iii) If U is concave, then for all μ ∈ M (O), ∂+

> f lat

U (μ)̸ = ∅. Furthermore, μ 7 →−∂+

> f lat

U (μ) is a monotone operator. Proof. Points (i) and (ii ) are trivial. We do not have the usual other implication in 

(ii ) since I restricted the notion of differentiability to elements which are bounded measurable functions. Point (iii ) follows from the Hahn-Banach Theorem. Indeed, since U is concave, the epigraph of −U is convex, and because U is defined every-where and usc, the interior of this epigraph is non-empty. Separating (−U (μ), μ )

from this interior yields that ∂+

> f lat

U (μ)̸ = ∅. The second part of (iii ) follows from an immediate computation. 

Remark 5.9. As usual in Banach spaces, when we are given an element in the sub-differential of a flat convex function, we know that we can choose the associated modulus of continuity to be 0, given that we are in the interior of its domain of definition. Thus for flat convex functions, sub-differentiability is in fact a global property. Note that the previous proof directly yields such a global element. 

# 5.3 Coupling convexity 

In this section, O is the closure of a smooth convex domain of Rd. I introduce here a not so usual notion of convexity on P(O), for which I will quickly make links with more standard notions. 

5.3.1 Basic definitions and examples 

Definition 5.10. • A set A ⊂ P (O) is coupling convex if, for all μ, ν ∈ A,for all t ∈ [0 , 1] , γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ), it holds that ((1 − t)π1 + tπ 2)#γ ∈ A.

• Let A ⊂ P (O) be a coupling convex set. A function U : A 7 → R ∪ { + ∞} 

is coupling convex if its epigraph {(v, μ ) ∈ R × A| v ≥ U (μ)} is convex for 

56 the product convexity on R × A issued from the coupling convexity on A, or equivalently, if for all μ, ν ∈ A, γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ), t ∈ [0 , 1] 

U (((1 − t)π1 + tπ 2)#γ) ≤ (1 − t)U (μ) + tU (ν).

• A function U is concave if −U is convex. 

This notion of convexity enjoys some non-usual properties listed in the next example. 

Example 5.11. • The set {μ} is coupling convex if and only if μ is a Dirac mass. 

• As a consequence: let μ0 ∈ P p(O) and let U : Pp(O) 7 → R ∪ { + ∞} defined as 0 on μ0 and +∞ everywhere else. Then U is convex along couplings if and only if μ0 is a Dirac mass. 

• Assume O = [ −1, 1] . The function μ 7 → ∫

5.3.2 Regularity of coupling convex functions 

Maybe a striking argument to link the previous notion of convexity with the ge-ometry of Wasserstein spaces is the fact that convexity along couplings implies regularity in Wasserstein distances. I present two results here, the first one in the case O = Rd and the second one which deals with a more compact case. I do not consider here functions which take the value +∞, even if the case in which O is compact can be seen as a case in which U (μ) = + ∞ if μ / ∈ P (O).

Proposition 5.15. Let p ∈ [1 , ∞), U : Pp(Rd) 7 → R be coupling convex and locally bounded (i.e. bounded on bounded sets). Then U is locally Lipschitz continuous, i.e. for any R > 0, there exists CR > 0 such that for any μ, ν ∈ P p(Rd) with 

Mp(μ), M p(ν) ≤ Rp,

|U (μ) − U (ν)| ≤ CRWp(μ, ν ).

Proof. The proof follows the usual one in vector spaces. For R > 0 define BR := 

{μ ∈ P p(Rd)|Mp(μ) ≤ Rp}. Let R > 0 and μ, ν ∈ BR, μ ̸ = ν, with Wp(μ, ν ) < 1.Then, for any t ∈ (0 , 1) there exists ˜μ ∈ P p(Rd) and γ ∈ Π( ν, ˜μ) such that 

μ = ((1 − t)π1 + tπ 2)#γ. Indeed, for this, we consider ˜γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ) and define 

˜μ := ( t−1(π1 − π2) + π2)# ˜γ. Then, by coupling convexity 

U (μ) ≤ (1 − t)U (ν) + tU (˜ μ).

Setting t = Wp(μ, ν ), we obtain 

U (μ) − U (ν) ≤ (U (˜ μ) − U (ν)) Wp(μ, ν ).

Proposition 5.16. Let O be the closure of a convex bounded domain of Rd. Let

Remark 5.17. This type of Lipschitz regularity might seem quite disappointing: why not obtain a stronger Lipschitz estimate, in W1 norm for instance ? It so happens that there are some monsters in the set of coupling convex functions over 

P(O) as the next example shows. It is somehow coherent with the fact that, if we interpret P(O) as a subset of some Pp(Rd), then, it contains no open ball in Wp

distances, only open balls in W∞.

# 5.4 Horizontal super-differentials 

In this section, O is still the closure of a smooth convex domain of Rd, and I focus on the case of super-differentials of functions on Pp(O) for 1 < p < ∞. For most of what follows, the convexity assumption on O can be relaxed quite easily, but then the formalism of manifolds introduced in Section 4.8 is more natural. The following notion of super-differential is much more involved and I start by explaining briefly why a naive definition is not appropriate, even if the main motivation shall come later on, when trying to prove comparison of viscosity solutions of HJB equations on Pp(O), in Part III. 

5.4.1 A first attempt 

Let U : Pp(O) 7 → R be a usc function. A first natural attempt to define horizontal super-differentials (which I will simply call super-differential in this section) is to take the definition of the horizontal differential and only ask for an inequality, i.e. we could say that ϕ is in the super-differential of U at μ if there exists a modulus of continuity ω such that for any ν ∈ P p(O), γ ∈ Π( μ, ν )

U (ν) ≤ U (μ) + 

> ∫
> O2

ϕ(x) · (y − x)γ(dx, dy ) + Cp(γ)ω(Cp(γ)) . (5.1) This point of view is too restrictive. Even if it is clear that such functions ϕ ∈ Lp′

> μ

are clearly elements of the super-differential, there are other ones that we shall need later on, especially in Part III. 60 The best arguments in favor of seeking a more general notion are to come later on. At this time, we can only argue using the interpretation of the lift of Section 4.4. Theorem 4.6 states that if U : H depends only on the law of its argument and is differentiable at some X ∈ H 7 → R, then ∇U (X) is a deterministic function of X. Such a statement has no reason to hold at the level of super-differential: if 

Z ∈ ∂+U(X), then Z is not necessarily a deterministic function of X. Thus we shall keep track of this "extra" randomness, and the objective of what follows is to explain how we can do it. 

5.4.2 Extending the super-differentials 

In this section, let p ∈ (1 , ∞). In order not to miss essential elements of super-differentials, I shall use the following definition, which can be seen as an extension of (5.1), which we could have first tried to use. In the following, for any Γ ∈P(O × Rd × O ), I use the notation ˜Cp(Γ) = Cp(( π1, π 3)#Γ) .

The previous definition allows one to mix, or randomize, the value of the ele-ment in the super-differential over x ∈ O , namely in such a way that the law of this randomization is ψx(dz ) ∈ P (Rd). 5.4.3 Sub-differentiability of coupling convex functions 

The aim of this section is to present what the analogue of the result: "a convex function is sub-differentiable on the interior of its domain" might be in this context of coupling convex functions. As the interior of the domain of a coupling convex function is technical to manipulate, I will present only some partial results here. I start by giving a result in the direction of Remark 5.9: for coupling convex functions, sub-differentiability is a global property. 

Proposition 5.29. Let O be a convex closed set, p ∈ (1 , ∞) and U : Pp(O) 7 →

Theorem 5.30. Let p ∈ (1 , ∞) and U : Pp(Rd) 7 → R be coupling convex and locally bounded. Then U is (horizontally) sub-differentiable everywhere, further-more, at any point μ, the modulus of continuity ω in the expression of the sub-differentiability can be replaced by 0.
