x 7 → − √x is not sub-differentiable everywhere on [0 , ∞). Proposition 5.33. Assume that O is compact (in addition to being convex closed already). Let U : P(O) 7 → R be coupling convex. Then, for every μ ∈ P (O) whose support is at distance at least ϵ > 0 of ∂O, there exists ℓ ∈ (L∞(Ω , Rd)) ∗ such that for any ν ∈ P (O), γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ) and (X, Y ) ∼ γU (ν) ≥ U (μ) + ℓ(Y − X).

Proof. Consider the lift U of U in EO := {X ∈ L∞(Ω , Rd), P(X ∈ O ) = 1 }, which is a convex subset of L∞(Ω , Rd). That is, U(X) = U (L(X)) if X ∈ EO, and 

U(X) = + ∞ else. Take μ as in the statement. As we saw above, any X ∼ μ lies in an open ball of EO for the ∥ · ∥ ∞ norm. Since U is convex on EO and X lies in the interior of the domain of U, we deduce that U is sub-differentiable at that point. We denote by ℓ ∈ (L∞)∗ this element in the sub-differential. The result follows. 

Remark 5.34. This remark is not directly related to the previous Proposition but had to be made somewhere. In this non-usual geometry, Dirac masses are not necessarily the extreme points of P(O), these are rather the measures which put mass on ∂O, contrary to what happens in the flat geometry where Dirac masses are the extreme points of P(O).

5.4.4 Coupling monotonicity 

I focus on the case O = Rd to simplify notations. Let p ∈ (1 , ∞) and μ ∈ P p(Rd).Consider the set 

Pμp′ := {γ ∈ P pp ′ (Rd × Rd), (π1)#γ = μ}.

Recall that thanks to the disintegration Theorem, sub and super-differentials of functions U : Pp(Rd) 7 → R at μ can be seen as subsets of Pμp′ . As a convention, I no write te A : Pp(Rd) ⇒ ∪μ∈P p(Rd)Pμp′ when for all μ ∈ P p(Rd), A(μ) ⊂ P μp′ .70 The notion of monotonicity associated to coupling convexity is defined as fol-lows. 

Definition 5.35. Let A : Pp(Rd) ⇒ ∪μ∈P p(Rd)Pμp′ . It is said to be coupling mono-tone if for all μ, ν ∈ P p(Rd), γ μ ∈ A(μ), γ ν ∈ A(ν) and Θ ∈ Π( γμ, γ ν ),

> R4d

(z1 − z2) · (y − x)Θ( dx, dz 1, dy, dz 2) ≥ 0.

The next result links coupling monotonicity and coupling convexity. 

Proposition 5.36. Let U : Pp(Rd) 7 → R be coupling convex and consider A :

Pp(Rd) ⇒ ∪μ∈P p(Rd)Pμp′ defined for μ ∈ P p(Rd) by 

A(μ) = {γ ∈ P pp ′ (Rd × Rd)| γ ∈ ∂−U (μ)}.

Then A is coupling monotone. Proof. The proof is standard. Let μ, ν, γ μ, γ ν , Θ be as in Definition 5.35. Then using γμ ∈ ∂−U (μ) for the first inequality and γν ∈ ∂−U (ν) for the second one (without modulus of continuity thanks to Proposition 5.29),

which yields the result. Just as it was the case for coupling convexity, coupling monotonicity can also be understood through the lifting procedure. Consider an operator A : Pp(Rd) ⇒

∪μ∈P p(Rd)Pμp′ . Define its lift ˜A : Lp ⇒ Lp′

through 

˜A = {Y ∈ Lp′

| L (X, Y ) ∈ A(L(X)) }.

The following holds trivially, thanks to Theorem 1.9. 

Proposition 5.37. The operator A is coupling monotone if and only if ˜A is mono-tone (in the usual sense in Banach spaces).

# 5.5 Sub-differential calculus on the torus 

In this section, I take some time to make explicit extensions of several results above to the case of the flat torus Td, since it will be of interest in Part III. Morally, sub-differentials have the same properties as the ones we just saw, the only issue is that the term y − x in the definition of the sub/super-differential makes no longer sense on the torus. Up to the addition of an element in Zd, y − x is the speed of a geodesic joining x to y in time 1. However, there may be multiple geodesics in this situation and thus some care is needed. Observe that in this flat case, the exponential map is simply defined as exp( x, v ) = x + v modulo Zd.I start by giving the definitions of sub/super-differential and by recalling that, since Td is a proper manifold, the Definition 4.19 is the appropriate notion of H-differential in this context. In this case, for η ∈ P 2(Td × Rd), I define 

CTd (η) =

Since notions of convexity on Td are more involved, I do not enter in the link between sub-differentiability and convexity in this context. Instead, I start by showing that W22 is still super-differentiable everywhere in this setting. 

Proposition 5.43. Remark 5.44. Let me note that the set of ζ as in the statement of the theorem is not empty and that, for instance, ζ(x, y ) can be defined as the unique element in 

[−12 , 12 )d such that, modulo the addition of an element in Zd, ζ(x, y ) = x − y.

I now turn to the analogue of Proposition 5.27, which has the virtue of insisting upon the fact that the proof of Theorem 4.6, originally due to Alfonsi and Jourdain in [6], is much more general than Pp(Rd).74 Proposition 5.45. Let U : P(Td) 7 → R and μ ∈ P (Td) such that U is both sub and super-differentiable at μ. Then U is H-differentiable at μ and ∂+U (μ) = 

∂−U (μ) = {x 7 → δDμU (μ)( x)}.Proof. I argue as in Proposition 5.27 and leave the extension of the first step of the proof to this case as an exercise (i.e. to show that ∂+U (μ) = ∂−U (μ) = {ψ}). It thus remains to show that this map ψ is deterministic. Once again, we can construct exactly the same perturbations ξ± as we did in the proof of Theorem 4.6, around a certain random variable X ∼ μ. The crucial point on Td is now to understand the sum X + ϵξ ±. For this we use the exponential map and consider 

X± = exp( X, ϵξ ±). To conclude the proof as we did in Theorem 4.6, it then suffices to show that L(X+) = L(X−). But since Td is a smooth manifold whose injectivity radius is bounded from below uniformly, the previous holds for ϵ small enough, since |ξ±| ≤ 1. In other words, for ϵ small enough, the variations ϵξ ± do not cross the cut-locus of Td. The rest of the proof then follows. Finally, I state without proof (which is a good exercise for the interested reader), the following. 

Proposition 5.46. Let U : P(Td) 7 → R.(i) If V : P(Td) 7 → R is (horizontally) differentiable at μ, then 

ψ ∈ ∂+U (μ) ⇔ x 7 → (Id + DV (μ)( x)) #ψx(dz ) ∈ ∂+(U + V )( μ).

(ii) Let V : P(Td) 7 → R. Then, for all ψ ∈ ∂+U (μ), ϕ ∈ ∂+V (μ), Θ ∈ P (Td ×

Rd × Rd) such that (π1, π 2)#Θ = μ(dx )ψx(dz ) and (π1, π 3)#Θ = μ(dx )ϕx(dz ),it holds that (π1, π 2 + π3)#Θ ∈ ∂+(V + U )( μ).(iii) If ψ ∈ ∂+U (μ), then for all λ ≥ 0, (x 7 → (λId )#ψx(dz )) ∈ ∂+(λU )( μ).(iv) Let ψ, ϕ ∈ ∂+U (μ), then, for any Θ ∈ P (Td ×Rd ×Rd) such that (π1, π 2)#Θ =

# 5.6 Geodesic convexity 

I conclude this section on convexity by mentioning the notion of geodesic convexity, which plays an important role, namely in the proof of several functional inequalities or in the study of gradient flows , but not in this book. I consider here O to be the closure of a smooth convex domain of Rd and start with the following. 

Definition 5.47. • A set A ⊂ P p(O) is geodesic convex if it contains all geodesics (in Pp(O)) between any pairs of its elements. 

75 • A function U : Pp(O) 7 → R ∪ { + ∞} is geodesic convex if, for any μ, ν ∈Pp(O), γ optimal in Wp(μ, ν ) and t ∈ [0 , 1] 

U (((1 − t)π1 + tπ 2)#γ) ≤ (1 − t)U (μ) + tU (ν).

The notion of geodesic convexity is more intuitive, for instance, we recover here that any singleton is geodesic convex. Clearly, coupling convexity is a stronger notion than geodesic convexity. Interestingly, for continuous functions which are finite everywhere, the two notions are equivalent, provided that d the dimension of the base space, is at least 2.

Proposition 5.48. Let d ≥ 2, U : Pp(O) 7 → R be a continuous geodesic convex function for 1 ≤ p < ∞. Then U is coupling convex. Proof. I argue by approximation, proving first that it is the case for coupling between finitely supported measures and then argue by density. Let N > 1 be an integer and consider CN the set of discrete measures μ of the form 

μx := 

> N∑
> i=1

1

N δxi ,

for x = ( xi)1≤i≤N ∈ (O)N , which are not supposed to be distinct. The two main observations on which this proof relies are: i) for paths of the form (μxt )t∈[0 ,1] 

where there are no collisions in (xt)t∈[0 ,1] , there exists a finite open cover (Ik) of 

[0 , 1] such that (μxt )t∈[0 ,1] restricted to each of the Ik is a geodesic; ii) around any segment x ∈ (O)N , there are collision-less segments in (O)N . I prove those two points first, and then explain how to use them to obtain the result. 

Step 1: Convexity along collisionless segment 

A segment in (O)N is a path of the form ((1 − t)x + ty )t∈[0 ,1] for x, y ∈ (O)N . It is called collisionless if the cardinality of the support of μxt is a constant in time. If the constant is equal to N for all time, then the terminology is quite transparent, but more generally, it allows for two (or more) particles to stick together during the whole time interval [0 , 1] . It is now a simple observation, relying on the continuity of the path in each of the coordinates, that around such a configuration at any time 

t0 ∈ (0 , 1) , there exists some ϵ > 0 such that (μxt )t∈[t0−ϵ,t 0+ϵ] is a geodesic, with an 

ϵ which depends only on the infimum between the positive distances between the particles. This follows from a computation that I leave to the interested reader. From the previous fact, we indeed obtain the open cover of [0 , 1] , on which the map t 7 → U (μxt ) is convex in any subintervals. Thus the map t 7 → U (μxt ) is in fact convex on [0 , 1] .

Step 2: Density of collisionless segments 

Let x, y ∈ (O)N and consider the segment ((1 − t)x + ty )t∈[0 ,1] . Clearly, such a 76 segment has collisions in general, for instance consider the case N = 2 , O = R2

and x = ((0 , 0) , (1 , 0)) , y = ((1 , 1) , (0 , 1)) , which has a collision at time t = 12 .However, such a system can be perturbed slightly so that no collision happens, for instance by replacing y by ((1 , 1 − ϵ), (0 , 1)) . I insist upon the fact that a collision is not the crossing of the trajectories of the particles, but the fact that, when the trajectories cross, the particles are at this crossing point at the same time. I do not reproduce the quite long computations of such a fact in general. I hope the reader is convinced by my small example and refer her or him to Proposition 6.4 in [47] for a complete proof. 

Step 3: Conclusion 

Let μ, ν ∈ P p(O) and γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ). I want to prove that U (((1 − t)π1 + tπ 2)#γ) ≤

(1 − t)U (μ) + tU (ν). Consider a sequence of empirical measures of the form 

N −1 ∑Ni=1 δ(xNi ,y Ni ) converging toward γ for Wp. Consider the associated segment 

((1 − t)xN + ty N )t∈[0 ,1] . From Step 2 , there exists (˜ xN , ˜yN ), at ∥ · ∥ ∞ distance of 

(xN , y N ) arbitrary close to 0, such that ((1 −t)˜ xN +t˜yN )t∈[0 ,1] is collisionless. Apply-ing Step 1 , for any t ∈ [0 , 1] , U (μ(1 −t)˜ xN +t˜yN ) ≤ (1 − t)U (μ˜xN ) + tU (μ˜yN ). Passing to the limit in the approximation of Step 2 , it follows that U (μ(1 −t)xN +ty N ) ≤

(1 − t)U (μxN ) + tU (μyN ) because of the continuity of U . Passing to the limit 

N → ∞ yields the required result. Note the importance of the dimension criteria d ≥ 2 in Step 2 of the proof. In fact, the result does not hold in dimension of the base space d = 1 , a counterex-ample can be found in [110]. From the previous result, the interest of geodesic convex functions (compared to coupling convexity) only arises when looking at more singular functions. Atypical example of such a function is the entropy E : P2(Rd) 7 → R ∪ { + ∞} defined in (4.9). 

Proposition 5.49. The entropy E is geodesic convex on P2(Rd) but not coupling convex. Proof. I only sketch the proof in P2(R), and refer to [104] for more details. Let 

μ, ν ∈ P 2(R) and consider (μt)t∈[0 ,1] a geodesic joining the two. If μ has no density, then, there is nothing to prove. If it is not the case, we know that there exists a non-decreasing map T : R 7 → R such that T#μ = ν. I argue as if T were differentiable. Furthermore, μt = ((1 − t)Id + tT )#μ. For all t ∈ [0 , 1) , μt has a density, and we can compute 

E(μt) = 

> ∫
> R

μ(x) log  

> (

μ(x)(1 − t) + tT ′(x)

> )

dx 

77 Using the concavity of the log , we thus obtain that E is geodesic convex. To show that it is not coupling convex, consider for instance the case in which μ

is the uniform distribution on [0 , 1] and compute the entropy of (π1+π2 

> 2

)#μ(dx )μ(dy ).It turns out that many evolutionary PDEs can be written as gradient flows (with respect to some Wasserstein metrics) of geodesic convex functionals, such as the entropy E. In the case of E, I leave as an exercise to verify that this PDE is the heat equation, which can be intuited by combining the computation for the H-derivative of E of Section 4.9 and formula ∆m = div (∇mm m). The geodesic convexity of the potential of the gradient flow translates into various useful quantitative stability properties of the associated PDE. 

# Bibliographical comments 

The notion of convexity, or the study of convex functions on normed vector space is the subject of many excellent textbooks, see for instance the book of Ekeland and Temam [70]. The notion of flat monotonicity shall play a fundamental role in the study of MFG master equations below. The study of coupling convexity for functions of probability measures has been much less studied. There have been recent developments, namely using coupling monotonicity, to provide complete studies of evolution equations in Wasserstein spaces, see for instance the work of Cavagnari, Savaré and Sodini [45, 46, 47]. In particular Proposition 5.48 is borrowed from [47]. In these works, the terminology used is the one of totally convex functions for the convexity, and the operators for which I defined a notion of coupling monotonicity are called multi-valued proba-bility vector field. I refer to the work of Pinzi and Savaré [112] for developments on the link between coupling convexity and general horizontal sub-differentiability. The notion of coupling convexity was studied by Lions, namely for its associated notion of monotonicity [101]. See also the respective books of Villani and San-tambrogio [114, 119] or the work of Parker [110] for developments on geodesic convexity. In fact, the notion of geodesic convexity (which is classical notion in the study of Riemannian manifolds) has attracted most of the attention on Wasser-stein spaces, since the seminal work of McCann [104]. The main reason for this is the importance of geodesic convex (and not coupling convex) functions which appear in the study of gradient flows in Wasserstein spaces, such as the entropy for instance. An associated notion of monotonicity has also been studied, namely for its role in MFG, see for instance the works of Gangbo, Meszaros, Mou and Zhang [78, 79] for instance. 78 I choose to focus the presentation on coupling convex functions, since, when interested in regular, say continuous, functions, this notion seems more natural. Moreover, the associated notion of monotonicity has also been proved of interest in works of Meynard [32, 105]. I believe the current presentation is quite new, in particular its links with the notion of horizontal sub-differentials. The current horizontal sub-differential calculus is presented in more details in Chapter 10 of the book of Ambrosio, Gigli and Savaré [10]. It plays a fundamental role in the study of HJB equation on Wasserstein spaces presented in Part III. Proposition 5.23 is a consequence of Theorem 3.17 in [80]. Proposition 5.49 is proven in [104]. 79 6 C1,α regularity of a function of a probability measure 

I present here C1,α spaces of regularity for functions of measures. There will be two definitions, one associated with the vertical point of view, and one with the horizontal one. As a convention, C1,1 stands for the Lipschitz regularity of the derivative and C0,1 for the Lipschitz regularity of the function itself. This section is quite independent of the rest of this book, but serves as a good illustration of differences between vertical and horizontal derivatives. I will not address the question of second order derivatives. 

# 6.1 Hölder regularity of the flat differential 

Let O be a measurable space and U : M(O) 7 → R be a flat differentiable function. For any μ ∈ M (O), recall that the V-derivative ∇U (μ) belongs to B(O), the space of bounded measurable functions, which is a normed vector space equipped with the ∥ · ∥ ∞ norm. Hence, continuity or Hölder regularity of ∇U is understood quite naturally through the following definition. 

Definition 6.1. Let U : M(O) 7 → R be V-differentiable and consider the map 

∇U : ( M(O), | · | ) 7 → (B(O), ∥ · ∥ ∞),μ 7 → ∇U (μ).

The function U is said to be: 

• V-C1,0 if ∇U is continuous. 

• V-C1,α for α ∈ (0 , 1] if ∇U is C0,α Hölder continuous. 

Standard developments can then be made and I do not go into further details here. 

# 6.2 Hölder regularity of the horizontal differential 

The natural aspect of the previous section completely disappears as soon as we are trying to use this approach on the horizontal differential. Indeed, let O be the closure of a smooth convex domain of Rd and consider a H-differentiable map 

U : Pp(O) 7 → R for 1 < p < ∞. Then, trying to give a notion of regularity of 

DU , we have to compare, as ν and μ get close to one another, DU (μ) and DU (ν).However, those two elements lie in respectively Lp′ 

> μ

(Rd) and Lp′ 

> ν

(Rd), which makes such a comparison far from obvious... 80 6.2.1 A notion of parallel transport 

Such a problem is classical in geometry. Indeed, for ϕ a smooth function over a Riemannian manifold M , at any x ∈ M , the differential of ϕ at x belongs to TxM .Moreover, for x̸ = y, comparing elements in TxM and TyM is not obvious. The standard tool is to use what is called parallel transport . The parallel transport of vector v ∈ TxM along a smooth curve θ : [0 , 1] 7 → M is a smooth curve valued in 

T M , the tangent bundle of M . This curve has to enjoy several properties, notably that it preserves angles (hence the terminology parallel transport). I will not need to use the concept of parallel transport in a general manifold in detail, and refer to classical textbooks on differential geometry for more on this concept. Observe that parallel transport is trivial in Rd, it is simply a translation. In particular, the parallel transport along a curve in Rd simply depends on the starting and ending points of the curve. The aim of the present section is to explain how we can use the classical par-allel transport as a heuristic for "parallel transporting" an element ϕ ∈ Lp′ 

> μ

into something more fitting for a comparison with an element ψ ∈ Lp′ 

> ν

. As we shall see, we will not be able to transport ϕ to an element of Lp′ 

> ν

in general. To realize such a parallel transport, a curve between μ and ν needs to be prescribed. It is the curve along which the transport shall be made. Since we are concerned with the horizontal derivative, it should be natural to look for a Lagrangian curve. Let μ ∈ P p(O), η ∈ P (C([0 , 1] , O)) such that (e0)#η = μ and 

ϕ ∈ Lp′ 

> μ

(Rd). Given a path θ in the support of η, the objective is to parallel transport ϕ(θ(0)) along the curve θ. Since at this level, parallel transport is trivial (we are in Rd), this procedure simply consists in assigning ϕ(θ(0)) to θ(1) . Hence, the path θ plays a role only through its starting and ending point. Thus, we can simply work with the coupling γ = ( e0, e 1)#η ∈ P p(O × O ) instead of the whole 

η. In cases in which the base space is not Rd, much more care is needed. The previous discussion leads to the following. 

Definition 6.2. Let μ, ν ∈ P p(O). A parallel transport of ϕ ∈ Lp′ 

> μ

(O, Rd) along the coupling γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ) is a probability measure Γ ∈ Π( μ(dx )δϕ(x)(dz ), ν ) such that (π1, π 3)#Γ = γ. Furthermore, we say that ϕ is parallel transported (by Γ,along γ) onto ϕ′ where (π3, π 2)#Γ = ν(dy )ϕ′

> y

(dz ).

This definition is quite transparent at the level of the lift in the case p =2, O = Rd. Indeed, if we take X ∈ H of law μ and interpret Z = ϕ(X) ∈ H as an element of the tangent space to H at X, then its parallel transport toward Y ∈ H

of law ν is still given by Z. Thus starting from (X, Z ), we end up in (Y, Z ). At the level of P2(Rd), all this information is given by the coupling Γ as in the previous definition. 81 More generally, we have the following existence and uniqueness result. 

Proposition 6.3. Let μ, ν ∈ P p(O), ϕ ∈ Lp′ 

> μ

(O, Rd) and γ ∈ Π( μ, ν ). Then, there exists a unique parallel transport of ϕ along γ.Proof. By using Lemma 1.3 on μ(dx )δϕ(x)(dz ) and γ(dx, dy ) whose first marginal is μ, we obtain the existence. Uniqueness is in fact also a consequence of Lemma 1.3. Indeed, the coupling μ(dx )δϕ(x)(dz ) is deterministic. This notion of parallel transport will help us to estimate the continuity of DμU .

Remark 6.4. Definition 6.2 is concerned with the parallel transport of a deter-ministic coupling in Ppp ′ (O × Rd). We could have defined parallel transport for general couplings, but I do not present it because it is of no interest here.

Once again, at the level of the flat derivative, the notion of C1,α regularity is quite common and I do not comment more on it here. At the level of the horizontal derivative, the spaces C1,α (Pp(O)) have been the subject of very few works, even though the space C1,1(P2(Rd)) plays an important role in several results of Chapter 5 in the book of Carmona and Delarue [43]. It turns out that for certain mean field optimal control problems (see Section 11.1), when some singular terms are present, C1,1 regularity is helpful to prove several properties of the associated value function. We noticed this idea with Lions and Souganidis in [30] and proved with Lions that we can create C1,1 approximations of functions using sup-convolution (as in Proposition 6.11) in [28]. This idea is an extension of the famous Lasry-Lions regularization in Hilbert spaces [92]. The regularity spaces and the notion of parallel transport presented here are the subject of [21] which is concerned with more general statements and settings. The notion of parallel transport in Wasserstein spaces has been studied before, notably by Ambrosio and Gigli [9], who studied a more Eulerian notion. Gigli also introduced the quantity defined in (6.1) in [81, 82]. More generally, the notion of tangent space to the Wasserstein space is often considered by analogy with Riemannian geometry and can lead to several interesting developments. I refer to Gigli [81], Ambrosio, Gigli and Savaré [10], Aussedat [11] and my work [21] for more on this topic. 86 7 Optimality conditions and perturbed optimization 

In this section I treat two topics which are linked to optimization problems over spaces of measures. The first topic is optimality conditions, and the second one concerns the existence and uniqueness of optimizers and how it can be achieved by perturbing slightly the problem. 

# 7.1 Optimality conditions 

Optimality conditions shall take different forms, depending on the geometry with which we look at the problem. 

7.1.1 The vertical point of view 

Optimality conditions in terms of the vertical derivative are quite standard. I only present a simple result as an illustration. Let O be a measurable space, E be either M(O) or P(O), F : E 7 → R and consider 

inf  

> μ∈E

F (μ). (7.1) When E = M(O), μ0 is optimal in (7.1) and F is V-differentiable at μ0, we of course have ∇μF (μ0) = 0 . When dealing with E = P(O), because of the many corners and boundaries of P(O), such optimality conditions only yield inequalities in general. Recall also that in this case, I choose a normalization for ∇μF .

Proposition 7.1. Let μ0 be a solution to (7.1) with E = P(O) and assume that 

F is (vertically) Gateaux differentiable at μ0, then ∇μF (μ0) ≥ 0 and ∇μF (μ0) = 0 

μ0 almost everywhere. Proof. For any y ∈ O , by optimality of μ0, for any t ∈ (0 , 1) 

F ((1 − t)μ0 + tδ y) − F (μ0)

t ≥ 0.

Passing to the limit t → 0, we deduce that ∫ 

> O

∇μF (μ0)( x)( δy − μ0)( dx ) ≥ 0. Using 

⟨∇ μF (μ0), μ 0⟩ = 0 , it follows that ∇μF (μ0)( y) ≥ 0 for all y ∈ O , which proves the first part of the claim. Using once again the normalization ⟨∇ μF (μ0), μ 0⟩ = 0 , we finally obtain that ∇μF (μ0) = 0 μ0-almost everywhere. Second order conditions could also be considered but they are not in the scope of this book. 87 7.1.2 The horizontal point of view

As mentioned above, in this geometry associated to horizontal derivatives, the extreme points are the measures which put mass close to the boundary of O. This can be seen through the following results.

As we shall see when studying HJB equations in non finite dimensional spaces, results in perturbed optimization are quite handy. If we were working on a Banach space, the typical form of results we need is the following, often called Stegall’s Lemma. 

Lemma 7.4. Let E be a reflexive Banach space and f : E 7 → R ∪ { +∞} be a lsc function bounded from below which satisfies 

lim  

> |x|E→∞

f (x)

|x| = + ∞.

Then, for all ϵ > 0, there exists y ∈ E′, ∥y∥E′ ≤ ϵ such that 

• The lsc function x 7 → f (x) + ⟨y, x ⟩ has a minimum at some x∗ ∈ E.

• For any (xn)n≥0 such that lim n→∞ f (xn) + ⟨y, x n⟩ = f (x∗) + ⟨y, x ∗⟩, (xn)n≥0

converges toward x∗.

A minimum point such as the one in the previous Lemma is called strongly exposed. The previous result has two main aspects: first it creates points of minimum, second it creates them such that they are exposed. In compact cases, creating points of minima of lsc functions is not a problem, however, making them strict (which is equivalent to exposed in compact cases) is useful to obtain various stability properties of PDEs, as we shall see in Part II. In infinite dimension, of course even the existence of a point of minimum is not guaranteed. Consider for instance the case of a separable Hilbert space H with basis (en)n≥1, and the set S ⊂ H defined by S = {(1 + 1 

> n

)en| n ≥ 1} which is closed. The function distance to S is continuous on the closed unit ball, but never attains its minimum which is 0.I also insist upon the fact that the perturbation used in the previous lemma is smooth (linear here) which is important in several applications, notably when try-ing to derive the optimality conditions at the point of minimum of the perturbed function. In the remainder of this section, I will establish two variants of Stegall’s Lemma. The first one is aligned with the vertical derivative, and is concerned with a com-pact case where we want to extend the existence of points of minima to the exis-tence of a strict minimum for a perturbation. The second one will be in the spirit of horizontal derivatives and deals with non-compact cases, thus when existence of minima is not clear without perturbations. 89 7.2.1 Vertical variants in compact cases 

Let E be either P(O) or M1(O), for O the closure of a smooth bounded domain of 

Rd, and F : E 7 → R be lsc for the weak topology. Since E is compact for the weak topology, F admits a minimum simply by lower semi-continuity. The following variant of Stegall’s Lemma allows one to obtain strict / strongly exposed points of minimum. Recall that Hk(O) is the usual Sobolev space. 

Proposition 7.5. For any ϵ > 0, k > d 

> 2

, there exists ϕ ∈ Hk(O) with ∥ϕ∥Hk ≤ ϵ

such that μ 7 → F (μ) + ⟨ϕ, μ ⟩ admits a unique minimizer on E, which is thus strongly exposed for the weak topology. Proof. We use two important properties of Hk(O). First, from standard Sobolev embeddings, Hk(O) ⊂ C (O), hence, E ⊂ (Hk(O)) ′. Second, it is a Hilbert space. Define the correspondence A : Hk(O) ⇒ E by 

A(ϕ) := argmin μ∈E F (μ) + ⟨μ, ϕ ⟩.

For every ϕ ∈ Hk(O), A(ϕ)̸ = ∅ since μ 7 → F (μ) + ⟨ϕ, μ ⟩ is lower semi continuous and E is compact, both for the weak topology. Furthermore, −A is cyclical mono-tone, i.e. for every n ≥ 2, ϕ1, . . . , ϕ n ∈ Hk(O) and μ1 ∈ A(ϕ1), . . . , μ n ∈ A(ϕn),

> n∑
> i=1

⟨ϕi − ϕi−1, μ i⟩ ≤ 0,

where ϕ0 := ϕn. Indeed, noting μn+1 = μ1, we have 

> n∑
> i=1

⟨ϕi − ϕi−1, μ i⟩ =

> n∑
> i=1

⟨ϕi, μ i − μi+1 ⟩.

By optimality of μi, we know that ⟨ϕi, μ i − μi+1 ⟩ ≤ F (μi+1 ) − F (μi). Hence, the cyclical monotonicity of −A follows because ∑ni=1 F (μi+1 ) − F (μi) = 0 .As a consequence of cyclical monotonicity, −A : Hk(O) ⇒ (Hk(O)) ′ is included in the sub-differential of a convex function over Hk(O), see Theorem 2.5 in [36]. This convex function has full domain since A has full domain, and it is lsc as it can be defined as the supremum of affine functions. Since Hk(O) is separable, Mazur’s Theorem (Theorem 1.20 in [111]) states that it is Gateaux differentiable on a dense set, hence its sub-differential is reduced to a singleton on each of those points of Gateaux-differentiability (Proposition 1.8 in [111]). Hence, on those points, A is single valued, so there is a unique point of minimum of the associated function. Since we are working on a compact set, it follows that this unique point of minimum is also strongly exposed. Indeed, if a strict minimum μ∗ was not exposed, there would exist a minimizing sequence living outside a small ball centered around μ∗

(for any W1 for instance). By compactness and lsc, this would imply the existence of a point of minimum other than μ∗ which is not possible. 90 Remark 7.6. This proof makes apparent a link between properties of differen-tiability of convex functions on a dense set and the existence of strongly exposed points of minimum of perturbed functions, one at the level of the dual of the other. This is at the core of the theory of perturbed optimization. Spaces in which convex functions are differentiable at many points are called Asplund spaces, and spaces in which one can create exposed points of almost minimum are said to satisfy the Radon-Nikodym property, and there is an important duality between the two, see for instance the book of Phelps [111] or the one of Diestel and Uhl [66]. 

I shall often use the previous result with a k sufficiently large so that the perturbation ϕ is in fact a C2 function. 

7.2.2 A horizontal variant 

The previous variant only dealt with the uniqueness of points of minimum as their existence was clear. In non-compact cases, it is not so clear how to make the previ-ous proof work, as one cannot guarantee immediately that A is not empty valued on sufficiently many points. I do not address the difficult question of the direct extension of the previous result to Pp(Rd), as it would require many additional developments. I rather present another variant, which is more in line with the horizontal regularity of functions, lifting approach or coupling convexity. I restrict myself to the case O = Rd.

# Bibliographical comments 

The optimality conditions presented above are classical. In the vertical case, they can be found in my paper [19] for instance. Topics of perturbed optimization have arisen both in optimization problems and in questions of geometry of Banach spaces. Stegall’s Lemma (Lemma 7.4) was first proven by Stegall in [117] and a self contained presentation can be found in the book of Phelps [111], in particular, see Theorem 5.15 for a statement in the more general case of Banach spaces with the Radon-Nikodym property (RNP). See also Bourgain [35] and Diestel and Uhl [66] for more on the topic of the RNP. 93 I presented first Proposition 7.5 in [19] to study MFG master equations, with a proof very similar to Rockafellar’s on cyclically monotone operators. We estab-lished Proposition 7.7 with Lions in [29] to study HJB equations on non-compact spaces of measures. 94 Bridge to Parts II and III 

In this Part I, I presented a sort of dichotomy in the study of functions of a mea-sure argument, which was essentially based on the type of variation we want to consider for this measure argument. Considering arguments in P(O) and restricting ourselves to transport or more generally to variations associated with displacement on the underlying space O,the notion of horizontal derivative naturally arises, and with it notions of convex-ity and monotonicity for instance. In Part III, I will study problems of optimally controlling such variations, as is done in optimal transport for instance. Hence, for all of Part III, the notion of H-derivative will play a central role. More pre-cisely, it will be the associated notion of sub/super-differentiability which will be of importance, as the PDE of interest does not necessarily have H-differentiable solutions. I insist here upon the fact that it will be the underlying variations of interest which will "select" the H-derivative. When dealing with mean field games in Part II, the general structure of the underlying variations is much more general. In this setting, the measure argument 

μ describes the distribution of players in the state space O. Without entering into too much detail for the moment, it is quite reasonable that players anticipate more general variations than simply ones given by transport. For instance, the mass of players taking part in the game could naturally vary. This leads us to the setting of the vertical derivative, and associated notions of monotonicity and convexity. Hence, only the V-derivative will appear in Part II. Once again, solutions to the PDE of interest will not be smooth in general, and very few properties will be needed, mostly sub/super-differentials and optimality conditions. Furthermore, it is the vertical derivative associated to the W1 distance that will be of interest, as in Section 4.6. In particular, it will be compatible with the weak topology. 95 Part II. Mean Field Game master equations 

# 8 Introduction to master equations 8.1 A (very) short background on mean field games 

Mean field game (MFG) theory was originally motivated by the mathematical study of dynamic nonatomic games. Its development is mainly based on the semi-nal work of Lasry and Lions [93, 94, 95, 97]. Of course, earlier studies of such games precede this work as for example, the economic models by Krusell and Smith [91], Scheinkman and Weiss [115], or the work in engineering by Huang, Caines, and Malhamé [83, 84, 85]. Using a terminology from economics or physics, the main objective of MFG is to characterize macroscopic quantities by aggregating some microscopic effects. In most interesting (mathematically at least) situations, the equation characterizing the macro quantity is forward, while the one characterizing microscopic effects is backward, with respect to time. The forward evolution comes from the usual com-putation of the evolution of certain quantities, when the backward structure comes from the usual accounting for anticipation: decision making through dynamic pro-gramming or computation of the value of an asset as the sum of its future expected gains for instance. I believe it is fundamental to insist upon the fact that, despite its name, MFG theory is not only concerned with games, but with more general micro-macro con-figurations or forward-backward structures. This important remark opens many models, which are not games, to the powerful mathematical developments made in the theory of MFG, see for instance [1, 5, 22]. It will also allow me to consider freely classes of equations, without the need to justify the existence of a proper game from which they originate. In any case, the aggregate of the microscopic scale usually takes the form of a non-negative measure m over some set O which describes the possible states of the microscopic quantities we wish to aggregate. Hence, MFG naturally leads to some analysis questions on spaces of measures. In some cases, it will only take the form of an evolution equation for a measure argument, and in other ones, it leads to the study of PDEs on spaces of measures. Due to its strong modeling power, this theory is currently the subject of exten-sive research by many mathematicians and is being applied in many fields such as engineering, physics, finance, economics, and more generally in social sciences. The most challenging mathematical object in MFG theory is arguably the 96 master equation (ME), which is usually a nonlinear PDE posed on a space of measures 1. When there is uniqueness of the underlying micro-macro equilibria, the solution of the ME allows for an almost complete description of the model. The study of this equation is the topic of this part. For a general presentation of MFG theory and the role of the master equation, I refer to the book by Carmona and Delarue [43, 44], and to the book of Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry and Lions [39] for early developments on this equation. 

# 8.2 A formal derivation of a master equation 

In this section, I give a formal and quite abstract derivation of the ME. As men-tioned above, MFGs usually involve the coupling of two scales: a microscopic one and a macroscopic one. In this abstract setting, it is not possible to provide a meaning to these scales. Hence, for the moment, I shall simply consider two features of the model, one is computed backwardly in time and the other one is computed forwardly in time. Let me call v the backward quantity and y the for-ward one. Here, I assume that v and y are valued into respectively F and E, two topological vector spaces. In most cases, the coupling between those two quantities appears as follows. Given T > 0 and a trajectory (yt)0≤t≤T , one can compute an associated trajectory 

(vt)0≤t≤T through the backward equation 

ddt vt + B[yt, v t] = 0 ,vT = G[yT ],

(8.1) where B : E × F 7 → F and G : E 7 → F are given operators. The notation [·] is simply here to insist upon the fact that the dependence can be non-local, notably in the important cases in which E and F are functional spaces. Similarly, given a time T > 0 and a trajectory (vt)0≤t≤T , one can compute the forward evolution of the quantity labeled y through 

ddt yt + A[yt, v t] = 0 ,y0 given ,

(8.2) where A : E × F 7 → E is another given operator. Numerous questions can be asked on the connections between the two previous equations. The most natural ones are questions of existence and uniqueness of a  

> 1Note that spaces of measures can be finite dimensional when the base space is finite.

97 solution (vs, y s)t≤s≤T for given t ≤ T and yt to the system (8.1)-(8.2) (on the time interval [t, T ]). If an underlying game is present, such a solution corresponds to a Nash equilibrium of the game. Many developments around existence, uniqueness or stability of such systems are possible. I pass over them here, and just insist upon the fact that the main mathematical difficulty lies in the forward-backward nature of the coupling. Another point of view consists in trying to condense all the information of the system (8.1)-(8.2) into a PDE whose unique solution U is the function which maps (t, y t) ∈ (−∞ , T ] × E to vt ∈ F , where (vs, y s)t≤s≤T is the unique solution to (8.1)-(8.2) on [t, T ]. This (ambitious) program requires first the uniqueness of the solutions of (8.1)-(8.2), and I shall come back to this important requirement later on. Assuming that this uniqueness result holds here, we find that, given a solution (vs, y s)t≤s≤T to (8.1)-(8.2) and such a map U , the following holds true 

∀s ∈ [t, T ], U (s, y s) = vs. (8.3) Indeed, because of the uniqueness result, given a solution (vs, y s)t≤s≤T to (8.1)-(8.2), for any s ∈ [t, T ], (vs′ , y s′ )s≤s′≤T is the unique solution to (8.1)-(8.2) on [s, T ]

starting from ys at time s. Taking the time derivative of (8.3) we obtain 

∀s ∈ (t, T ), ∂sU (s, y s) − ⟨ A[ys, U (s, y s)] , D y⟩U (s, y s) = −B[ys, U (s, y s)] ,

where ⟨ξ, D y⟩U (s, y ) just stands in a quite formal way, for the fact that we are taking the derivative of U , with respect to the variable y, in the direction given by ξ. Thus, using the definition of U , we finally arrive at the master equation 

−∂tU (t, y ) + ⟨A[y, U ], D y⟩U = B[y, U ] in (−∞ , T ) × E. (8.4) Equation (8.4) has the typical form of a MFG master equation. In this general framework, the master equation is a non-linear transport equation. The forward-backward system (8.1)-(8.2) represents the characteristics of the ME (8.4). In this setting, solving the system or the ME is equivalent. I just explained how to solve the ME given solutions to the systems for any initial conditions (in a uniqueness regime). Conversely, given a solution U to (8.4), it is a simple exercise to verify that the unique solution (vt, y t)0≤t≤T to (8.1)-(8.2) on [0 , T ] starting from y0 at time 0 satisfies ddt yt = −A[yt, U (t, y t)] .

Equation (8.4) is naturally associated to the terminal condition 

U (T, y ) = G(y) in E. 

Of course, it is quite often natural that the equation is only posed on a subset of 

E, but I do not enter into this question in this introductory part. 98 8.3 The various master equations 

In several situations, the exact form of (8.4) is perturbed, for instance by terms modelling stochasticity. Below are some comments on the variations the previous structure can possess. 

8.3.1 Different spaces E and F

When E, F are finite dimensional, equation (8.4) is self explanatory. The notation 

⟨· , ·⟩ then stands for the Euclidean scalar product and Dy for the gradient operator in the direction y. Those types of MEs are called finite dimensional. Exactly the same interpretation holds when E = F is a separable Hilbert space. In this case, we talk about Hilbertian MEs. In some cases, as we shall see in Section 8.5, F will be some functional sub-space of C(O) and E a subset of M+(O). In this case, terms of the form ⟨v, D y⟩U (s, y )

have to be understood as a derivative with respect to a measure argument, and I refer to Part I for more details on this. I shall call such equations MEs on sets of measures. 

8.3.2 Terms modelling noises 

In certain important cases, the forward equation (8.2) is in fact of a stochastic na-ture. In those situations, the backward equation (8.1) is quite ill-adapted. Indeed, to solve it, one has to be given a path (yt)t≥0. But if this path is stochastic, typi-cally adapted to a natural filtration (Ft)t≥0, then simply plugging it in (8.1) leads to a solution to this equation which is not at all adapted to (Ft)t≥0. Hence, this backward equation should be replaced by some backward stochastic equation, in the sense of backward SDEs introduced by Pardoux and Peng [109]. The study of the system (8.1)-(8.2) then becomes much more involved whereas the study of the associated master equation usually does not increase too much in difficulty. I said usually since, depending on the nature of the stochasticity, the terms arising from it can be quite different as we shall see. In these stochastic cases, the derivation of the master equation stays the same, it is still defined through the now stochastic forward-backward system. The only change is the need to use an appropriate vari-ant of Ito’s Lemma instead of the chain rule when differentiating the relation (8.3). 

Terms modelling jumps 

Random jumps in the dynamics of the forward variable y can be modelled by 99 considering the following extension of (8.4) 

−∂tU + ⟨A[y, U (t, y )] , D y⟩U + λ(U − T ∗U (t, T y)) = B[y, U ] in (−∞ , T ) × E, 

where λ ≥ 0, T : E 7 → E is a linear operator, and T ∗ : F 7 → F is a form of an adjoint of T . Such terms appear if, at random times given by a Poisson process of intensity λ, the variable y is transformed through the map T , i.e. the underlying variable jumps from y to T y. I do not recall the derivation of such terms and refer to the second section of our paper with Lasry and Lions [24]. Such terms are not so difficult to treat and provide in general good examples to test whether techniques on MEs without noise terms have a chance to be gener-alized to more complex ones. Typical examples of such operators T are translation. 

Additional stochastic parameter 

In other cases, randomness can be carried out through an additional variable 

θ ∈ Rk for instance. In such models, the operators A and B depend on the value 

θ of the parameters as well, and this value satisfies an SDE of the form 

dθ t = b(θt)dt + σ(θt)dW t,

where (Wt)t≥0 is a standard k dimensional Brownian motion on Rk, and b : Rk 7 →

Rk, σ : Rk 7 → M k(R) are given Lipschitz functions. In such cases, the solution to the ME has to depend on the value of the parameters as well. We are then led to consider MEs of the form 

−∂tU (t, y, θ ) + ⟨A[y, U, θ ], D y⟩U −b(θ)∇θU − 12T r (σσ T (θ)D2 

> θ

U )= B[y, U, θ ] in (−∞ , T ) × E × Rk.

Typical examples of such situations include cases in which θ represents an en-vironmental factor, such as the climate, or a price which affects all players. 

The variable y is perturbed by a diffusion 

Finally, cases have been considered in which the forward variable y is directly affected by a diffusion, that is cases in which (8.2) is replaced by an SDE or stochastic PDE for instance. These kinds of models need to be treated with a lot of care. Indeed, if (8.2) is stochastic, the variants of Ito’s Lemma needed to differentiate (8.3) might be non-trivial and involve second order derivatives on E.General forms of such equations are then simply MEs of the form of (8.4) with second order terms in y.100 I insist upon the fact that such models, despite the mathematical attractiveness that follows from the analysis of these difficult second order terms, can often be avoided and be recasted simply through an additional stochastic parameter. 

# 8.4 Creation of singularities and how to prevent them 

As mentioned in Section 8.2, the derivation of the ME only makes sense in a uniqueness regime of the forward-backward system (8.1)-(8.2). Here, I explain on a simple example what happens when we are not in such a regime, and give a formal definition of the monotone regime, that will be detailed in several examples later on. 

8.4.1 Singularities on the simplest master equation 

The simplest non-linear ME one can think of might be the Burgers’ equation in one dimension 

∂tu + u ∂ xu = 0 , in (0 , ∞) × R, (8.5) with initial condition u0 : R 7 → R given. (The time has simply been reversed to study a forward equation instead of a backward one.) The solution is here a function u : [0 , ∞) × R 7 → R. One can check that there is an explicit formula for the solution to (8.5) which is given by 

∀t ≥ 0, x ∈ R, u(t, x ) = u0(( Id + tu 0)−1(x)) , (8.6) where (Id + tu 0)−1 refers to the inverse function of Id + tu 0, and where Id : x 7 → x

is the identity. The previous formula turns out to be extremely insightful on the type of sin-gularity that can happen for the ME. Indeed, this formula only makes sense when 

Id + tu 0 is invertible. If u0 is C-Lipschitz, we know that such a condition will be true for all time t < C −1. It is also a simple exercise to verify that the solution given by formula (8.6) is the unique reasonable solution to (8.5). Starting from a C-Lipschitz initial condition u0, if we reach a time t∗ such that 

Id + t∗u0 is no longer invertible, it happens that u(t, ·) will develop a singularity as t → t∗ and that no continuous solution exists after t∗. In other words, a discontinuity appears, even if we started from a Lipschitz initial condition, and it is quite difficult to make sense of the equation after that time. 

Remark 8.1. In this one dimensional setting, one can select a "good" discon-tinuous solution after t∗, namely by using the notion of entropic solution to the 

101 associated scalar conservation law. However, if we consider the multi-dimensional equation given by 

∂tu + ( u · ∇ x)u = 0 , in (0 , ∞) × Rd,

where now u0 : Rd 7 → Rd and u : [0 , ∞) × Rd 7 → Rd, the situation is much more complex. The formula (8.6) is still valid and the study of the solution before the appearance of discontinuities is possible. But what happens after that is still only poorly understood... 

8.4.2 Monotone regimes to prevent the creation of singularities 

The formula (8.6) makes quite apparent the requirements needed on u0 to allow its use for all time. Indeed, if u0 is non-decreasing, then Id + tu 0 is invertible for all time t ≥ 0, and the equation is somehow well posed, even if u0 is not (Lipschitz) continuous for instance. This fact generalizes easily to the multi-dimensional set-ting of Remark 8.1 if we replace the non-decreasing property with the notion of monotonicity. In addition to preventing the creation of singularities, this non-decreasing as-sumption can be interpreted in terms of the associated forward-backward system, precisely as a uniqueness regime. Indeed, the forward-backward system associated to (8.5) is given by { ˙vt = 0 , v0 = u0(y0),

˙yt = vt, yT given .

Taking (v1, y 1) and (v2, y 2) two solutions of the system, we see that 

−(u0(y10 ) − u0(y20 ))( y10 − y20 ) =

ddt

Hence, because u0 is non-decreasing, we obtain that v1 

> t

= v2 

> t

for almost all t, hence the fact that (v1, y 1) = ( v2, y 2) since both are constant. A simple generalization of the previous argument leads to the following formal definition of a monotone regime for (8.4). 

Definition 8.2. The monotone regime for (8.4) is the conjunction of the following properties: 

• The space F ⊂ E′.

• The map G : E 7 → E′ is monotone. 

• The map (A, B ) : E×E′ 7 → E×E′ is monotone, that is for all (x1, y 1), (x2, y 2) ∈

E × E′

⟨A[x1, y 1] − A[x2, y 2], y 1 − y2⟩E×E′ + ⟨B[x1, y 1] − B[x2, y 2], x 1 − x2⟩E′×E ≥ 0.

102 Of course, several extensions can be considered, for instance by considering maps A, B, G which are not defined everywhere. To obtain uniqueness of the associated forward-backward system, some strict monotonicity is needed. Fur-thermore, it is an open question to understand to what extent such monotone regimes are limiting assumptions, or in other words if there exist other general classes of assumptions which yield uniqueness of the forward-backward system. Note that on the simple Burgers equation mentioned above, the formula of the explicit solution is defined for all times if and only if we are in the monotone regime, as asking that Id + tu 0 is invertible for all t ≥ 0 is equivalent to demanding that u0

is non-decreasing. Hence, on this simple equation, it is not possible to avoid this monotone regime to consider regular solutions on arbitrary long time intervals. 

# 8.5 The most studied example 

Here I present the original example of a MFG considered by Lasry and Lions [95]. As I shall show, considering slight variations on this model, we can arrive at the various MEs described in section 8.3. 

8.5.1 Definition of the mean field game 

The following description of the game is only formal. We consider a continuum of players of mass 1. Each player is described by a parameter x ∈ Rd. We call Rd

the state space, and x the state of the player. The players are initially distributed according to μ ∈ P (Rd). All players control the evolution of their state. The evolution of the state of a player is given by the process (Xt)t≥0, solution to the SDE 

dX t = αtdt + √2σdW t, (8.7) where σ ≥ 0 is a parameter, (Wt)t≥0 is a standard Brownian motion on a standard probability space (Ω , A, (Ft)t ≥0, P) and (αt)t≥0 is the control of the player consid-ered. The control has to be an adapted Rd valued process, in particular, a player cannot anticipate on the future of the Brownian motion. Each player chooses its control (αt)t≥0 on its own and two distinct players are facing independent realiza-tions of the Brownian motion. A player whose state evolves through (Xt)t≥0 and whose control is (αt)t≥0 faces the cost 

E 

> [∫T
> 0

L(Xt, α t, m t)dt + G(XT , m T )

> ]

, (8.8) where L : Rd ×Rd ×P (Rd) 7 → R+ and G : Rd ×P (Rd) 7 → R+ are given cost functions, 

T > 0 is the duration of the game and (mt)t≥0 is the P(Rd) valued process which 103 describes the evolution of the distribution of players. That is, for a measurable 

A ⊂ Rd and t ∈ [0 , T ], mt(A) is the proportion of players which are in A at time 

t. The presence of the expectation in (8.8) stands for the fact that the players are risk neutral with respect to the randomness present in the evolution of their state. 

8.5.2 Equilibria of the MFG 

I insist upon the fact that (8.8) does not simply represent a cost that a given player has to minimize in (αt)t ≥0. It is only the case if (mt)t∈[0 ,T ] is known, which is clearly not the case in general. If it is the case, then the players can find their optimal response to (mt)t≥0 by means of dynamic programming. Namely they can introduce the value function u : [0 , T ] × Rd 7 → R defined by 

u(t, x ) := inf  

> (αs)s∈[t,T ]

L(Xs, α s, m s)ds + G(XT , m T )

> ∣∣∣∣∣

> ]

,

where the infimum is taken over admissible controls and (8.7) holds in (t, T ). They then solve the associated HJB equation which is given by 

−∂tu − σ∆u + H(x, ∇xu, m t) = 0 in (0 , T ) × Rd,u(T, x ) = G(x, m T ) in Rd, (8.9) where H, the Hamiltonian of the problem, is given by H(x, p, m ) = sup α∈Rd {− L(x, α, m )−

α · p}. Given a sufficiently smooth solution u to (8.9) (which thus requires a path 

(mt)t≥0 to be given as well), a player can choose the control 

α∗
