:= −DpH(Xt, ∇xu(t, X t), m t)

to minimize (8.8). As just mentioned, players have to decide which path (mt)t≥0 they are going to anticipate. Particularly interesting anticipations are called equilibria 2, which are defined in the following way: a path (mt)t≥0 is an equilibrium of the MFG if, given that all players anticipate (mt)t≥0, there is a way for the players to play optimally according to this anticipation, which is such that the evolution of their distribution in the state space is actually going to be (mt)t∈[0 ,T ].I leave as an exercise to the interested reader to verify, using the interpretation of Fokker-Planck equation of Remark 2.1, that we can read in the coupling of the  

> 2There is a slight abuse of terminology here, as equilibria usually refer to the actions of the players, and not to their distribution.

104 following system the notion of equilibrium. 

> 

−∂tu − σ∆u + H(x, ∇xu, m t) = 0 in (0 , T ) × Rd,∂tm − σ∆m − div (DpH(x, ∇xu, m )m) = 0 in (0 , T ) × Rd,m|t=0 = μ, u(T, x ) = G(x, m T ) in Rd.

(8.10) All equilibria (mt)t≥0 are associated to a solution to (8.10) and vice-versa. I do not give a precise statement about the associated monotone regime for the moment, and refer to Hypothesis 9.10 below for the case in which H(x, p, m ) = ˜H(x, p ) − f (m)( x).

8.5.3 The associated master equation 

Before deriving the associated master equation, I need to make precise what type of derivative on the space of measures I am going to use. As mentioned at the end of Part I, it is natural in this MFG context that the underlying measure evolves more generally than simply by following flows of ODEs or SDEs. For instance, players could jump in the state space, or they could leave or enter the game. This strongly suggests to work with the vertical derivative. This might seem strange, as for the present game, such behaviour is not authorized, and it will remain the case in the rest of this part, because I choose to concentrate the study on a simple master equation. However, I strongly encourage the reader to extend the following study to a case in which the Fokker-Planck equation in (8.10) contains a term in λm for 

λ a constant, to realize that in such a case, the use of the V-derivative is necessary. Following the derivation of the ME given in Section 8.2, the ME associated to the forward-backward system (8.10) is 

−∂tU − ⟨∇ μU (t, μ, x, ·), div (DpH(·, ∇xU (t, μ, ·), μ )μ) + σ∆μ⟩− σ∆xU + H(x, ∇xU, μ ) = 0 in (0 , T ) × P (Rd) × Rd. (8.11) It is naturally associated to the terminal condition 

U (T, μ, x ) = G(μ)( x) in P(Rd) × Rd.

In (8.11), the notation ⟨· , ·⟩ stands for the extension of the L2(Rd) scalar product. Sticking with the terminology of Section 8.2, the forward variable is here μ or m

and U (t, μ ) is a function on the state space. However, sometimes we use the natu-ral identification between [0 , T ] × P (Rd) 7 → (Rd 7 → R) and [0 , T ] × P (Rd) × Rd 7 → R,such as is done for G just above. 105 The equation (8.11) is the master equation associated to the MFG I just pre-sented. Its solution U should be referred to as the value of the MFG. Indeed, when it is well defined, U (t, μ, x ) is the (unique) cost a player in state x ∈ Rd, at time 

t ∈ [0 , T ], facing the distribution of other players μ ∈ P (Rd) is going to pay, under the fundamental condition that players are going to follow the (unique) equilib-rium. This is yet another opportunity to insist upon the fact that the solution to the ME is well defined only under a uniqueness assumption. Note that by considering a finite discretization of Rd, we can construct a finite dimensional ME which is an approximation of (8.11). Indeed, it suffices to con-sider standard finite difference schemes as approximation to the forward-backward system (8.10), and then derive the associated ME. I do not give all the details of such an approximation as it is: i) standard in view of the literature on finite difference schemes, ii) quite heavy in terms of notation that we shall not need in the following. 

# 8.6 The Hilbertian framework 

There is another ME that we can derive directly from this MFG in the case σ = 0 ,which is called the Hilbertian ME. It consists in a much more Lagrangian approach to the description of the distribution of players. In terms of modeling, this approach consists in replacing the probability measure μ ∈ P (Rd) by a random variable 

X : Ω 7 → Rd, where (Ω , A, P) is a standard probability space, which is the space of labels. One can for instance think about Ω as the space of names of players. Hence, a distribution of players is here simply X : Ω 7 → Rd which associates to a player (through its label) its position. In this framework, a strategy profile (i.e. a strategy for all players) can be summarized by a map α : [0 , T ] × Ω 7 → Rd and it yields the following evolution from an initial distribution of players X0 : Ω 7 → Rd

dX t = αtdt. 

Hence the Hilbertian analogue of (8.10) is given by 

> 

−∂tu(t, x ) + H(x, ∇xu, L(Xt)) = 0 in (0 , T ) × Rd,dX t = −DpH(Xt, ∇xu(t, X t), L(Xt)) dt in (0 , T ) × Ω,X0 given , u(T, x ) = G(x, L(XT )) in Rd.

Instead of directly considering the associated master equation, we differentiate with respect to x the HJB equation above and set v = ∇xu to arrive at the 106 forward-backward system 

> 

−∂tv(t, x ) + DxH(x, v, L(Xt)) + DpH(x, v, L(Xt)) Dxv = 0 in (0 , T ) × Rd,dX t = −DpH(Xt, v (t, X t), L(Xt)) dt in (0 , T ) × Ω,X0 given , v(T, x ) = DxG(x, L(XT )) in Rd.

Finally, if we considered directly the ME associated to the previous forward-backward system, we would end up with a solution as a function V : [0 , T ] × (Ω 7 →

Rd) 7 → (Rd 7 → Rd). Instead of such a function, we shall consider, to simplify nota-tion, a function V : [0 , T ] × (Ω 7 → Rd) 7 → (Ω 7 → Rd) defined through the following relation 

V (t, X )( ω) := V(t, X )( X(ω)) . (8.12) Finally, restricting ourselves to the subset L2(Ω , Rd) ⊂ (Ω 7 → Rd), we are able to write the ME 

−∂tV +DxH(X, V, L(X))+ ⟨DpH(X, V, L(X)) , ∇X ⟩V (t, X ) = 0 in (0 , T )×L2(Ω , Rd),

(8.13) where ⟨· , ·⟩ here denotes the canonical scalar product in H := L2(Ω , Rd) and ∇X

the gradient operator in H.Several comments are here in order: 

• The derivation of (8.13) might seem mysterious if one does not remark that differentiating (8.12), say formally in H, leads to 

∇X V (t, X )( ω) = ∇xV(t, X )( X(ω)) + ∇X V(t, X )( X(ω))( ω).

• The restriction to L2(Ω , Rd) is purely arbitrary at this formal level, and only has the advantage of using the Hilbertian structure. In practice, this kind of restriction has to be justified by the fact that at the initial time, 

E[|X0|2] < ∞, as well as growth conditions on H to ensure that the dynamics will stay inside L2(Ω , Rd). Similar equations in Lp(Ω , Rd) space for p̸ = 2 

could also have been considered. 

• However probabilistic this framework may seem, note that the underlying model is purely deterministic, and the language of random variables is used to arrive at a compact formulation. One of the main interests of this Hilbertian reformulation is that it makes apparent another monotone structure in the MFG, different from the one described in Hypothesis 9.10 below. Indeed, in this case, the natural monotone structure is given by the following. 107 Definition 8.3. The monotone regime associated to the Hilbertian setting consists of the following two assumptions: 

• The map X 7 → DxG(L(X))( X) is monotone as a map from H to H.

• The map (X, Y ) 7 → (−DxH(X, Y, L(X)) , D pH(X, Y, L(X))) is monotone as a map from H × H to H × H.

Remark 8.4. The fact that this Hilbertian framework not only works but can produce similar results of existence and uniqueness as the previous setting, is an excellent justification of the fact that, contrary to what can be read in several papers or books, indistinguishability is absolutely not a feature of MFG. Indeed, in the Hilbertian case, players can be distinguished by their label and the study can be carried out in a similar fashion. 

Remark 8.5. The Hilbertian framework is well suited for an analysis of the as-sociated master equation through the use of the H-derivative, but I do not expand more on it in this simple presentation. 

# 8.7 A master equation without a game 

In contrast with the previous example which can be looked at as the most typical MFG, I now insist on a model which yields a ME, without any proper underly-ing game, but simply because of the existence of an underlying forward-backward structure. Consider a market in which a good (or product) is exchanged. We assume that there is a constant stream of demand D for the product, when expressed in money. The product is supplied by small producers. In order to produce the product, and then to sell it on the market, they need to buy a unit of production capital, which will produce a constant unit stream of product. The products are then sold on the market at a certain price. For the sake of simplicity, we assume that the market will only be open during the time interval [0 , T ]. At time t ∈ [0 , T ], we denote by 

Kt > 0 the number of active units of production, by Vt the value that a unit of production will generate in [t, T ], and by Pt the price at which the product is sold. I assume perfect competition between the producers. Because the amount of cash available to buy the product is constant, the price is given by Pt = D/K t.Given a trajectory of number of production units (Kt)t∈[0 ,T ], the value Vt is com-puted backwardly by summing over the future revenue it will generate as follows 

Vt = 

> ∫Tt

DKs

ds. 

108 On the other hand, I make the assumption that units of production are bought and sold depending on the revenue they generate, so that, at time t, if machines (units of production) are generating a value Vt, then we have 

ddt Kt = λ(Vt − c),

for λ, c > 0 two constants. Hence, we have a forward backward structure, simply by differentiating the integral definition of Vt, which can be summarized in  

> 
> ddt

Vt = − DKt , 

> ddt

Kt = λ(Vt − c),K0 given , VT = 0 .

There is a ME associated to this forward-backward system, which is satisfied by the value U (t, K ) a unit of production will generate, given that the current number of active unit of production is K and that we are at time t. It is the following 

−∂tU − λ(U − c)∂K U = DK in (0 , T ) × (0 , ∞),U (T, K ) = 0 in (0 , ∞).

It is worth noting that this ME is quite easy to understand, as it does not involve analysis in infinite dimension. Finally, no proper game has been defined between the producers in the market, only rules, but this ME still makes sense. 

Remark 8.6. In this case, we are in a monotone regime, in fact an anti-monotone one (that is −U solves a ME in monotone regime). Indeed, the terminal value is constant thus monotone, and λ > 0, D > 0 yield the correct monotonicity proper-ties. 

# 8.8 Bibliographical comments 

For a nice introduction to MFG, I refer the reader to Cardaliaguet and Porretta [40]. In addition to the references to which I pointed at the beginning of this section, I also refer the reader interested in MFG systems to Achdou and Capuzzo-Dolcetta [2] for numerical methods and toward Porretta [113] for weak notions of solutions to MFG systems. I will not comment further on the general literature on MFG but rather focus on the one associated to the ME. Other economic models were derived in Gabaix et al [77] and in Achdou et al [3] using MFGs. The ME was derived by Lasry and Lions while discussing MFG in Chicago with Lucas, who informed them of the Krusell-Smith model [91] which has a stochastic component 109 in the variation of macroscopic quantities (the so-called common noise). It then became apparent that MEs are essential tools in macro-economics and links were made with previous models which were in fact finite dimensional MEs such as Scheinkman and Weiss [115] or Conze, Lasry and Scheinkman [52]. Recently, numerical computations to provide a solution to the Krusell-Smith model were provided by Achdou, Lasry and Lions [4]. The first mathematical developments on MEs are due to PL Lions, in finite dimensional cases, and were presented in his lectures at Collège de France [100]. In 2015, he presented an unpublished work on the Hilbertian case, extending his analysis in finite dimension. In the meantime, an extensive study of the ME through the formula (8.3) was done by Chassagneux-Crisan-Delarue [50], which was later extended in a sort of complete study in the book of Cardaliaguet-Delarue-Lasry-Lions [39], which was also continued by Jakobsen and Rutkowski [87] in a slightly different setting. Accounts of these studies are also presented in the book of Carmona-Delarue [44]. More recent and efficient studies have been carried out since, and they will be presented or mentioned below. The monotone regime was identified in the first paper on MFG by Lasry and Lions [95] as the natural condition to ensure uniqueness of solutions to the MFG system, and thus of the underlying Nash equilibria. They also found variants of these assumptions, for instance in finite dimensional frameworks, in cases in which the Hamiltonian H depends on both ∇xu and m, as well as in the Hilber-tian setting. This latter setting was also used in Gangbo and Meszaros [78] and Gangbo, Meszaros, Mou and Zhang [79], independently, under the name displace-ment monotonicity. The last model of a master equation without a game originates from the mod-elling done in either Achdou et al [1] or in Bertucci, Bertucci, Lasry and Lions [22]. 110 9 Uniqueness and stability of the solution to the master equation 

As mentioned in Section 8.4, MEs will develop singularities in general and their study in the presence of singularities is not very realistic at the moment. In this section, we shall see that, under monotonicity assumptions, we can define a notion of solution to the ME which enjoys stability properties, as well as uniqueness. In the next part, when dealing with questions of existence, we shall see that mono-tonicity assumptions are also important to prevent the creation of singularities. The rest of this section is divided into two parts. First, I shall be concerned with finite dimensional MEs, then with MEs on spaces of probability measures. 

# 9.1 A first result of well-posedness 

Consider a bounded domain Ω of Rd whose boundary is C2, functions U0 : ¯Ω 7 →

Rd, F, G : ¯Ω × Rd 7 → Rd defined on the closure ¯Ω of Ω and the following ME 

∂tU (t, q ) + ⟨F (q, U (t, q )) , ∇q⟩U (t, q ) = G(q, U (t, q )) in (0 , ∞) × ¯Ω,U |t=0 = U0. (9.1) The unknown U in the previous equation is valued in Rd. In (9.1) and in all that follows which is concerned with this finite dimensional ME, ⟨· , ·⟩ denotes the Euclidean scalar product. Note also that the previous equation has to hold on the boundary of ¯Ω. I apologize for the use of Ω which has nothing to do with a probability space in this context of finite dimensional MEs. Assumptions on F, G and U0 shall be made later on. As I do not want to enter a rather complex discussion about what happens at ∂Ω, I shall assume that the following condition holds. 

Hypothesis 9.1. For all q ∈ ∂Ω, V ∈ Rd,

⟨F (q, V ), η (q)⟩ ≥ 0,

where η(q) is the unit outward normal vector to ∂Ω at the point q.

This assumption is purely technical and should not worry the reader too much. In the usual infinite dimensional master equation, the analogous Hypothesis 9.1 is verified. It simply states that any potential underlying trajectory of the forward variable stays inside ¯Ω. Hypothesis 9.1 shall always be valid and I will not recall it in every result. In this finite dimensional setting, the monotone regime of interest is summa-rized in the following assumption. 111 Hypothesis 9.2. The mapping (G, F ) : ¯Ω × Rd 7 → R2d is monotone, and so is 

U0 : ¯Ω 7 → Rd.

The next result is probably the most important one on the monotone regime. Its proof highlights how monotonicity implies uniqueness of solutions to (9.1). 

Proposition 9.3. Assume that Hypothesis 9.2 holds. Then, there exists at most one smooth solution U : [0 , ∞) × ¯Ω 7 → Rd of (9.1) . Furthermore, such a solution is a monotone map in q, for all t ≥ 0.Proof. Consider U and V two such solutions and define W : R+ × ¯Ω2 7 → R by 

W (t, q, p ) = ⟨U (t, q ) − V (t, p ), q − p⟩.

Step 1: W is non-negative. Note that for any t ≥ 0, q, p ∈ Ω, we have the relations 

∇qW (t, q, p ) = DqU (t, q ) · (q − p) + U (t, q ) − V (t, p ),

∇pW (t, q, p ) = DpV (t, p ) · (p − q) + V (t, p ) − U (t, q ).

Using the fact that both U and V solve (9.1), we thus obtain that 

∂tW + F (q, U ) · ∇ qW + F (p, V ) · ∇ pW == ⟨G(q, U ) − G(p, V ), q − p⟩ + ⟨F (q, U ) − F (p, V ), U − V ⟩ in (0 , ∞) × Ω2.

Observe that, since U0 is monotone, W |t=0 ≥ 0. From the previous PDE, by using the fact that (G, F ) is monotone, it follows 

∂tW + F (q, U ) · ∇ qW + F (p, V ) · ∇ pW ≥ 0.

We want to show that W ≥ 0. Assume that it is not the case. Thus, there exists 

λ > 0 such that it is also not the case for W + λt . Consider T > 0 and a point of minimum (t∗, q ∗, p ∗) of W + λt on [0 , T ] × ¯Ω2. Clearly t∗ > 0. Note that if t∗ < T ,then ∂tW (t∗, q ∗, p ∗) = −λ and if t∗ = T then ∂tW (t∗, q ∗, p ∗) ≤ − λ. If q∗, p ∗ ∈ Ω,then we obtain that ∇qW (t∗, q ∗, p ∗) = ∇pW (t∗, q ∗, p ∗) = 0 and thus that 

−λ ≥ 0,

which is a contradiction. Hence, it remains to treat the case where q∗ or p∗ ∈ ∂Ω. It suffices to remark that in this case, Hypothesis 9.1 implies that ⟨F (q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , ∇qW (t∗, q ∗, p ∗)⟩ ≤ 

0 if q∗ ∈ ∂Ω. Hence, we still obtain that −λ ≥ 0 which is a contradiction. Thus 

W ≥ 0.112 Step 2: U = V . Consider a point (t, q ) ∈ [0 , ∞) × Ω. For ϵ > 0 sufficiently small, the ball of center q and of radius ϵ lies in Ω. Thus, for any v ∈ Rd, ∥v∥ ≤ 1,

⟨U (t, q + ϵv ) − V (t, q ), ϵv ⟩ ≥ 0.

This implies that U = V on R+ × Ω, since they are continuous, the equality also holds on ¯Ω. The monotonicity of U then follows by non-negativity of W since 

V = U .As we shall see in Section 10, the monotone regime is in fact not essential in the previous result, as uniqueness can be recovered directly from the regularity. Nonetheless, the proof of this result will allow us to understand a key feature of the stability of the solutions of MEs in monotone regimes. 

# 9.2 Monotone solutions to finite dimensional master equations 

In the previous proof, the uniqueness of solutions to (9.1) was obtained by proving the positivity of W through a comparison principle-type argument. The main idea I want to follow here is to define a concept of solution with the minimum regularity requirements, so that the previous proof can still be carried out. The use of this argument formally required three steps: 

• Using the relation that for all i ∈ { 1, . . . , d },

∂qi W (t, q, p ) = ⟨Dqi U (t, q ), q − p⟩ + U i(t, q ) − V i(t, p ), (9.2) as well as the equation satisfied by U (and its counterpart for V ) to derive an equation for W .

• Considering a point of minimum of W .

• Using (9.2) at this point of minimum to derive a contradiction. Therefore, to maintain this strategy, we only need information on U at the point of minimum of W . Since there is no way to know a priori what those points are going to be, we need to require information on U at points which are minima of functions of the form (t, q ) 7 → ⟨ U (t, q ) − V, q − p⟩ for V ∈ Rd, p ∈ ¯Ω. Moreover, at such a point of minimum, the equation (9.1) satisfied by U is only used to obtain information about the variations of ⟨U (t, q ), q − p⟩.113 To illustrate the above, let us consider the following case: let V ∈ Rd, p ∈ ¯Ω,and (t∗, q ∗) be a minimum point of (t, q ) 7 → ⟨ U (t, q ) − V, q − p⟩. Using the fact that 

U is a solution to (9.1) on R+ × ¯Ω, we obtain 

∂t⟨U (t∗, q ∗), q ∗ −p⟩+F (q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) ·DqU (t∗, q ∗)·(q∗ −p) = ⟨G(q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , q ∗ −p⟩.

(9.3) Assume that q∗ is in the interior of Ω. Then we have 

0 = U (t∗, q ∗) − V + ⟨DqU (t∗, q ∗), q ∗ − p⟩. (9.4) This is clearly the analogue of (9.2). We then deduce that 

∂t⟨U (t∗, q ∗), q ∗ − p⟩ = ⟨G(q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , q ∗ − p⟩ + ⟨F (q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , U (t∗, q ∗) − V ⟩.

(9.5) On the other hand, if q∗ ∈ ∂Ω, thanks to Hypothesis 9.1, we still have 

∂t⟨U (t∗, q ∗), q ∗ − p⟩ ≥ ⟨ G(q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , q ∗ − p⟩ + ⟨F (q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , U (t∗, q ∗) − V ⟩.

(9.6) This is exactly the type of information that will be encoded in the definition of so-called monotone solutions, or M-solutions. A fundamental observation is now in order: the relations (9.5) and (9.6) above do not involve any derivatives of U with respect to q! By invoking the theory of viscosity solutions, initiated by Crandall and Lions [55], we will handle the time derivative term using a test function 3. To this end, observe that if ϑ is a smooth function of t and (t∗, q ∗) is a minimum point of (t, q ) 7 → ⟨ U (t, q ) − V, q − p⟩ − ϑ(t),then we can replace the first term in the above inequality to obtain 

ϑ′(t∗) ≥ ⟨ G(q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , q ∗ − p⟩ + ⟨F (q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , U (t∗, q ∗) − V ⟩. (9.7) We have now seen all the main motivations behind the following definition. 

Definition 9.4. A continuous function U : R+ × ¯Ω 7 → Rd is called a M-solution 

to (9.1) if for all T > 0, V ∈ Rd, p ∈ ¯Ω, ϑ ∈ C 1(R), and (t∗, q ∗) ∈ (0 , T ] × ¯Ω, point of strict minimum of the function (t, q ) 7 → ⟨ U (t, q ) − V, q − p⟩ − ϑ(t) on [0 , T ] × ¯Ω,we have 

ϑ′(t∗) ≥ ⟨ G(q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , q ∗ − p⟩ + ⟨F (q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , U (t∗, q ∗) − V ⟩. (9.8) I called such solutions monotone solutions when I introduced them. In this book addressing broader topics, I use this terminology M-solutions to avoid confusion.  

> 3More details on viscosity solutions are given in Part III of this book.

114 Remark 9.5. I only require this relation to hold at points of strict minimum for two main reasons: first, because it is sufficient, as we will see; and second, because in some cases it is not clear whether it would hold at all minimum points. It is for instance the case for MEs of MFGs of optimal stopping, as I explained in [17]. It will also allow us to obtain strong stability properties for such solutions. 

# 9.3 Uniqueness and stability in finite dimension 

Given the computations that preceded Definition 9.4, we immediately have the following result. 

Proposition 9.6. Let U : [0 , ∞) × ¯Ω 7 → Rd be a classical solution to (9.1) . Then 

U is also an M-solution to (9.1) .

Given the way we constructed Definition 9.4, the following uniqueness result should not come as a surprise. 

Theorem 9.7. Under Hypothesis 9.2, there exists at most one M-solution to (9.1) 

with initial condition U0. Moreover, if such a solution U exists, then for all t ≥ 0,

U (t, ·) is a monotone map. Proof. Let U and V be two M-solutions. We want to show that W defined by 

W (t, q, p ) := ⟨U (t, q ) − V (t, p ), q − p⟩ is non-negative. Assume that there exists 

(T, ˜q, ˜p) ∈ (0 , ∞) × ¯Ω2 such that 

W (T, ˜q, ˜p) < 0.

Then there exist κ, ϵ > 0 such that for all α > 0,

inf  

> t,s ∈[0 ,T ],p,q

⟨U (t, q ) − V (s, p ), q − p⟩ + α(t − s)2 + ϵ(t + s) ≤ − κ < 0.

Using a trivial variant of Stegall’s Lemma in finite dimension (see Section 7.2), for any η > 0, there exist a, b ∈ Rd, ξ1, ξ 2 ∈ R such that |a| + |b| + |ξ1| + |ξ2| ≤ η

and 

(t, s, q, p ) 7 → ⟨ U (t, q ) − V (s, p ), q − p⟩ + α(t − s)2 + ϵ(t + s) + ⟨a, q ⟩ + ⟨b, p ⟩ + ξ1t + ξ2s

has a strict minimum on [0 , T ]2 × ¯Ω2 at (t∗, s ∗, q ∗, p ∗). Assume first that t∗, s ∗ > 0.Since U is an M-solution to (9.1), we obtain 

−ϵ − 2α(t∗ − s∗) − ξ1 ≥⟨ G(q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , q ∗ − p∗⟩

+ ⟨F (q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) , U (t∗, q ∗) − V (s∗, p ∗) + a⟩.

115 The analogous inequality for V gives 

−ϵ − 2α(s∗ − t∗) − ξ2 ≥⟨ G(p∗, V (s∗, p ∗)) , p ∗ − q∗⟩

+ ⟨F (p∗, V (s∗, p ∗)) , V (s∗, p ∗) − U (t∗, q ∗) + b⟩.

Adding the two inequalities yields 

−2ϵ ≥⟨ G(q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) − G(p∗, V (s∗, p ∗)) , q ∗ − p∗⟩

+ ⟨F (q∗, U (t∗, q ∗)) − F (p∗, V (s∗, p ∗)) , U (t∗, q ∗) − V (s∗, p ∗)⟩ + O(η).

Using the monotonicity of (G, F ), we obtain ϵ ≤ 0 (for small enough η compared to ϵ), a contradiction. Thus it simply remains to treat the case t∗ = 0 or s∗ = 0 .Let us consider the case t∗ = 0 (the other is symmetric). From the definition of (t∗, s ∗, q ∗, p ∗) we get 

⟨U0(q∗) − V (s∗, p ∗), q ∗ − p∗⟩ + αs 2 

> ∗

+ ϵs ∗ + ⟨a, q ∗⟩ + ⟨b, p ∗⟩ + ξ2s∗ ≤ − κ

2 . (9.9) Choosing η sufficiently small implies in particular that s∗ is of order (√α)−1.Since V is continuous and satisfies the initial condition, we also have that 

lim inf  

> s→0

⟨U0(q∗) − V (s, p ∗), q ∗ − p∗⟩ ≥ 0.

This yields a contradiction in (9.9) for large enough α because of the mono-tonicity of U0 since, along a subsequence, because of the compactness of ¯Ω, p∗ → ¯p

for some ¯p ∈ ¯Ω and thus V (s∗, p ∗) → U0(¯ p).Hence we have shown W ≥ 0. Now, for any t ≥ 0, q ∈ Ω, and ϵ > 0 small enough so that q ± ϵv ∈ Ω for all |v| ≤ 1, we get 

⟨U (t, q ) − V (t, q − ϵv ), ϵv ⟩ ≥ 0.

Dividing by ϵ and letting ϵ → 0, we obtain 

⟨U (t, q ) − V (t, q ), v ⟩ ≥ 0.

Therefore, U (t, q ) = V (t, q ), and the equality also holds on [0 , T ]× ¯Ω by continuity. Setting U = V , we have also shown the monotonicity of U in q.The previous uniqueness result justifies the notion of M-solution introduced in Definition 9.4. Another good argument in favor of this notion is the stability that they enjoy, which is highlighted in the next result. 116 Proposition 9.8. Let (Gn, F n)n≥0 be a sequence of functions converging locally uniformly to (G, F ) : ¯Ω × Rd 7 → R2d. Let (Un)n≥0 be a sequence of M-solutions to 

(9.1) (associated to (Gn, F n)), such that Un →n→∞ U locally uniformly. Then U

is an M-solution to (9.1) .Proof. Let T > 0, V ∈ Rd, p ∈ ¯Ω, ϑ a smooth real-valued function, and (t∗, q ∗) ∈

(0 , T ] × ¯Ω a point of strict minimum of (t, q ) 7 → ⟨ U (t, q ) − V, q − p⟩ − ϑ(t). For all 

n ≥ 0, once again by Stegall’s Lemma, there exist sequences (an)n≥0 and (ξn)n≥0

with |an| + |ξn| ≤ 1/n , such that the function 

(t, q ) 7 → ⟨ Un(t, q ) − V − an, q − p⟩ − ϑ(t) − ξnt

has a strict minimum on [0 , T ] × ¯Ω at (tn, q n). Since (t∗, q ∗) is a strict minimum, 

(tn, q n) → (t∗, q ∗) as n → ∞ . Because tn > 0 and Un is an M-solution, we have: 

ϑ′(tn) + ξn ≥ ⟨ Gn(qn, U n(tn, q n)) , q n − p⟩ + ⟨Fn(qn, U n(tn, q n)) , U n(tn, q n) − V − an⟩.

Passing to the limit yields the result. 

Remark 9.9. Considering points of strict minimum is very useful in this proof. Indeed, it is not clear that general (non-strict) minima can be approximated by a sequence of minima, whereas this is guaranteed for strict minima. This is mainly due to the fact that we constrained the shape of the function we are minimizing. In particular, we do not allow terms such as |q − q∗|2.

# 9.4 Monotone solutions of master equations on spaces of measures 

In this section, I want to introduce an analogue notion of M-solution for MEs on spaces of probability measures such as 

∂tU − ⟨∇ μU (t, μ, x, ·), div (DpH(·, ∇xU (t, μ, ·)) μ) + σ∆μ⟩− σ∆xU + H(x, ∇xU ) = f (μ)( x) in (0 , ∞) × M 1(Td) × Td,U |t=0 = U0.

(9.10) where ⟨· , ·⟩ denotes the extension of the L2(Td, R) scalar product. The data of the problem are H : Td × Rd 7 → R, f : M1(Td) 7 → C (Td, R), U0 : M1(Td) 7 → C (Td, R),and σ > 0. Here, as in Section 3, ∇μ denotes the vertical (or flat) derivative with respect to the measure argument μ. Even if this use of ∇μ is only formal, because typical solutions are not necessarily V-differentiable, the point of view here is more aligned with the one of Section 4.6. Namely, M1(Td) will be equipped with the 117 natural extension of the W1 distance and not with the distance in total variation. Hence, define for μ, ν ∈ M 1(Td),

W1(μ, ν ) = sup 

> ∫
> Td

ϕ(x)( μ − ν)( dx ), (9.11) where the supremum is taken over ϕ : Td 7 → R such that Lip (ϕ) ≤ 1 and ∥ϕ∥∞ ≤ 1.Note the notation W1 is slightly abused here, since the previous does not exactly coincide with the 1-Wasserstein distance. Indeed, the diameter of Td is greater than 1 in general. This should not be a worry for the careful reader, and the not careful one can continue to ignore it. Note that compared to (8.11), there are three main differences. 1. First, the state space is not Rd but Td. This implies that any set of the form 

[0 , T ]×M 1(Td)×Td is compact. This will spare us the discussion on boundary conditions or behaviour of the solution at infinity. The latter are interesting questions, but they are not treated here to focus on the monotonicity and its implications. 2. I have here assumed that the dependences in ∇xU and μ are separated in 

H. This will ease the presentation of the associated monotone regime. 3. The ME is set on M1(Td) rather than on P(Td). This is mainly a choice of modelling: I want to insist upon the fact that the restriction to P(Td)

is useless and that we can consider situations in which fewer players can be present. This will allow me to avoid some uninteresting technicalities. The notion of M-solution to (9.10) shall be similar to the one for (9.1). The main idea is to adapt the definition from the previous section, drawing the following analogy: 

Gi(q, U ) ← f (μ)( x) − H(x, ∇xU ) + σ∆xU, F i(q, U ) ← − div (DpH(x, ∇xU )μ) − σ∆μ, (9.12) where i corresponds to x and q corresponds to μ. The main difference is that, whereas in the previous section we considered solutions U (t, q ) valued in a finite dimensional space, U (t, μ ) will now be valued in some infinite dimensional subspace of (Td 7 → R). Hence, the regularity of U (t, μ ) with respect to the state variable 

x is a fundamental question in the analysis of (9.10), notably because our new operators F and G are differential (in x) operators. To keep the present discussion quite simple, I will simply restrict my attention to solutions U such that for all t, μ ,

U (t, μ ) ∈ C 2(Td, R), and will comment later on possible extensions in this direction. 118 As I did in the finite dimensional case, the starting point on M-solutions shall be to consider a proof of uniqueness / propagation of monotonicity. Such uniqueness is a priori only valid in the monotone setting, which will be here defined by 

Hypothesis 9.10. The Hamiltonian H is convex in its second argument. The mappings f, U 0 : M1(Td) 7 → C (Td, R) are monotone, that is for all μ, ν ∈ M 1(Td)

⟨f (μ) − f (ν), μ − ν⟩ ≥ 0,

and the same holds for U0.

The following result holds. 

Proposition 9.11. Assume Hypothesis 9.10 holds. Then, there exists at most one smooth solution to (9.10) .

Smooth refers here to the fact that the solution is V-differentiable everywhere, and that its V-derivative is a smooth function of all its arguments. 

Proof. The proof is quite similar to the one of Proposition 9.3. Consider U and V

two solutions, and define W by 

W (t, μ, ν ) = ⟨U (t, μ ) − V (t, ν ), μ − ν⟩.

As a consequence of the identity 

∇μW (t, μ, ν ) = U (t, μ ) − V (t, ν ) + ⟨∇ μU (t, μ ), μ − ν⟩,

and the similar one for ∇ν , it is easy to check that W is a solution to 

∂tW − ⟨∇ μW (t, μ, ν, ·), div (DpH(·, ∇xU (t, μ, ·)) μ) + σ∆μ⟩− ⟨∇ ν W (t, μ, ν, ·), div (DpH(·, ∇xV (t, ν, ·)) ν) + σ∆ν⟩

= ⟨f (μ) − f (ν), μ − ν⟩ + ⟨σ∆U − H(·, ∇xU (t, μ, ·)) , μ − ν⟩

+ ⟨σ∆V − H(·, ∇xV (t, ν, ·)) , ν − μ⟩− ⟨ U (t, μ, ·) − V (t, ν, ·), div (DpH(·, ∇xU (t, μ, ·)) μ) + σ∆μ⟩− ⟨ V (t, ν, ·) − U (t, μ, ·), div (DpH(·, ∇xV (t, ν, ·)) ν) + σ∆ν⟩ .

Remark that the terms in σ cancel out. Using the convexity of H, we obtain 

⟨∇ x(U (t, μ, ·) − V (t, ν, ·)) · DpH(·, ∇xU (t, μ, ·)) , μ ⟩

+⟨H(·, ∇xV (t, ν, ·)) − H(·, ∇xU (t, μ, ·)) , μ ⟩ ≥ 0.

Using the monotonicity of f we obtain 

∂tW − ⟨∇ μW (t, μ, ν, ·), div (DpH(·, ∇xU (t, μ, ·)) μ) + σ∆μ⟩− ⟨∇ ν W (t, μ, ν, ·), div (DpH(·, ∇xV (t, ν, ·)) ν) + σ∆ν⟩≥ 0.

(9.13) 119 Since W |t=0 ≥ 0, we can conclude, as in the finite dimensional case, that W ≥ 0,namely by invoking a slight generalization of the first order conditions of Propo-sition 7.1 to M1(Td) and the invariance by the associated flows of M1(Td). This can also be obtained directly by considering a point of minimum of W (t, ·, ·), and using the computations of Proposition 4.24. To conclude, it now remains to show that W ≥ 0 ⇒ U = V , since the non-negativity of W already proved the propagation of monotonicity. Take t ≥ 0 and 

μ ∈ M 1(Td) such that |μ| < 1. For ϵ ∈ (0 , 1 − | μ|), we can compute for any 

ν ∈ P (Td)

ϵ⟨U (t, μ + ϵν ) − V (t, μ ), ν ⟩ ≥ 0.

Hence, by dividing by ϵ and by using the continuity of U , we deduce that U (t, μ, ·) ≥

V (t, μ, ·) in Td. By symmetry we deduce that U (t, μ ) = V (t, μ ) and by continuity that the equality holds for all μ ∈ M 1(Td).Based on the same reasoning as previously, we are thus led to consider the following definition. 

Definition 9.12. A continuous function U : R+ × M 1(Td) × Td 7 → R is an M-solution to (9.10) if: 

• For every t > 0 and μ ∈ M 1(Td), the map x 7 → U (t, μ, x ) belongs to 

C2(Td, R).

• The map (t, μ, x ) 7 → ∇ xU (t, μ, x ) is bounded over [0 , T ] × M 1(Td) × Td for any T > 0.

• For every T > 0, ϕ ∈ C 2(Td, R), ν ∈ M 1(Td), ϑ ∈ C 1(R), and (t∗, μ ∗) ∈

(0 , T ] × M 1(Td) a point of strict minimum of the map 

(t, μ ) 7 → ⟨ U (t, μ, ·) − ϕ, μ − ν⟩ − ϑ(t)

on [0 , T ] × M 1(Td), we have: 

ϑ′(t∗) ≥⟨ f (μ∗) − H(·, ∇xU (t∗, μ ∗)) + σ∆xU (t∗, μ ∗, ·), μ ∗ − ν⟩

+ ⟨U (t∗, μ ∗, ·) − ϕ, −div (DpH(·, ∇xU (t∗, μ ∗, ·)) μ∗) − σ∆μ∗⟩.

(9.14) 

Remark 9.13. This definition of a solution to the master equation only requires continuity with respect to the measure variable μ, the bound on ∇xU is discussed below. 

120 9.5 Uniqueness and stability on spaces of measures 

As we could expect, the uniqueness of M-solutions to (9.10) in the monotone regime holds, as the next result shows. 

Theorem 9.14. Assume Hypothesis 9.10. Then there exists at most one M-solution to (9.10) and if such a U exists, it is such that U (t, ·) is monotone for all times t ≥ 0.Proof. Consider two M-solutions U and V and define the function W by 

W (t, s, μ, ν ) = ⟨U (t, μ ) − V (s, ν ), μ − ν⟩.

I want to show that for all time t ≥ 0, W (t, t, ·, ·) ≥ 0. Arguing by contradiction, I assume that there exists T > 0, ϵ, κ, ¯η > 0 such that

2( s∗ − t∗)α − ϵ ≥⟨ f (μ∗) − H(·, ∇xU (t∗, μ ∗)) + σ∆xU (t∗, μ ∗, ·), μ ∗ − ν∗⟩ + ξ1

121 Since η > 0 can be arbitrarily small compared to ϵ > 0, we arrive at a contradic-tion. Hence, either s∗ = 0 or t∗ = 0 . Arguing as in Theorem 9.7, the limit α → ∞ 

leads to a contradiction as well since W |t=s=0 is non-negative. Hence, W (t, t, ·, ·) ≥ 0 and the rest of the proof follows from the end of the proof of Proposition 9.11. 

Remark 9.15. Note that the bound in ∇xU is fundamental to estimate the remain-ing terms in ϕ1, ϕ 2 in the proof. Such perturbations are useful to prove stability results as the one below, as well as to study some more involved problems like optimal stopping. However, note that to simply establish uniqueness of solutions to (9.10) , we could have define M-solutions by asking for the required relation to hold at any point of minimum, thus avoiding the use of the perturbations, and the uniform estimate on ∇xU as well. 

There is also a stability of M-solutions in this infinite dimensional setting. It can be formulated as follows. 

Proposition 9.16. Let (Hn)n≥0 be a sequence of functions in C1(Td × Rd) which converges locally uniformly (in C1(Td × Rd)) toward some function H and a se-quence (fn)n≥0 in C(M1(Td), C(Td, R)) which converges locally uniformly toward some function f . Consider (Un)n≥0 a sequence of M-solutions to (9.10) on [0 , T ] ×M1(Td) × Td (associated with Hn and fn) and assume that (Un, ∇xUn, ∆xUn)n≥0

converges uniformly toward (U, ∇xU, ∆xU ) for some function U : [0 , T ]×M 1(Td) 7 →C2(Td, R). Then, U is an M-solution to (9.10) associated with H and f .

The proof, which is a simple adaptation of Proposition 9.8 to this new setting, is left as an exercise. 

Comments on the regularity in the x variable 

In all this section, the regularity of the function U with respect to the spatial vari-able x has been treated in a quite simplistic manner. I assume sufficient regularity (namely C2 in x) so that all the techniques used in the finite dimensional case could be applied here. This is a strong requirement and it has as a consequence that existence of such solutions is quite hard to establish. A natural question is to understand whether or not these types of regularity assumptions can be avoided. A first answer is that when those uniformly elliptic terms are present in the equation (the σ∆), second order regularity in x typically holds. This type of answer is not entirely satisfactory as it leaves open cases in which those second order terms are not uniformly elliptic. Another natural idea is to try to change slightly the definition of M-solution to restrict our attention to probability measures with more regularity, so that the derivatives of U could be understood in a weaker sense. Such 122 ideas have been investigated by Cardaliaguet and Souganidis [42] in the case σ = 0 ,and partially by Cecchin and me [23] in the case σ > 0.It is fair to say that the problem of lowering the regularity requirement in x of the value function U of MFG MEs is mostly open at the moment. 

# 9.6 Bibliographical comments 

The proof of Proposition 9.3 is due to PL Lions and was presented in [100]. Iintroduced the notion of monotone solutions (M-solutions) in [17, 19], to treat respectively finite dimensional MEs and ones on sets of measures. This notion was also used by Cardaliaguet and Souganidis [42] to tackle the case σ = 0 , see also [18] for cases on Hilbert spaces. In [23], we used with Cecchin the notion of monotone solution to show convergence of the finite dimensional approximations of MEs in continuous state space, notably when the state space is discretized. Other known results of uniqueness or stability of MEs relied on the regularity of the solution, and will be mentioned at the end of the next section. 123 10 Existence of solutions to master equations 

To study questions of existence of solutions to MEs, I shall take a quite similar route to the one of the uniqueness section above. Based on another proof of unique-ness, namely one involving regularity rather than monotonicity, I will introduce the notion of Lipschitz solution, and suitable properties that they enjoy. In this section, I want to provide an existence theory for solutions to MEs 

before the appearance of shocks. The notion of solution considered here shall be different from the one of M-solutions. This will lead to developments that could seem at first sight orthogonal to the ones of uniqueness of the previous section. I shall clarify the links between the two notions at the end of this section. I will start by giving the heuristics behind Lipschitz solutions in the finite dimensional setting. Then, I will present the notion of Lipschitz solution in finite dimension, as well as the main results behind it. I will then study in less detail the analogue notion in infinite dimension. Comments on the links between Lipschitz solutions and how to prove that shocks do not appear in monotone regimes will be the last part of this section before the concluding bibliographical comments. 

# 10.1 Regularity implies uniqueness for master equations 

In this section, I elaborate on the comment I made after Proposition 9.3, that when the solution to the ME is smooth, we do not need the monotone regime to claim uniqueness. Let me insist upon the fact that these kinds of general statements are quite standard in the study of non-linear PDEs, and I simply present them because their proofs are instructive. 

Proposition 10.1. Assume that (G, F ) : ¯Ω × Rd 7 → R2d is locally Lipschitz con-tinuous. Let U : [0 , T ] × ¯Ω 7 → Rd be a smooth solution to (9.1) . Then, there exists no other smooth solution to (9.1) with initial condition U |t=0 .Proof. Consider V a smooth solution to (9.1) with initial condition U |t=0 . Define 

W (t, q ) = U (t, q ) − V (t, q ). Observe that W solves 

∂tW +F (q, V )·∇ qW = G(q, U )−G(q, V )+( F (q, V )−F (q, U )) ·∇ qU in (0 , T )× ¯Ω.

(10.1) Take t ≥ 0, and consider i, q such that W i(t, q ) = max 1≤j≤d,p ∈ ¯Ω{W j (t, p )}, which always exist since ¯Ω is compact. Evaluating (10.1) at i, q and recalling the as-sumption I made on the behaviour of F at the boundary of Ω, we obtain that 

∂tW i(t, q ) ≤ C∥W (t, ·)∥∞(1 + ∥DqU (t, ·)∥∞),

124 where C is a Lipschitz constant of (G, F ) on ¯Ω × B(0 , ∥U ∥∞ + ∥V ∥∞). Since a similar computation could be made at points of minimum of W , we have in fact proven that ddt ∥W (t, ·)∥∞ ≤ C∥W (t, ·)∥∞(1 + ∥DqU (t, ·)∥∞),

because such an estimate holds pointwise at any point of extremum of W (t, ·).Since U is smooth and [0 , T ] × ¯Ω is compact, we know that there exists K > 0

such that sup t≤T ∥DqU (t, ·)∥∞ ≤ K − 1. Hence, using Gronwall’s Lemma, we find that 

∥W (t, ·)∥∞ ≤ ∥ W (0 , ·)∥∞eCKt , (10.2) from which the result follows. The key insight from the previous result is the following. In the end, we are able to derive a quantitative propagation of error (10.2) which relies uniquely on the regularity of the data (through the constant C), which is fair to assume, and on the Lipschitz regularity of one of the two solutions (the constant K). Hence, we can hope that Lipschitz regularity is a pivotal one and that it might be enough to define a concept of solution which yields existence easily. 

# 10.2 Lipschitz solutions in finite dimension 

In this section, I show how to define a concept of solution to the ME (9.1) whose sole required regularity is Lipschitz continuity with respect to the variable q ∈ ¯Ω,as it was the crucial quantity in the proof of Proposition 10.1. I will freely use the notation ∥DqU ∥∞ to denote the Lipschitz constant of a map U : ¯Ω 7 → Rd.First, note that (9.1) is of the form 

∂tU (t, q ) + ( V (t, q ) · ∇ q)U (t, q ) = B(t, q ) in (0 , ∞) × ¯Ω, (10.3) with V and B given by V (t, q ) = F (q, U (t, q )) and B(t, q ) = G(q, U (t, q )) . Observe that for equations of the type (10.3), representation formulas are available. Indeed, given (sufficiently regular) V and B, the unique solution to (10.3) with initial condition U0 is given by 

U (t, q ) =  

> ∫t
> 0

B(t − s, q (s)) ds + U0(q(t)) , (10.4) where (q(s)) s∈[0 ,t ] is the solution to 

dds q(s) = −V (t − s, q (s)) , (10.5) 125 with initial condition q(0) = q.4 Let us note that this characteristic formula is well-defined as soon as V is Lipschitz in its second variable, uniformly with respect to its first. Indeed, in such a case, the usual Cauchy-Lipschitz theory applies nicely to (10.5). Denote by Φ( V, B ) the function given by (10.4)-(10.5). We shall see that this mapping Φ is well defined on Lipschitz functions, as it is claimed above. Furthermore, the following shows how to define a solution to (9.1) based on this operator Φ.

Definition 10.2. Let T > 0. A function U : [0 , T ]× ¯Ω 7 → Rd is a Lipschitz solution to (9.1) on [0 , T ] if 

• There exists C > 0 such that for all t ∈ [0 , T ], q ∈ ¯Ω, ∥DqU (t, ·)∥∞ ≤ C.

• U = Φ( F (·, U (·, ·)) , G (·, U (·, ·))) .

Remark 10.3. In the previous definition and in what follows, I abuse slightly no-tation to denote the Lipschitz constant of U (t, ·) with respect to q by ∥DqU (t, ·)∥∞.

As expected, this notion of solution is consistent with the classical one. 

Proposition 10.4. Let U : [0 , T ] × ¯Ω 7 → Rd be a smooth solution to (9.1) . Then 

U is a Lipschitz solution to (9.1) .

The proof is trivial and left as an exercise to the reader. The main result on Lipschitz solution is the following. 

Proposition 10.5. Assume that U0 is Lipschitz, that F and G are locally Lips-chitz. Then there exists Tc ∈ (0 , ∞] and U : [0 , T c) × ¯Ω 7 → Rd such that: for any 

T < T c, U is a Lipschitz solution to (9.1) on [0 , T ], for any V Lipschitz solution to (9.1) on [0 , T ′], we have T ′ < T c and V = U |t≤T ′ . Moreover, either Tc = ∞, or

EC := {U : [0 , T ] × ¯Ω 7 → Rd, ∥U ∥∞ ≤ C, ∥DqU ∥∞ ≤ C}.

∥Ψ( U )∥∞ ≤ T K (1 + C) + K, 

where K denotes a constant which bounds ∥DqG∥∞ and ∥U0∥∞. Hence, we see that if C > K , then for T sufficiently small, ∥Ψ( U )∥∞ ≤ C. From now on, we can

It follows that, possibly choosing T smaller, Ψ is a contraction in the supremum norm. By a classical Picard iteration argument, we can then show that for all 

U ∈ EC , the sequence (Ψ k(U )) k≥0 converges, provided C is large enough and T

small enough. This limit is unique since Ψ is a contraction. It remains to show the existence of a maximal time of existence. It is quite straightforward as it simply follows from the remark that the constant C and T in the previous depend only on ∥U0∥∞ + ∥DqU0∥∞ (in addition to the dependence on 

F , G and Ω of course...). Hence, we can repeat the previous argument by replacing 

U0 by U (T, ·), and then actually do it an infinite number of times to create an increasing sequence of times (Tn)n≥0, on which a unique Lipschitz solution exists. Then, only one of two things happens. Either the sequence of times Tn that we construct in such a way is unbounded, or it is bounded. In the second case, it must be that ∥U (Tn, ·)∥∞ + ∥DqU (Tn, ·)∥∞ → ∞ as n → ∞ , and the divergence has to come from the derivative, since otherwise we can easily show that U has to remain bounded. This result shows that Lipschitz regularity in q implies uniqueness of solutions to (9.1). This uniqueness has to be taken with care since it says nothing about uniqueness in weaker classes of notions of solutions. Indeed, a priori, the existence of a Lipschitz solution U prevents the existence of another Lipschitz solution on the same time interval. For instance a weaker solution V (e.g. a monotone solution) to (9.1) could co-exist with U and still U̸ = V . The next result argues in the opposite direction. It is a form of weak-strong uniqueness result in the sense that the existence of a Lipschitz solution prevents the existence of certain weak solutions, namely ones that can be approximated uniformly by smooth functions. 128 Proposition 10.6. Consider a Lipschitz solution U to (9.1) on the time interval 

[0 , T c). Consider for T ∈ (0 , T c), ϵ > 0, a classical solution Vϵ of 

|∂tVϵ + ⟨F (q, V ϵ), ∇q⟩Vϵ − G(q, V ϵ)| ≤ ϵ in (0 , T ) × ¯Ω,

|Vϵ(0 , q ) − U0(q)| ≤ ϵ in ¯Ω. (10.6)

Remark 10.7. From the previous result we can indeed deduce that the Lipschitz solution attracts the limits of approximations of (9.1) , since the constant C in 

(10.11) or (10.7) does not depend on Vϵ.

# 10.3 Lipschitz solutions in infinite dimension 

In this section, I present how to extend the notion of Lipschitz solution to the infinite dimensional equation (9.10). I will go over this generalization relatively briefly as the main ideas are the same ones as in the finite dimensional case. The main difficulty in generalizing the previous case lies, as always, in the fact that (9.10) involves derivatives of U with respect to x, or in other words that the infinite dimensional analogues of F and G are differential operators. In this case, we will be interested in the gradient of the value function defined by W := ∇xU . Formally, it is a solution to 

∂tW − ⟨∇ μW (t, μ, x, ·), div (DpH(·, W (t, μ, ·)) μ) + σ∆μ⟩ − σ∆xW

+ DxH(x, W ) + DxW · DpH(x, W ) = ∇xf (μ)( x) in (0 , ∞) × M 1(Td) × Td.

(10.12) This equation only involves linear terms in the derivatives of W with respect to x.One can therefore give an analogue definition of a Lipschitz solution in this case, once again based on a fixed point procedure on the underlying linearized transport equation. Of course, we need here to specify the distance with which M1(Td) will be equipped, so that we can talk about Lipschitz solutions. In this section on Lipschitz solutions, all statements shall be made on the space (M1(Td), W1), see (9.11).

Similar results of well-posedness at the level of U can be established but I do not focus on these types of questions here. 133 10.4 Filling in the blanks between existence and uniqueness 

Up to now, we saw two different notions of solutions to the ME. One is based on an a priori short time horizon result of well-posedness, and another one based on the fact that, in the monotone regime, we can hope for global well-posedness (through the propagation of monotonicity and the uniqueness of M-solutions). In this sec-tion, I present links between these concepts, namely that Lipschitz solutions are M-solutions, and that propagation of monotonicity can lead to propagation of reg-ularity under reasonable assumptions. Once again, I will mainly focus on the case of the finite dimensional ME (9.1). There are, of course, several important and meaningful difficulties when trying to generalize the following in infinite dimension, but they exceed the scope of this book and references are given below. 

10.4.1 From Lipschitz solutions to M-solutions 

For master equations in finite dimension, Lipschitz solutions are also M-solutions as the next result shows. 

Proposition 10.13. Assume that the assumptions of Proposition 10.5 hold. Let 

U : [0 , T ] × ¯Ω 7 → Rd be a Lipschitz solution to (9.1) on [0 , T ], then U is an M-solution to (9.1) on [0 , T ].Proof. First remark that U is continuous in time, thus continuous. It follows from the definition of a Lipschitz solution as well as from the regularity of F and G

(namely local Lipschitz regularity). It is an easy fact to verify and the interested reader can estimate the difference between U (t, q ) and U (s, p ) using the definition of U and the stability of the ODE quite simply. Let V ∈ Rd, ˜q ∈ ¯Ω, a smooth real function ϑ and (t0, q 0) ∈ (0 , T ] × ¯Ω a point of strict minimum of the function (t, q ) 7 → ⟨ U (t, q ) − V, q − ˜q⟩ − ϑ(t) on [0 , T ] × ¯Ω.For all t ∈ [0 , T ], q ∈ Ω,

⟨U (t, q ) − V, q − ˜q⟩ − ϑ(t) ≥ ⟨ U (t0, q 0) − V, q 0 − ˜q⟩ − ϑ(t0). (10.14) Introduce (q(s)) s∈[0 ,t 0] the solution to 

dds q(s) = −F (q(s), U (t0 − s, q (s))) ,

with initial condition q0. Evaluating (10.14) at t0 − ϵ, q (ϵ) for ϵ > 0, we obtain 

⟨U (t0 − ϵ, q (ϵ)) − V, q (ϵ) − ˜q⟩ − ϑ(t0 − ϵ) ≥ ⟨ U (t0, q 0) − V, q 0 − ˜q⟩ − ϑ(t0).

134 Rewriting this inequality yields 

⟨U (t0 − ϵ, q (ϵ)) − U (t0, q 0), q (ϵ) − ˜q⟩ + ϑ(t0) − ϑ(t0 − ϵ) ≥ ⟨ U (t0, q 0) − V, q 0 − q(ϵ)⟩.

Since U is a Lipschitz solution to (9.1), we deduce that 

⟨−  

> ∫t0
> t0−ϵ

G(q(t0 − s), U (s, q (t0 − s))) ds, q (ϵ) − ˜q⟩ + ϑ(t0) − ϑ(t0 − ϵ)

≥ ⟨ U (t0, q 0) − V, q 0 − q(ϵ)⟩.

Dividing by ϵ and taking the limit ϵ → 0, we recover the relation that M-solutions must satisfy, thanks to the definition of (q(s)) s∈[0 ,t 0], hence the claim follows. 

Remark 10.14. If Lipschitz solutions are M-solutions, then one could wonder about the use of the weaker concept of M-solution. There are two main arguments in favor of the interest of M-solutions. The first one is that, using the stability explained in Propositions 9.8 and 9.16, we can prove existence of M-solutions which are not Lipschitz continuous, as for instance in [19]. Such solutions are particularly helpful when we consider MEs (say (9.1) ) with data (say F and G) which are not Lipschitz continuous. The other type of case of interest for M-solutions is when the ME is so intricate that we are not able to write it properly, as is the case in MFG of optimal stopping, see [17] for instance. In such situations, the underlying evolution of the forward quantity can be discontinuous, and thus the notion of Lipschitz solution quite meaningless, while the value function stays continuous and the notion of M-solution makes perfect sense. 

10.4.2 Existence of solutions on arbitrary long time intervals I: a priori estimates 

To justify that, in monotone regimes, we can prove existence of solutions on arbi-trary long time intervals, a natural step is to try to prove a Lipschitz estimate on the solution to (9.1). If such an estimate can be justified, then from Proposition 10.5, we know that the critical time of existence must be +∞. In this section, we focus on proving formally an a priori estimate, and in Section 10.4.3 on how we can justify it. In the following, I shall be quite formal, and simply assume that all the func-tions involved are sufficiently smooth to justify all the computations. What I want to establish is a quantitative estimate on the regularity of U , the solution to (9.1), based on the regularity of F, G and U0.135 The main idea of the following a priori estimate is that the propagation of monotonicity of Proposition 9.3 already gave half of an estimate. Indeed, because 

U is monotone, for any q, p ∈ ¯Ω, t ≥ 0,

⟨U (t, p ) − U (t, q ), p − q⟩ ≥ 0.

Taking, if possible, p = q + ϵξ for ξ ∈ Rd and ϵ > 0, dividing by ϵ and taking the limit ϵ → 0 yields the inequality 

ξ · DqU (t, q ) · ξ ≥ 0.

Hence, if we are able to show an estimate of the form 

∀T > 0, ∃A, ∀t ≤ T, q ∈ ¯Ω, ξ ∈ Rd, ξ · DqU (t, q ) · ξ ≤ A|ξ|2,

then we would have indeed proven an L∞ estimate on (the symmetric part) of 

DqU . Because of the monotonicity, bounding from above quantities such as ξ ·

DqU (t, q ) · ξ is harder than bounding them from below. Hence, I shall investigate in the following bounds from below on quantities of the form 

ξ · DqU (t, q ) · ξ − A|DqU (t, q )ξ|2,

which imply uniform estimates on DqU . Objects of the nature of DqU shall play a strong role in the proof. To lighten notation, I fix a convention that for any map 

ϕ : ¯Ω 7 → Rd, x, y ∈ Rd

x · Dqϕ · y := 

> d∑
> i,j =1

xi∂qi ϕj yj ,

with the same convention if only x or y is used. 

Proposition 10.15. Assume that U0, F and G are smooth Lipschitz functions, that U is a Lipschitz solution associated to them, and that there exists α > 0 such that (

DqG DqFDuG DuF

> )

≥ αId, DqU0 ≥ αId. (10.15) 

Then Z defined by Z(t, q, ξ ) = ξ · DqU (t, q ) · ξ − β|DqU (t, q ) · ξ|2 is non-negative for some β > 0 which only depends on α, ∥DqU0∥∞, ∥DqG∥∞ and ∥DqF ∥∞.Proof. Start with the case β = 0 . = ξD qG(q, U )ξ − (DqU · ξ)DuG(q, U )ξ − ξD qF (q, U )( DqU · ξ)+ ( DqU · ξ)DuF (q, U )( DqU · ξ)

− 2β(DqU · ξ)DqG(q, U )ξ + 2 β(DqU · ξ)DqF (q, U )( DqU · ξ).

Using the α-monotonicity of (G, F ), we obtain that the right-hand side is bounded from below by 

α(|ξ|2 + |DqU · ξ|2) − 2β(DqU · ξ)DqG(q, U )ξ + 2 β(DqU · ξ)DqF (q, U )( DqU · ξ)

≥ α(|ξ|2 + |DqU ξ |2) − βK (|ξ|2 + |DqU · ξ|2),

where K = 4 max( ∥DqG∥∞, ∥DqF ∥∞). Hence, if β < α/K , Z is a solution to 

∂tZ + ⟨F (q, U ), ∇qZ⟩ + ( ∇ξZ)DuF (q, U )( DqU · ξ) − (∇ξZ)DuG(q, U )ξ ≥ 0.

Furthermore, if β < α  

> ∥DqU0∥2

, then Z|t=0 ≥ 0. Hence, arguing with a comparison principle, we indeed obtain that Z ≥ 0.

Remark 10.16. The comparison principle used to conclude that Z ≥ 0 is not detailed above. Since ¯Ω is compact, Z smooth and quadratic in ξ, I leave it as an exercise to the interested reader. Remark also that the relation (10.16) is the analogue of (9.2) used earlier to show the propagation of monotonicity. 

Note that in the previous result, I used more than simple monotonicity and Lipschitz regularity, a stronger form of monotonicity was quite necessary to get this a priori estimate. There are variants of this result where the requirements are still stronger than monotonicity, but weaker than the one used here, see [24, 100] for instance. There are counter-examples when we only assume simple monotonicity. 137 10.4.3 Existence of solutions on arbitrary long time intervals II: justification of the a priori estimate 

In order to finally show existence of a Lipschitz solution to (9.1) on arbitrary long time intervals, it remains to justify the previous a priori estimate, or in other words to show that a Lipschitz solution will actually verify the quantitative estimate ∥DqU ∥∞ ≤ β−1, even though it is not a smooth solution to (9.1) as in the statement of Proposition 10.15. In order to do so, we are going to focus on a less differentiable quantity than the Z of Proposition 10.15 which is the quantity 

W defined by 

W (t, q, p ) = ⟨U (t, q ) − U (t, p ), q − p⟩ − β|U (t, q ) − U (t, p )|2. (10.17) In fact we are now going to establish the exact analogue of Proposition 10.15 on this non-differentiated quantity. 

Proposition 10.17. Consider U a Lipschitz solution to (9.1) on [0 , T ]. Assume that U0 is Lipschitz, that G and F are Lipschitz continuous and that (10.15) holds for some α > 0, then W defined by (10.17) is non-negative for some β depending only on α, ∥DqU0∥∞, ∥DqG∥∞ and ∥DqF ∥∞.Proof. Assume that the result does not hold. Consider a point (t0, q 0, p 0) of min-imum of W . Choosing β sufficiently small, we know that t0 > 0 and U (t0, q 0)̸ =

U (t0, p 0). In particular, for any s < t 0

⟨U (t0, q 0) − U (t0, p 0), q 0 − p0⟩ − β|U (t0, q 0) − U (t0, p 0)|2 ≤⟨U (t0 − s, q (s)) − U (t0 − s, p (s)) , q (s) − p(s)⟩ − β|U (t0 − s, q (s)) − U (t0 − s, p (s)) |2,

(10.18) where q′(s) = −F (q(s), U (t0 − s, q (s))) , q (0) = q0 and p′(s) = −F (p(s), U (t0 −

s, p (s))) , p (0) = p0. Using the definition of Lipschitz solutions, we obtain that 

U (t0, q 0) − U (t0, p 0) = U (t0 − s, q (s)) − U (t0 − s, p (s)) + 

> ∫s

G(q(s′), U (t0 − s′, q (s′))) − G(p(s′), U (t0 − s′, p (s′))) ds ′.

Hence, plugging this information in (10.18), dividing by s and taking the limit 

s → 0 yields 

⟨G(q0, U (t0, q 0)) − G(p0, U (t0, p 0)) , q 0 − p0⟩

using the monotonicity of (G, F ) we obtain that 

α(|q0−p0|2+|U (t0, q 0)−U (t0, p 0)|2) ≤ 2β⟨U (t0, q 0)−U (t0, p 0), G (q0, U (t0, q 0)) −G(p0, U (t0, p 0)) ⟩.

138 Hence, the result follows, since the previous yields a contradiction if β is below some threshold which does not depend on U , but simply on G and α.The previous result implies a quantitative Lipschitz bound on U since it yields 

sup 

> t≤T

∥DqU (t, ·)∥∞ ≤ β−1.

Thus the maximal time of existence of Lipschitz solutions has to be +∞. In particular, we have proven the following. 

Corollary 10.18. Assume that U0 is Lipschitz, that G and F are Lipschitz contin-uous and that (10.15) holds for some α > 0. Then, there exists a unique Lipschitz solution U to (9.1) on [0 , ∞).

Similar developments can also be made in the continuous state space case, following the same techniques, and I refer to the next section for references. 

# 10.5 Bibliographical comments 

The question of existence of solutions to the MFG MEs is of course deeply linked to the question of the notion of solution we are considering. Early results of existence of a solution through the methods of characteristics (which is not presented here and relies mostly on the formula (8.3)) were presented by Lions in [100]. This technique was then also used by Chassagneux, Crisan and Delarue [50] and later by Cardaliaguet, Delarue, Lasry and Lions [39]. The latter provided a detailed analysis of smooth solutions to the MEs by means of the associated forward-backward system. Specifically, the authors showed that under strong regularity requirements, we can differentiate the forward-backward system sufficiently many times to prove that the function defined through (8.3) is indeed smooth, and thus the unique solution to the ME. Results were given in different monotone settings by Gangbo, Meszaros, Mou and Zhang [79], and MEs were notably studied in so-called anti-monotone regimes by Lions and Seeger [102] and Mou and Zhang [106]. See also Mou and Zhang [107] which also passes through the characteristics. A method involving splitting was also introduced by Cardaliaguet, Cirant and Porretta in [38]. The notions of monotone solutions and Lipschitz solutions to the ME are weaker notions of solutions, which require weaker assumptions, and can be established by more abstract methods. The existence of monotone solutions through compact-ness arguments was presented in my works [17, 19]. With Lasry and Lions, we introduced the notion of Lipschitz solutions in [26], and showed existence of such solutions, also making the link with more regular notions of solutions. 139 The Lipschitz a priori estimate on the solution to the ME, which is crucial to establish the existence of solutions, was first established by Lions in [100], and later refined by Lasry, Lions and me in [24] in finite dimensional cases, and by myself in [25] and by Cardaliaguet et al in [39] in the continuous state space case. A complete scheme of proof of the existence of Lipschitz solutions on large time intervals was written by Meynard and me in more involved cases in the series of papers [31, 32, 105]. 140 Part III. Mean field Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equations on the space of probability measures 

In this part, I focus on mean field Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) equations on spaces of probability measures and their link with mean field optimal control prob-lems. I will start by showing how we can derive such equations from optimal control problems, omitting other areas where such PDEs appear, such as in large devia-tions for instance. I will then concentrate on the notion of viscosity solution to such equations, which is the correct one in this context. The notion of viscosity solution allows for an extremely robust proof of uniqueness of solutions, which involves, as usual in viscosity solution theory, the crucial comparison principle. I will then establish existence of solutions for cases arising from optimal control (I will also comment on more general cases). Finally, I will apply these methods to a problem of optimal transport toward a stochastic target. 

# 11 Derivation of an HJB equation 

In this section, I want to present what mean field optimal control problems are and how they give rise to HJB equations on the spaces of probability measures. Throughout this section, O shall denote either Td or Rd. Furthermore, let me mention that the problem of interest in this section is clearly an optimal transport problem. However, the following presentation is quite different from usual ones in this theory. 

# 11.1 The optimal control problems 

Starting from a measure μ ∈ P (O) at time t = 0 , we are interested in transporting it optimally in a certain time interval [0 , T ]. The optimality is with respect to a cost which is to be given just below. As we saw in Section 2, there are several ways to consider variations on P(O). Here, we focus on the case in which the controller chooses speeds, or velocities, which are going to transport the initial measure μ,as in Section 2.6. In particular, general Lagrangian evolutions, or randomization are going to be allowed. To introduce the problem, assume that the controller chooses a (smooth) ve-locity field α : [0 , T ] × O 7 → Rd, (t, x ) 7 → α(t, x ) such that ∥Dxα∥∞ < ∞, then the evolution (mt)t∈[0 ,T ] of the initial measure μ will be given through the continuity 141 equation 

∂tm + div (αm ) = 0 in (0 , T ) × O , (11.1) with initial condition m0 = μ. In this case, we are going to consider costs of the form 

˜J (α, m ) :=  

> ∫T
> 0
> ∫
> O

L(x, α (t, x ), m t)mt(dx )dt + G(mT ),

where L : O × Rd × P (O) 7 → R+ and G : P(O) 7 → R+ ∪ { + ∞} are given functions. The interpretation of the cost functional ˜J is quite natural: 

• G represents a standard terminal cost, which can be infinite to model con-straints on the final measure mT .

• At time t, and position x, if the speed chosen is α(t, x ), the cost is augmented by L(x, α (t, x ), m t)mt(dx )dt . The fact that this quantity is linear in mt(dx )

plays a fundamental role in the following, namely because it yields the shape of the HJB equation below. It is here that we see the mean field part of the problem. Indeed, if we forget about the dependence of L in m, the cost is linear in mt(dx ), the number of particles at x at time t.As we saw in Section 2, these kinds of dynamics are not entirely satisfactory as they do not allow us to go from any measure to any other measure under rea-sonable assumptions. The classical solution in optimal transport theory to this problem has been to consider the (very natural) possibility for the controller to choose a velocity field α which is discontinuous in x, namely since ˜J does not re-quire any regularity on α. In this context, (11.1) may have multiple solutions and the control is then not only α but a pair (α, m ). In the following I consider the set-ting of mixed flows of Section 2.6, which is more natural in these types of problems. In what follows, I shall assume quite naturally that the controller can randomize its controls. Formally, this means that the controller has access to a way to draw randomly the control it chooses. This setting is natural in games, where it is called mixed strategies, or in optimal control, where it is sometimes called relaxed controls. Mathematically, the controller can choose a randomization space K and a mixed vector field b : [0 , T ] × O × K 7 → Rd, and an associated weak solution 5

˜m ∈ C ([0 , T ], P(O × K)) to the continuity equation 

∂t ˜m + div x (b(t, x, k ) ˜ m) = 0 in (0 , T ) × O × K, (11.2) such that (π1)# ˜m0 = μ. As presented in Section 2.6, from the point of view of dynamics, it is equivalent to choosing a standard probability space (Ω , A, P) and

Several remarks are now in order. 

• Under reasonable growth conditions, see Remark 13.8 below, the problem (11.4) is equivalent to 

inf  

> (X,Z )

J1(X, Z ), (11.5) where the infimum is taken over pairs (Xt, Z t)t∈[0 ,T ] such that L(X0) = μ

and (11.3) is satisfied almost surely for all t ∈ [0 , T ]. This is an immediate consequence of Proposition 2.6 above. 

• The randomization set K in (11.4) or the probability space in (11.5) are in fact also a part of the decision of the controller. But since they can be fixed (provided that they are sufficiently rich), I shall omit this choice in the following. 143 • I am here dealing with specific controlled trajectories, namely only the speed is controlled and there are no external forces. Generalizations are of course possible, and several other control trajectories are natural, for instance as in mean field optimal stopping or singular control. 

• When the cost L is convex in its second argument, we immediately see that the problems (11.4) and (11.5) are also equivalent to the infimum over all pair (α, m ) which are measurable, with m ∈ C ([0 , T ], P(O)) such that (11.1) is satisfied in a weak sense, of the cost ˜J (α, m ). Indeed, one problem has a lower value than the other because it is a relaxation (hence more controls are available). Jensen’s inequality and developments following Remark 2.7 give the other inequality. Of the two problems (11.4) and (11.5), it is not clear that one has a strictly better form than the other. This is meant in terms of notation of course, since I just stated that they are equivalent. The formulation of (11.4) is more insightful about the type of relaxation phenomena that appear for the controller, but it is quite a bit heavier in terms of notation. On the other hand, (11.5) uses the powerful language of the probability theory to convey the same idea in a more efficient way, but can be misleading about the nature of the problem, especially when there is an actual randomness in the problem. 

# 11.2 Derivation of mean field optimal control from control of 

# N -particle systems 

I derive here the problems described above as the mean field limit of optimal control problems of systems of N particles. Since the convergence of such problems is not a topic of this book, I am going to be quite brief, and simply state some ideas. Let N ≥ 1 and consider (xi

The main interest of the previous computation is to lead us to one of the main issues of problems such as (11.4), namely that Wasserstein distances (and more generally value functions of such problems) are not H-differentiable, see Remark 4.31. Hence, we shall need a weak notion of solution to (11.10) in order to charac-terize the value function as the unique solution, in a sense to be given in the next section, of the associated HJB equation. 148 11.5 Formal optimality conditions in deterministic settings 

I present here another way to address problems such as (11.4), which is particularly efficient when the problem at hand is deterministic. Since this other approach uses different tools from the ones introduced in this book, I will stay formal and only present it for the sake of completeness. I start from almost the same problem as (11.4), except that I assume that the cost does not mix the dependencies in α and m, and work at the level of the non-relaxed problem:

where the supremum is taken over all u ∈ C 1, and where I did not simply multiply the constraint (11.12) by the Lagrange multiplier but also perform an integration by parts. Standard questions of optimization and calculus of variations can then be raised on this inf sup problem. Arguing again formally, the optimality conditions of the previous problem can be written in terms of the following coupled PDEs 

−∂tu + H(x, ∇xu) = ∇μF(mt) in (0 , T ) × O ,αt(x) = −DpH(x, ∇xu(t, x )) in (0 , T ) × O ,∂tm + div x(αm ) = 0 in (0 , T ) × O ,m|t=0 = m0, u|t=T = ∇μG(mT ).

This kind of system is of the same structure as (8.10) in MFG, and its study can allow, under certain convexity assumptions on the problem, one to obtain optimal controls. 149 Bibliographical comments 

HJB equations are a central object in the mathematical analysis of optimal control problems. I do not recall this general literature in finite dimension, and simply refer to Bardi and Capuzzo-Dolcetta [12] for deterministic problems and to Fleming and Soner [76] for stochastic problems. I focus here on the case of equations posed on the set of probability measures. There are several independent starting points for the study of HJB equations on spaces of probability measures. An important one was the course of Lions [99] in which he studied an HJB equation on P2(Rd) as an illustration of the calculus he introduced on this space through the Hilbertian lift. An equation was also derived and studied by Cardaliaguet and Quincampoix in [41] to study a zero sum game. Quincampoix then continued to work on HJB equations with Jimenez and Marigonda [89, 103], associated with optimal control problems, using a quite geometric point of view. Motivated by questions of large deviations in statistical physics, Feng produced an impressive list of works on the topic with several co-authors [8, 72, 73, 74, 75]. This last set of works is mainly concerned with HJB equations having singular terms which are the horizontal differential of some functionals, except for Ambrosio and Feng [8] which deals with so-called metric cases. Such problems have also been considered independently as optimal control problems, by a long list of authors: Cosso, Gozzi, Kharroubi, Pham and Rosestolato [53], Daudin and Seeger [63], Soner and Yan [116], Bayraktar, Ekren and Zhang [15]; or because of their links with MFG, for instance by Cecchin and Delarue [49]. The study of optimal control problems of the form of (11.4) has also been carried out through usual optimality conditions, without passing through the HJB equation, as in Section 11.5. It is for instance the case of all results on optimal transport, especially on its dynamic formulation. I do not expand too much on this literature either but refer the reader to [16, 114]. 150 12 Viscosity solutions to HJB equations and uniqueness of solutions 

This section introduces the notion of viscosity solutions to mean field HJB equa-tions on P(O). Before doing so, I recall some standard facts about viscosity solu-tions to first order HJB equations in finite dimension. Then, the notion of viscosity solutions is presented on P(Td) and then on Pp(Rd). Both times, a comparison result is established 

# 12.1 Viscosity solutions in finite dimension 

The goal of viscosity solution theory is, given PDEs for which a formal comparison principle holds, to define a weak notion of solution to these PDEs for which a comparison principle still holds. In other words, for equations for which, formally, we can say that super-solutions are above sub-solutions, viscosity solution theory aims at making rigorous such statements for weak solutions. In this section, I consider 

u + H(x, ∇xu) = 0 in O, (12.1) where H : O × Rd 7 → R is the Hamiltonian, u : O 7 → R is the solution and O is still either Td or Rd. I will sometimes allow myself to write |x − y| instead of d(x, y ),where d(·, ·) is the distance on Td and | · | the Euclidean norm. This equation is said to satisfy a comparison principle, in the following sense. 

Proposition 12.1. Let u and v be two smooth functions such that 

u + H(x, ∇xu) ≤ 0 in O,v + H(x, ∇xv) ≥ 0 in O.

Assume also that lim sup |x|→∞ u(x) − v(x) ≤ 0. Then u ≤ v.Proof. Consider a maximizing sequence (xn)n≥0 of sup x∈O u(x)−v(x). If |xn| → ∞ ,then the result is proved. If not, up to a subsequence, (xn)n≥0 has a limit point 

x∗ which is a point of maximum of u − v. In particular, ∇xu(x∗) = ∇xv(x∗).Evaluating the inequalities satisfied by u and v at x∗, we obtain 

0 ≥ u(x∗) + H(x∗, ∇xu(x∗)) = u(x∗) + H(x∗, ∇xv(x∗)) ≥ u(x∗) − v(x∗).

Hence the result follows. 151 The comparison principle implies the uniqueness of smooth solutions to (12.1) in the compact case, and uniqueness of smooth solutions that share the same be-havior at infinity in the non-compact case. Indeed, considering two such solutions 

u1 and u2, they each can play the role of either u or v in the previous proposition. Hence we deduce that u1 ≤ u2 ≤ u1. A possible way to understand the theory of viscosity solutions is to interpret it as the weakest definition of a notion of solution to (12.1), such that the comparison principle is preserved. Observe that, a priori, the roles played by u and v in the previous proposition are not symmetric. We must therefore define notions of super-solution and sub-solution, as we do now. 

Definition 12.2. A usc (resp. lsc) function u : O 7 → R is a viscosity sub-solution (resp. super-solution) to (12.1) if, for every smooth function ϕ : O 7 → R, and every 

x0 ∈ O which is a point of maximum (resp. minimum) of u − ϕ, we have 

u(x0) + H(x0, ∇xϕ(x0)) ≤ 0 (resp. ≥ 0) .

A locally bounded function u is a viscosity solution to (12.1) if its lower and upper semi-continuous envelopes (resp. u∗ and u∗) are resp. viscosity super-solution and sub-solution. 

From this definition, we can easily compare a viscosity super-solution with a smooth sub-solution, or a viscosity sub-solution with a smooth super-solution. The main question is in fact whether one can compare a viscosity super-solution with a viscosity sub-solution. The standard technique to answer this is the so-called doubling of variables method, which is the main argument in the next proof. 

Proposition 12.3. Assume that there exists a modulus of continuity ω such that for all q ∈ Rd, x, y ∈ O |H(x, q ) − H(y, q )| ≤ ω((1 + |q|)|x − y|). (12.2) 

Furthermore, if O = Rd, assume that for any R > 0, there exists CR such that for all x ∈ Rd, q 1, q 2 ∈ B(0 , R )

|H(x, q 1) − H(x, q 2)| ≤ CR|q1 − q2|. (12.3) 

Let u be a viscosity subsolution and v a viscosity supersolution to (12.1) , both bounded. Then u ≤ v.Proof. We start with the compact case. Consider a point of maximum (xϵ, y ϵ) of 

w : ( x, y ) 7 → u(x) − v(y) − 12ϵd(x, y )2.

u(xϵ) + H(xϵ, ϵ −1(xϵ − yϵ)) ≤ 0,v(yϵ) + H(yϵ, ϵ −1(xϵ − yϵ)) ≥ 0.

Definition 12.8. A usc (resp. lsc) function u : O 7 → R is a viscosity sub-solution (resp. super-solution) to (12.1) if, for all q ∈ ∂+u(x) (resp. q ∈ ∂−u(x)), we have

# 12.2 Viscosity solutions on the space of probability measures: the compact case 

In this section, I focus on the HJB equation 

U (μ) + 

> ∫
> Td

H(x, D μU (μ)( x), μ )μ(dx ) = 0 in P(Td), (12.5) where H : Td × Rd × P (Td) 7 → R is a function on which regularity assumptions shall be made later on, and Dμ stands for the horizontal derivative on Td (Section 4). Motivated by Section 11.4, there is a need to characterize non-differentiable solutions to (12.5), as we do not expect the typical solution to be differentiable. As mentioned earlier, in finite dimension, viscosity solutions can be defined equivalently with either super-differentials or smooth test functions. In this setting, it is not the case. The reason for this is quite apparent from Part I. Indeed, smooth functions have super-differentials which are deterministic, compared to the richness that one can obtain in general. Hence, because the functions we plan to characterize as viscosity solutions are not smooth, restricting ourselves to smooth test functions is equivalent to forgetting a potentially huge part of their super/sub-differentials. Note that the price to pay for such a choice is that the 155 existence of such solutions shall then be harder to prove, compared to what should happen if I restricted the attention to smooth test functions. For the integral term to be well defined in (12.5), a growth assumption on H

is necessary. We defined in Section 5.5 elements in super/sub-differentials with an integrability condition. Thus, the following condition shall be sufficient 

∃C > 0, ∀x ∈ Td, q ∈ Rd, μ ∈ P (Td), |H(x, q, μ )| ≤ C(1 + |q|2). (12.6) 

Remark 12.9. Note that even if HJB equations which are obtained through dy-namic programming, as in the previous section, are such that H is convex in its second argument, I consider here a more general situation and do not impose any condition of the sort. Such non-convex Hamiltonians are for instance involved in dynamic zero-sum games. 

All topological statements on P(Td) are made with respect to the weak topology for the rest of this section. The previous discussion leads to the following definition. 

Definition 12.10. A usc (resp. lsc) function U : P(Td) 7 → R is said to be a viscosity sub-solution (resp. super-solution) to (12.5) if for any μ ∈ P (Td), ψ ∈

∂+U (μ), (resp. ψ ∈ ∂−U (μ)), it holds that
