Remark 12.16. This proof of the stability of viscosity solutions is in fact a proof of the stability of sub/super differentials. 

Note that a requirement on the continuity of H in its second argument is needed in this kind of stability argument. It is classical and I do not know gen-eral proofs which work without it. Usually, stability proofs in viscosity solution theory are simpler, namely because of the equivalent definition of viscosity solu-tion involving smooth test functions. In this case, because we need to work with sub/super-differentials, the proof is more technical (even though it still follows classical arguments and does not require additional assumptions). 

Remark 12.17. In this section, I made the choice of the 2-Wasserstein distance. Since we are on a compact set, all Wasserstein distances give the same topology, however, their choice plays a role in some quantitative results or in the definition of super-differential for instance. All the arguments presented here can easily be extended to the case p ∈ (1 , ∞), by using Wpp instead of W22 .

# 12.3 Viscosity solutions on Wasserstein spaces: the non-compact case 

As is slightly apparent in the previous section, integrability conditions of elements of the super-differential, as well as growth conditions on the Hamiltonian, play an important role in the theory of viscosity solutions on spaces of probability mea-sures. The same goes in finite dimension for the growth of the Hamiltonian. In the non-compact case that I will now treat, such aspects are even more important. Here, I will focus on a particular setting: an equation set on Pp(Rd) for p ∈ (1 , ∞),with a time dependent problem and a Hamiltonian with an appropriate growth. The time dependent case is presented, as opposed to the stationary case of the previous section, simply for the sake of completeness and does not play a strong role in the following. However, in order to avoid quite challenging technicalities 160 due to the presence of the boundary condition in time (the initial condition), the following results are restricted to continuous viscosity solutions. The main equation of interest is here 

∂tU +

> ∫
> Rd

H(x, D μU (t, μ )( x), μ )μ(dx ) = 0 in (0 , T ) × P p(Rd), (12.13) together with initial condition 

U |t=0 = U0.

The Hamiltonian H satisfies for some C > 0

∀x, q ∈ Rd, μ ∈ P (Rd), |H(x, q, μ )| ≤ C(1 + |q|p′

), (12.14) so that the equation is well defined on elements of the sub/super-differentials, together with the usual continuity requirement 

∀x, y ∈ Rd, q ∈ Rd, μ, ν ∈ P p(Rd),

|H(x, q, μ ) − H(y, q, ν )| ≤ ω((1 + |q|)( |x − y| + Wp(μ, ν ))) , (12.15) and an additional estimate on the growth of DqH to handle perturbation terms 

∀x ∈ Rd, q ∈ Rd, μ ∈ P p(Rd),

|DqH(x, q, μ )| ≤ C(1 + |q|p′−1). (12.16) Note that convexity in q of H would imply this last requirement, but I do not assume convexity here. The length T > 0 of the time interval is arbitrary. 

Definition 12.18. A usc (resp. lsc) function U : [0 , T ] × P p(Rd) 7 → R is said to be a viscosity sub-solution (resp. super-solution) to (12.13) if for any t ∈ (0 , T ], μ ∈Pp(Rd), (θ, ψ ) ∈ ∂+U (t, μ ), (resp. (θ, ψ ) ∈ ∂−U (t, μ )) it holds that

if U ∗ is a sub-solution and U∗ a super-solution.

Theorem 12.19. Let p ∈ (1 , ∞). Assume that (12.14) , (12.15) and (12.16) hold. Let U, V ∈ BUC ([0 , T ] × P p(Rd)) be respectively viscosity sub and super-solution to

Recall that BU C is the space of bounded uniformly continuous functions. The proof of this result is similar to the one of Theorem 12.12, but has to deal with three additional difficulties: the time variable, the non-compactness of Pp(Rd) and the integrability condition of the elements of the super-differential which has to be in agreement with the growth of the Hamiltonian. Furthermore, an estimate of the form of (12.16) is required to deal with the various perturbations introduced in the proof. It is also important to note that this comparison result holds for sub and super-solutions which are in BUC. Such a regularity may seem arbitrary at the moment but I comment on it after the proof.

which also leads to a contradiction. Hence the result. 164 Remark 12.20. The assumption that U and V are in BUC is extremely helpful to deal with two problems at hand: i) the equation is posed on a non-compact space, ii) the problem is time dependent. It is in fact a very insightful exercise for the interested reader to verify that when one of these two problems is not present, one can simply assume that U is usc and V lsc. Nonetheless, here a difficulty truly exists. In practical applications, it is treated on a case by case basis, either by establishing that natural candidates for being viscosity solutions are in BUC, or by proving more information on the behavior of these candidates near infinity in space, to compactify the problem. Also, in certain situations, we can hope to prove the uniform continuity of the solution only close to the initial time (using a Cauchy-Lipschitz type result on small time). 

# 12.4 Bibliographical comments 

The theory of viscosity solutions has been introduced by Crandall and Lions in [55] to address first order Hamilton-Jacobi equations in finite dimension. This theory turned out to be the right one for such equations. I do not recall all the long literature on this topic but simply point out what I think are key elements. A major difficulty in the theory of viscosity solutions is the treatment of second order equations. A breakthrough was made by Jensen in [88] and a complete presentation of the basics on viscosity solutions can be found in the survey of Crandall, Ishii and Lions [54]. Perhaps the two main studies which are absent from the previous (must-read) survey are the questions of growth of solutions in unbounded domains, for which I refer to Ishii [86] or to Alvarez [7] in convex cases, and the half-relaxed limit of Barles and Perthame [13] their use in Barles and Souganidis [14] which are key illustrations of the powerful stability of viscosity solutions. Crandall and Lions published a series of papers [56, 57, 58, 59, 60, 61, 62] to provide a thorough study of HJB equations in Banach spaces, see also the book by Fabbri, Gozzi and Swiech [71] for more on this subject. For HJB equations on sets of probability measures, the notion of viscosity solutions presented here (which I believe is the right one) stems from my work [20]. In the non-compact case, the comparison principle was done by Lions and me in [29], see also our work with Ceccherini Silberstein for more details on such a notion [33]. Prior to that, several other notions were proposed. Lions proposed in [99] to define a notion of viscosity solution through its lift, which works well when we are on P2(Rd), because the latter is then lifted into the Hilbert space of square-integrable random variables. Another notion, somehow closer to the one presented here, focused only on deterministic elements of the super-differential. In such a way, comparison principles can only be obtained for particular Hamiltonians, such as it was the case in Cardaliaguet and Quincampoix [41], Marigonda and Quincampoix [103] or in Jimenez, Marigonda and Quincampoix [89]. The link 165 between this last notion and the one of Lions (using the lift) was studied by Gangbo and Tudorascu in [80]. Other notions of viscosity solutions were also considered in cases in which the HJB equations have additional singular terms but I do not comment on it here. When the Hamiltonian H is convex, using techniques of Alvarez [7], we provided with Lions a more general version of a comparison result in the non-compact case in [29]. 166 13 Regularity and viscosity properties of the value function, existence of viscosity solutions 

In this section, I show how to establish various properties on the value function of mean field optimal control problems. In particular, I focus on regularity estimates and on the fact that the value function is a viscosity solution to the associated HJB equation. I shall not treat general questions of existence of solutions, i.e. for equations which are not given by an optimal control problem. For such cases, I give a simple existence result for equations that can be lifted on the space of random variables. I will focus on the case of P(Td) for most of this section, mainly for two reasons: i) it allows for simpler notation than in the non-compact case, ii) it forces us to adopt a formalism on the space of measures as it prohibits simply lifting the equation on a Banach space of random variables with appropriate integrability conditions. Consider U defined for t ∈ [0 , T ), μ ∈ P (Td) by 

U (t, μ ) := inf

I shall make assumptions on L and G incrementally, to highlight what is used in each proof. For the moment, I simply assume that L, G ≥ 0, and that G is bounded (and that they are measurable...). So that U is well defined for every t, μ .First I will establish some regularity estimates on U : boundedness, continuity, Lipschitz continuity. # 13.1 Regularity estimates on the value function 

13.1.1 Boundedness 

For this first case, it is quite immediate to verify the boundedness property. When dealing with problems which are stochastic and have terminal constraints, as in Section 14, such bounds will turn out to be more difficult to obtain. 167 Proposition 13.1. For any t ∈ [0 , T ], μ ∈ P (Td),

inf G ≤ U (t, μ ) ≤ G (μ) + ( T − t)

Proof. The first property follows from the non-negativity of L, the second from the sub-optimality of the control which consists in not moving. 

13.1.2 Continuity through controllability 

Generally speaking, there are two main ways to prove that a value function is continuous. The first one relies on the controllability of the problem, while the second involves the use of the "same" control starting from two different measures. By controllability, it is understood that the controller can drive the state of the system, in any non-zero time interval, wherever it wants. This section presents the first approach and the second one is then presented in Section 13.1.3 just below. A first technical result consists in remarking that, in these kinds of problems, it is always almost optimal to wait a little bit before starting to make decisions. It is a time regularity result on the value function. 

Lemma 13.2. Assume that 

∃C > 0, ∀x ∈ Td, z ∈ Rd, μ ∈ P (Td), Dz L(x, z, μ ) · z ≤ C(1 + L(x, z, μ )) . (13.3)

Remark 13.3. Here and in several proofs below, I use almost optimal controls, whereas it can in fact be proven that optimal controls exist under mild assumptions. The current presentation has the virtue to be easily extendable to stochastic cases, where the existence of optimal controls is more involved, or to zero-sum games. In any case, the important question of the existence of optimal controls is not addressed here. 

The growth condition (13.3) implies in fact more than the inequality used in the previous proof. For instance, it implies that there exist constants C > 0, k > 1

such that 

∀x ∈ Td, z ∈ Rd, μ ∈ P (Td), L(x, z, μ ) ≤ C(1 + |z|k), (13.4) 169 where k is the constant C in (13.3) and C in the previous estimate is another constant. The previous result of time regularity allows me to prove the following result of regularity in space.

(because we cannot choose ϵ too large). Thus we have a local Lipschitz estimate, with a constant which depends only on time. The triangle inequality then implies that this Lipschitz estimate is in fact global. 

Remark 13.5. Since the terminal cost G is not assumed to be continuous, it is natural that the estimate above deteriorates as t → T . In fact, the previous result can be understood as a regularizing effect, even though such terminology is in general reserved for results proved at the level of the PDE, whereas here it is done at the level of the optimal control problem. It is a general feature of controllable problems, to have a Lipschitz continuous value function, and it is also well known that HJB equations have regularizing effects. 

The previous leads easily to global Lipschitz continuity of the value function. 170 Proposition 13.6. Assume that (13.3) holds. Then, for any ϵ > 0, there exists 

C > 0 such that for all 0 ≤ t, t ′ ≤ T − ϵ, μ, μ ′ ∈ P (Td),

|U (t, μ ) − U (t′, μ ′)| ≤ C(|t − t′| + Wk(μ, μ ′)) . (13.5) 

Proof. Consider (t, μ ), (t′, μ ′) ∈ [0 , T ) × P (Td) and assume that t′ > t . Consider-ing any control for U (t, μ ) which does nothing in [t, t ′], it follows that U (t, μ ) ≤

U (t′, μ ) + ( t′ − t) ∫ 

> Td

L(x, 0, μ )dμ . Thanks to Lemma 13.2, there exists a C(t′ − t)-optimal control for U (t, μ ) which does nothing in the time interval [t, t ′], for C

depending only on ϵ and U (t, μ, w ), i.e. 

U (t, μ ) ≥ U (t′, μ ) − C(t′ − t).

Hence, Lipschitz regularity in t is proven, and the result follows. 

13.1.3 Lipschitz regularity through parallel transport 

I present here another way to obtain the regularity of the value function. This second method is, in my opinion, more elegant than the previous one, as it does not require a lot of playing around with the control problem. It will also make more apparent the use of mixed flows. The main result is the following. 

Proposition 13.7. Assume that there exists C > 0, k > 1 such that L and G

satisfy 

∀μ, ν, |G (μ) − G(ν)| ≤ CWk(μ, ν ),

∀x, z, μ, C−1|z|k − C ≤ L(x, z, μ ),

∀x, y, z, μ, ν, |L(x, z, μ ) − L(y, z, ν )| ≤ C(1 + |z|k−1)( d(x, y ) + Wk(μ, ν )) 

Then, there exists C0 depending only on C and T such that for all t ∈ [0 , T ], μ, ν ∈P(Td),

|U (t, μ ) − U (t, ν )| ≤ C0Wk(μ, ν ).

Remark 13.8. Because of the coercivity of the Lagrangian, all admissible controls are sufficiently integrable to apply Proposition 2.6, and thus justify the equivalent reformulation of the value function with a minimization over processes. Proof. # 13.2 The value function is a viscosity solution 

In this section, I show that the value function defined in (13.1) is indeed a viscosity solution to

(13.10) I shall use Definition 12.18, with the obvious adaptation to this case in which time has been reversed and sub/super differentials with respect to the measure argument are taken as in Section 12.2. I focus on a case with quadratic growth to lighten notation and leave to the interested reader the extension to the case p̸ = 2 .Several requirements are in order on the costs. I now state them and they shall be in force for the rest of this section. 

Hypothesis 13.11. 1. The growth condition (13.3) holds with C = 2 , which guarantees quadratic growth of the Lagrangian. 2. The Hamiltonian H : ( x, q, μ ) 7 → H(x, q, μ ) is such that DqH is a Lips-chitz function in x, uniformly in (q, μ ) and such that, uniformly in q, it is uniformly continuous in (x, μ ).3. There exists C > 0 such that 

|L(x, q, μ ) − L(x, q, μ ′)| ≤ C(1 + |q|)W2(μ, μ ′),

4. There exists C > 0 such that 

C−1|q|2 − C ≤ L(x, q, μ ). (13.11) 173 This implies that, for possibly another C > 0,

|H(x, q, μ )| ≤ C(1 + |q|2).

Furthermore, from the convexity of H in q, we obtain that DqH has at most a linear growth in q.

In particular, under the previous assumptions, the value function is Lipschitz continuous away from t = T thanks to Proposition 13.6. Furthermore, Remark 13.8 is also valid here.

13.2.2 Super-solution property 

Proving the viscosity super-solution property is slightly more technical and this can be understood quite simply. It requires similar developments as in the previous proof, but this time we cannot simply use the sub-optimality of a single control, but rather have to keep many of them, sufficiently many so that the infimum is not perturbed in fact. Proceeding in this way requires the inversion of the infimum and the limit κ → 0, after having divided by κ in the proof of Proposition 13.12. Such an inversion has no reason to be true in general. There are several ways to bypass this difficulty. One consists in remarking that near optimal controls are in fact such that this inversion holds. Another consists in regularizing the problem, namely by restricting the set of controls so that the inversion is correct, and then pass to the limit in this regularization. I present the two approaches, even if the first one is more elegant, as the second one requires a Lemma which can be of independent interest, namely for questions of stability of viscosity solutions. In fact it involves the same techniques of stability that were presented in Theorem 12.15. In both cases, the crucial assumption is the coercivity of the cost (13.11). 175 By regularity of almost optimal trajectories 

From the coercivity of the cost, we can extract regularity of near optimal controls as the next result shows.

13.2.3 Behaviour at the final time and characterization of the value function 

I now conclude by showing that U can be in fact characterized as the unique viscosity solution to (13.9) with terminal condition G. The behaviour of the value 180 function at the terminal time is of importance here and I shall come back to this later on. From the perspective of viscosity solutions, the most natural assumption to treat the condition at terminal time is to establish that the value function is continuous there, which the next result states. 

Proposition 13.18. Assume that Hypothesis 13.11 holds and that G is continuous. Then, the value function U is continuous on [0 , T ] × P (Td) and U (T, μ ) = G(μ)

for any μ ∈ P (Td).Proof. Let (tn, μ n)n≥0 be a [0 , T ] × P (Td) valued sequence converging to some 

(T, μ ∗) for μ∗ ∈ P (Td). From Proposition 13.1, we know that for all n ≥ 0,

U (tn, μ n) ≤ (T − tn)

Hence the result is proved, since the previous implies lim inf n→∞ U (tn, μ n) ≥ G (μ∗).We now have all the tools to prove the main result of this section. 

Theorem 13.19. Assume that Hypothesis 13.11 holds and that G is continuous. Then, there exists a unique continuous viscosity solution to (13.9) with terminal condition G, it is given by the value function of the problem. Proof. We already saw that the value function satisfies all these requirements. If another function V satisfies them, then in particular (V − U )|t=T = 0 and, from an immediate adaptation of Theorem 12.19 to this equation on P(Td), it follows that V ≤ U ≤ V . Hence the result. 181 Remark 13.20. The case of discontinuous functions is much more involved, and I do not want to treat it here. Remark for instance that if G is discontinuous, then for two viscosity solutions U and V to (13.9) satisfying the terminal condition 

G, one does not have that V ∗ ≤ U∗, in fact the reverse inequality holds. Hence, applying the comparison principle in such a case is more delicate. 

# 13.3 Existence of viscosity solutions for equations that can be lifted 

The previous sections can be seen as an existence result for (13.9), when the Hamiltonian stems from an optimal control problem. There are various other ways to obtain the existence of viscosity solutions, in finite dimension at least: approximation by more regular equations, Perron’s method, and representation through values of zero-sum games. Here I take some time to explain how we can build on an existing result for HJB equations with non-convex Hamiltonians on certain Banach spaces, which is based on the latter technique just mentioned, and which is due to Crandall and Lions [56]. Let p ∈ (1 , ∞) and consider the Hamiltonian H : Rd × R × Rd × P p(Rd) 7 → R

and the problem of existence of a viscosity solution to 

U (μ) + 

> ∫
> Rd

H(x, U (μ), D μU (μ)( x), μ )μ(dx ) = 0 in Pp(Rd), (13.16) where H is assumed to satisfy: 

• There exist constants C > 0 and l ≤ min(2 , p ′) such that for all x, y, q 1, q 2 ∈

Rd, for all r, v ∈ R, for all μ, ν ∈ P p(Rd),

|H(x, r, q 1, μ ) − H(y, v, q 2, ν )| ≤ C(|q1| + |q2|)|x − y|++C(|r − v| + |q1 − q2| + min(1; Wp(μ, ν )))(1 + |q1|l−1 + |q2|l−1).

• For all x, q, μ , the map r 7 → H(x, r, q, μ ) is non-decreasing. In particular, H is not supposed to be convex in q, hence it does not necessarily come from an optimal control problem as was the case in the first part of this Section 13. The current subsection can thus be thought of as presenting more general results of existence, in the case O = Rd. The focus is put here on station-ary equations, but similar results could also be proven on time dependent problems. The existence of solutions to (13.16) can be established through the existence of viscosity solutions to 

U(X) + E[H(X, U(X), D U(X), L(X))] = 0 in Lp, (13.17) 182 where DU(X) stands for the (Fréchet) differential of U : Lp 7 → R at X. For a function U : Lp 7 → R, I denote by ∂+U(X) and ∂−U(X) the usual Fréchet super and sub-differentials of U at X ∈ Lp, which are thus convex closed subsets of Lp′

.A viscosity solution to (13.16) is defined similarly to what has been done above and a viscosity solution to (13.17) is defined as follows. 

Definition 13.21. Let U : Lp 7 → R be a continuous function. It is a viscosity sub-solution (resp. super-solution) to (13.17) if for any Z ∈ ∂+U(X) (resp. Z ∈

∂−U(X)), 

U(X) + E[H(X, U(X), Z, L(X))] ≤ 0 ( resp. ≥ 0) .

It is a viscosity solution to (13.17) if it is both a viscosity sub and super-solution. 

Under the current requirements on H, Theorem 1 in [56] proves that there exists at most one viscosity solution to (13.17). Furthermore, (13.17) is the lift of (13.16) and the following result links the two equations. 

Proposition 13.22. If U : Pp(Rd) 7 → R is a viscosity solution to (13.16) , then its lift U is a viscosity solution to (13.17) . If U : Lp 7 → R is a continuous viscosity solution to (13.17) , then for X ∈ Lp, U(X) depends only on the law of X, i.e. it is equal to some U (L(X)) , and U is a viscosity solution to (13.16) .Proof. The first assertion is a direct consequence of Proposition 5.21. Indeed, for 

Z ∈ ∂+U(X), from Proposition 5.21 γ := L(X, Z ) ∈ ∂+U (L(X)) . Hence, since U

is a viscosity sub-solution, denoting μ = L(X)

U (μ) + 

> ∫
> Rd×Rd

H(x, U (μ), z, μ )γ(dx, dz ) ≤ 0.

But the previous is exactly 

U(X) + E[H(X, U(X), Z, L(X))] ≤ 0.

For the second one, consider a one-to-one bi-measurable, measure preserving map-ping τ : Ω 7 → Ω and define V(X) = U(X ◦ τ ). Consider Z ∈ ∂+V(X). Then, for all Y ,

U(Y ◦ τ ) ≤ U (X ◦ τ ) + E[Z · (Y − X)] + o(∥Y − X∥),

which implies that Z ◦ τ ∈ ∂+U(X ◦ τ ). In particular, since U is a viscosity sub-solution to (13.17), 

V(X) + E[H(X ◦ τ, V(X), Z ◦ τ, L(X))] ≤ 0

Since 

E[H(X ◦ τ, V(X), Z ◦ τ, L(X))] = E[H(X, V(X), Z, L(X))] ,

183 we obtain that V is also a viscosity sub-solution to (13.17). Arguing similarly, we deduce that V is also a viscosity super-solution, hence a viscosity solution. Since H

satisfies the requirements of Theorem 1 in [56], the uniqueness of viscosity solutions to (13.17) yields V = U. Hence, since τ is any one-to-one measure preserving mapping and U is continuous, it follows that for any X, Y which have same law, 

U(X) = U(Y ). In particular, U is the lift of some function U : Pp(Rd) 7 → R, which is necessarily a viscosity solution to (13.16), thanks to Proposition 5.21. 

Remark 13.23. Note that the previous result does not imply that a viscosity sub-solution to (13.17) is the lift of a viscosity sub-solution to (13.16) , as there may exist sub-solutions which are not lifts. However, if we assume, in addition to the sub-solution property, such an invariance property, then the pull-down is indeed a viscosity sub-solution to (13.16) .

The previous result allows one to extend instantly results of existence of (13.17) to (13.16). There are numerous results of existence of first order HJB equations on Banach spaces, and I will not review all of them, nor prove the one I am going to state here, since the framework needed for the study of such equations is quite far from the tools developed and studied here. I simply mention that, in my opinion there are two general approaches to such problems. The first one, maybe the simpler, consists in approximating (13.17) with finite dimensional elements. We can then use existence result for finite dimensional equations and pass to the limit in this approximation. The second one consists in studying particular Hamiltonians H for which, (13.17) arises as the so-called Hamilton-Jacobi-Isaacs equation of a differential zero-sum game. Existence is then obtained by verifying that the value function of the game is in fact a viscosity solution to the associated equation. By various approximation results, one can then extend the class of Hamiltonians for which one can prove existence. The first type of method is for instance presented in Section 3.7 in [71] or used in [27]; while the second one is for instance the object of [57] from which we extract the next result as a simpler version of their Theorem 1.1 (ii). Note that in both methods, stability properties of viscosity solutions are fundamental to pass to the limit. 

Theorem 13.24. Under the standing assumptions on H, there exists a (unique) viscosity solution to (13.16) .

I do not provide a self-contained proof of this result, and invite the readers who are not familiar with [57] to simply omit it. 

Proof. I indicate how to verify that the assumptions (H0)-(H4) of Theorem 1.1 (ii) in [57] are satisfied. First, two auxiliary functions need to exist. We can take 

d(X, Y ) = ∥X − Y ∥p and η(X) = 2(1 + E[|X|p]) 1 

> p

. Next, their assumption (H1) is 184 directly assumed here. Assumptions (H0) and (H2)-(H4) follow from the following computation, for X, Y ∈ Lp, P, Q ∈ Lp′

, r, s ∈ R, μ = L(X) and ν = L(Y ).

E[|H(X, r, P, μ ) − H(Y, s, Q, ν )|]

≤ E[C(|r − s| + |P − Q| + Wp(μ, ν ))(1 + |P |l−1 + |Q|l−1)] + CE[|X − Y |(1 + |P | + |Q|)] 

≤ C∥X − Y ∥p(1 + ∥P ∥p′ + ∥Q∥p′ ) + C(Wp(μ, ν ) + |r − s|)(1 + ∥P ∥l−1(l−1) + ∥Q∥l−1(l−1) )+ C∥P − Q∥p′ (1 + ∥P ∥l−1 

> p′

+ ∥Q∥l−1 

> p′

).

Since Wp(μ, ν ) ≤ ∥ X − Y ∥p, (H0) and (H2) in [57] follow. For (H4), we need to evaluate the previous for P = Q = λ(X − Y )|X − Y |p−2∥X − Y ∥1−pp and r = s.Using ∥P ∥l−1 ≤ ∥ P ∥p′ = λ, we deduce (since l − 1 ≤ 1)

E[H(X, r, P, μ ) − H(Y, r, Q, ν )] ≤ C(2 λ)d(X, Y ) + C min(1; d(X, Y )) .

The assumption (H4) is then verified by realizing that, setting G(r) = C the requirement in Theorem 1.1 (ii) in [57] follows. 

# 13.4 Bibliographical comments 

In the analysis of an optimal control problem, obtaining various regularity proper-ties of the value function is now a classical topic. In finite dimension, I refer to the book of Bardi and Capuzzo-Dolcetta [12] for deterministic problems, and to the one of Fleming and Soner [76] for stochastic problems. The results I presented here are mostly taken from my work [20]. Usually a minimum of regularity needs to be established before trying to verify that the value function is indeed a viscosity solution to the associated HJB equation. The sub-solution property is quite often easier to prove (when looking at mini-mization problems), as is apparent here, namely because we need to have informa-tion on a single trajectory, that we can choose. The super-solution is usually more involved, as is the case here. A typical technique, which I choose to avoid here because it is not the most general one, consists in proving first that an optimal control exists. We are then in a similar position as for the sub-solution property, except for the fact that the minimizer is quite often simply given by some abstract result and we know little information about it. The two techniques I presented are the most general ones. The regularity of controls of finite cost is a powerful tool and I learned Lemma 13.13 from Giacomo Ceccherini Silberstein. The truncation of the controls has the interest of passing through Proposition 13.16, which follows the lines of Proposition 4.3 in Crandall, Ishii and Lions [54], and which is a fun-damental tool when one wants to establish some stability properties of viscosity solutions. I chose not to present many stability results as they seem to me easy to 185 establish given the results I already presented here. I hope that this will not be held against this presentation. I insist that, even though additional care is needed, the techniques presented here should be of interest for proving similar results in differential games. For instance, they could be of interest to prove that the value function of some zero-sum games is indeed the unique viscosity solution of the associated Hamilton-Jacobi-Isaacs equation. The treatment of the terminal condition in time is always a difficulty that has to be taken with care. Finally, using the formula given by the value function is a quite natural way of obtaining existence of a viscosity solution, even if restricted to HJB equations with convex Hamiltonians. Note that the result of existence of Crandall and Lions [57] that I used in Section 13.3 relies on a similar idea, but with a representation through values of two-player zero-sum games instead. 186 14 A mean field optimal control problem with noise: stochastic optimal transport 

In this section, I introduce a variant of the problem considered in Section 11.1. There are two main differences here. The first one is that this new problem incor-porates a stochastic component, and the second one is that it has a state constraint at the terminal time, which thus corresponds to a terminal cost G which is valued in R ∪ { +∞} .Several arguments already presented can be adapted to these cases, and I will not detail all these adaptations, focusing mostly on the challenging parts of this problem. I start by presenting the model, I then explain why the associated value function is indeed well defined by providing bounds. We will then see why it is indeed a viscosity solution to the associated PDE and I will conclude by explain-ing how it can be characterized as the unique viscosity solution with appropriate boundary requirements. All this section is restricted to the case O = Td, and I will see P(Td) equipped with the topology of weak convergence. 

# 14.1 The model 

14.1.1 The optimization problem and the value function 

The problem of interest here is fairly similar to the one considered in Section 11.1, except that at time t = T , the state of the current measure mT needs to be equal to the value of a P(Td) valued, adapted, stochastic process (νt)t∈[0 ,T ] defined on a standard filtered probability space (Ω , A, (Ft)t≥0, P).There are several such processes (νt)t∈[0 ,T ] that could be considered and I present here three of them. 

The reveal case 

The first, and simpler one, simply consists in a process which is constant equal to ν0 ∈ P (Td) in [0 , T / 2) and which is constant in time afterwards, of law Θ ∈P(P(Td)) , in [T / 2, T ]. In other words, at time T / 2, a terminal target is randomly drawn according to the law Θ. For instance, if Θ = 12 (δν1 + δν2 ), then the target at terminal time is ν1 with probability 12 or ν2 with probability 12 .

The jump case 

In this second case, there is a sequence of Poisson times (τi)i≥1 given a Poisson process of intensity λ : [0 , T ] 7 → R+. The intensity λ is assumed to be smooth. Hence, almost surely, the sequence of random times is finite. Then, at each of those times, the state of the target process (νt)t≥0 jumps from νt−

> i

to T (νt−

> i

) = νt+

> i

,for a certain function T : P(Td) 7 → P (Td). In this framework, we could also have 187 considered a case in which at each of those times, instead of this jump function T ,the new value of the target process is drawn from a known distribution. 

The diffusion case 

In this third and last setting, the target process (νt) is given by 

νt := ( τWt )# ¯ν

for a given ¯ν ∈ P(Td) and (Wt)t≥0 is the strong solution to 

dW t = σ(t)dB t, (14.1) with initial condition W0 = 0 , for (Bt)t≥0 a standard d dimensional Brownian mo-tion and a continuous function σ : [0 , T ] 7 → R. More general SDEs could also be considered under standard regularity assumptions on the drift and volatility. 

The control problem and the value function 

As usual in stochastic optimal control, the controls are allowed to be random, but they have to be adapted to the filtration (Ft)t≥0, or more generally to the filtration generated by the underlying randomness. Furthermore, I assume a sort of risk neutrality so that it is the expected value of the (now stochastic) cost which is minimized. In this case, this leads to the minimization problem

# 14.2 Bounds on the value function 

The first step in the analysis of (14.2) is to show that there exist admissible con-trols of finite cost. This will guarantee that the value function is not equal to +∞

everywhere. In addition it will allow us to work nicely away from t = T . Indeed, if we prove that U (T − ϵ, μ, ν ) is bounded for ϵ > 0, uniformly 9 in μ and ν, then we will be able to treat the problem on [0 , T − ϵ] by using U (T − ϵ, ·, ·) as a terminal cost. Furthermore, in addition to obtaining bounds on the value function, I shall show a precise behaviour of the value function near t = T .Because of the terminal constraint, to obtain bounds on the value function, a more precise control on the cost is needed. Here, I assume that the cost L satisfies 

∃C > 0, p ∈ (1 , ∞), ∀x ∈ Td, z ∈ Rd, 0 ≤ L(x, z ) ≤ C(1 + |z|p). (14.5)   

> 9In a non-compact case such as P2(Rd), it is the local boundedness which is of course sufficient.

Remark 14.4. A few comments on the hypotheses of the result. 

• The assumption (14.7) is purely technical (and thus quite regrettable). It is verified by any function such that λ(t) ∼ C(T − t)α for any C > 0, α > −1.However it is not automatically verified, as for instance λ(t) = − ddt (e−(T −t)−2

)

does not verify it. I do not know whether this can be removed. 

• On the contrary, (14.6) is quite important in our proof! This assumption yields an integrability condition on the product λM . Such an integrability condition is crucial. Note for example that if λ is constant, then we require (among other things), that M ∈ L1

> loc

, which is not the case for a quadratic cost. See the example below. 

The following example provides insight on what happens when the assumptions of Proposition 14.3 are not in force.

The previous bound allows one to prove regularity of the value function away from {t = T }, namely by using the controllability of the problem, as in Section 13.1.2. 

Proposition 14.6. Assume that Hypothesis 13.11, and (14.6) , (14.7) hold. Then, for any ϵ > 0, for any ν ∈ P (Td) the function (t, μ ) 7 → U (t, μ, ν ) is Lipschitz continuous in [0 , T − ϵ] × P (Td).Proof. This follows simply from the proof of Proposition 13.6. Indeed adapting exactly the same argument yields the required result, because we know that U is uniformly bounded on [0 , T − ϵ] × P (Td). Moreover, the dependence in ν does not affect the proof. To obtain the regularity in ν (which is not particularly needed in the rest of the analysis), I pass by the dynamic programming principle. 195 Proposition 14.7. Under the assumptions of the previous Proposition, for any 

t < t + ϵ < T , μ, ν ∈ P (Td),

I do not provide a proof of this result, which follows from standard techniques. I rather refer the reader to Claisse, Talay and Tan [51] for similar considerations, in more difficult situations in finite dimension, and to Djete, Possamai and Tan [68] in infinite dimension, namely because we have already proven that U is continuous in 

(t, μ ), while, almost surely, the variable ν will only take a finite number of values, hence the measurability of U in ν does not raise any issue. I can now state the following continuity result in ν.

Proposition 14.8. Assume that Hypothesis 13.11, and (14.6) , (14.7) hold. Then, for any ϵ > 0, the function (t, μ, ν ) 7 → U (t, μ, ν ) is continuous on [0 , T −ϵ]×P (Td)2.Proof. First note that we already established the continuity in μ. This implies that when λ ≡ 0, the Lipschitz regularity in ν is also established (it suffices to reverse time in this deterministic setting). Thus Udet satisfies the conclusion of the Proposition. I now make use of the dynamic programming principle. Let θ ∈ (0 , T )

(which is going to be close to T ) and β be the function given by Proposition 14.3, which is Hölder continuous. Let t ∈ [0 , T − ϵ], μ ∈ P (Td), ν, ν ′ ∈ P (Td). From the dynamic programming principle, we know that 

|U (t, μ, ν ) − U (t, μ, ν ′)| ≤ sup 

> m∈P (Td)

|U (θ, m, ν ) − U (θ, m, ν ′)|.

Using the Lipschitz continuity of Udet and Proposition 14.3 yields 

|U (t, μ, ν ) − U (t, μ, ν ′)| ≤ C Wp(ν, ν ′)(T − θ)p−1 + 2 β(T − θ).

Remark that, minimizing over θ alone yields some Hölder modulus of continuity for U in ν. Hence the result is proved because we already knew the Lipschitz regularity in the other variables. 

# 14.3 The value function is a viscosity solution 

In this section, I explain how to adapt the proofs of Section 13.2 to this stochastic setting. In all this section, the value function is the function U defined in (14.3) 196 in the jump case. Here, Hypothesis 13.11, and (14.6), (14.7) are in force. Before showing that the value function is indeed a viscosity solution, the definition of viscosity solution needs to be (slightly) adapted to this new setting. Namely, since U depends on two measure arguments, the notions of sub/super differentials need to be adapted. This is a bit arbitrary since the equation only involves the derivatives of U with respect to its first measure argument. Hence, I do not justify the definition in this case, and hope that the reader can deduce it by herself or himself. 

Definition 14.9. A usc (resp. lsc) function U : [0 , T ) × P (Td)2 7 → R is said to be a viscosity sub-solution (resp. super-solution) of (14.4) if for any t ∈ [0 , T ), μ, ν ∈P(Td) and (θ, ψ, ϕ ) ∈ ∂+U (t, μ, ν ) (resp. ∈ ∂−U (t, μ, ν ))

−θ +

> ∫
> Td×Rd

H(x, z )ψx(dz )μ(dx ) + λ(t)( U (t, μ, ν ) − U (t, μ, T ν)) ≤ 0 (resp. ≥ 0) .

A viscosity solution to (14.4) is a locally bounded function U such that U∗ is a viscosity super-solution and U ∗ is a viscosity sub-solution. 

Using the dynamic programming principle and the bounds obtained on the value function just above, we can argue as if we were dealing with a continuous bounded terminal cost. First, the property of viscosity sub-solution can be proven following exactly the same argument, as in the deterministic case. 

Proposition 14.10. The value function U is a viscosity sub-solution to (14.4) .Proof. Take (t, μ, ν ) ∈ [0 , T ) × P (Td)2 and (θ, ψ, ϕ ) ∈ ∂+U (t, μ, ν ). Consider a standard probability space (Ω ′, A′, P′) (not the one on which the process (νt) is defined, but another one, on which the controller can randomize its controls upon), and a couple (Xt, Z ) of random variables on Ω′ such that L(Xt, Z )( dx, dz ) = 

μ(dx )ψ(x, dz ). In particular, this couple is deterministic as a random variable over Ω. As in the deterministic case, define the ODE 

dX s = −DqH(Xs, Z )ds for s ∈ (t, T ),

with initial condition Xt at time s = t. Once again, this process is deterministic from the point of view of Ω. Take κ ∈ (0 , T −t) and arguing as in the deterministic setup, we end up with 

U (t, μ, ν ) ≤ EP

> [

EP′ 

> [∫ t+κt

L(Xs, −DqH(Xs, Z )) ds + U (t + κ, L(Xt+κ), ν t+κ) 

> ] ∣∣∣∣∣

νt = ν

> ]

.

It suffices now to use the tower property on the first expectation to write it as the expectation of the expectation conditioned on the number of jumps n of the target 197 process in [t, t + κ]. Since we have the equivalences, as κ → 0, P(n = 0) ∼ 1,

P(n = 1) ∼ λ(t)κ, P(n ≥ 2) ≤ Cκ 2, we can indeed conclude as in the proof of Proposition 13.12. Proving the viscosity super-solution property can be done exactly as in the deterministic case, namely by considering first the value function U δM , for M > 0,

δ ∈ (0 , T ), defined for (t, μ, ν ) ∈ [0 , T − δ) × P (Td)2 by 

U δM (t, μ, ν ) = inf   

> (b, ˜m)

E  

> [ ∫T−δt
> ∫
> Td×K

+ U (T − δ, (π1)# ˜mT −δ, ν T −δ)|νt = ν

> ]

where the infimum is taken over the same controls as for U in (14.3), except for the fact that, almost surely, in the time interval [t, T − δ], the control b is bounded by M everywhere. The fact that the restriction is only asked up to T − δ is necessary for the value function to be bounded. Indeed, otherwise, there will be a controllability issue which would imply that U δM = + ∞.In what follows, I am going to fix δ and pass to the limit M → ∞ to show that 

U is a viscosity solution on the time interval (0 , T − δ). Then, since δ is arbitrary, this will lead to the required property. Showing that U δM is a viscosity super-solution and that the lim M →∞ U δM is also a viscosity super-solution is the exact analogue of what has been done in the deterministic case, once we use the same conditioning as in the proof of Proposition 14.10 just above. Hence, to complete the proof of the fact that U is a viscosity super-solution, it only remains to show that lim inf M →∞ U δM = U .

Lemma 14.11. For any (t, μ, ν ) ∈ [0 , T − δ] × P (Td)2, lim M →∞ U δM (t, μ, ν ) = 

U (t, μ, ν ).Proof. I proceed as in the deterministic case. Let (t, μ, ν ) ∈ [0 , T ) × P (Td)2 and consider an ϵ-optimal control (b, ˜m) for U (t, μ, ν ), in the time interval [t, T − δ].Recall that this control is thus an adapted process to the filtration of the probabil-ity space (Ω , A, (Ft)t≥0, P). For M > 0, I want to define an associated admissible control (bM , ˜mM ) for UM (t, μ, ν ). This can be done exactly as previously, for each 

ω. Indeed, for P almost every ω ∈ Ω, the control (b, ˜m) can be truncated (only on 

[t, T − δ]), exactly as is being done in Lemma 13.17. Hence, for almost every ω, we have a process (XM,ω s (ω′), Z M,ω s (ω′)) s∈[t,T −δ] defined on another probability space 

(Ω ′, A′, P′). A priori, this second probability space should depend on ω itself, but from standard probabilistic arguments, we can always assume that it is constant 198 in ω.If we are able to prove that 

lim   

> M→∞

EP  

> [∫T−δt

EP′ [|ZM,ω s − Zωs |p]ds 

> ]

= 0 , (14.9) then, it is a simple exercise to verify that the computation of Lemma 13.17 yields the result. But once again, (14.9) follows from the dominated convergence theorem, and the double dependence in ω ∈ Ω and ω′ ∈ Ω′ does not perturb the argument. Hence, the result is proved. I insist that in the previous proof, I looked at the optimal control problem at hand as a problem with a continuous terminal cost at time t = T − δ, hence the truncation of the controls does not raise an issue with the constraint at terminal time. Putting all the pieces of this section together, we have proven the following. 

Theorem 14.12. The value function U is a viscosity solution to (14.4) in (0 , T )×P(Td)2.

# 14.4 Characterization of the value function as a viscosity solution 

To conclude the present study of the value function U , I will show that it can be characterized as being the unique viscosity solution to (14.4) satisfying the behaviour obtained in Proposition 14.3. The standing assumptions are here the same as in the previous section, that is, Hypothesis 13.11, and (14.6), (14.7) hold. In addition, I focus on the case p = 2 to lighten notation. 

Theorem 14.13. Assume that λ is continuous and that T is Lipschitz continuous for W2. Then, there exists at most one viscosity solution V to (14.4) on (0 , T ) ×P(Td)2 satisfying

Proof. The proof works in the same way as the comparison principles I presented in Section 12 except for two features: i) the presence of the term in λ and ii) the non-usual behaviour at the terminal time. I only highlight how those differences need to be handled. The structure of the proof is the same. We consider V1, V 2,respectively sub and super-solution to (14.4) satisfying both (14.10), and we focus on points of maxima of
